☆ AgentRoboCritic: Understanding the Capability of Frontier Agents on Direct Robot Control
Yiyang Du, Yuxuan Kuang, Qitao Zhao, Yixuan Wang, Zhanqiu Guo, Yi Xu, Xin Li, Wenbin He, Liu Ren, Chenyan Xiong
Frontier multimodal language agents such as GPT-6 Astra have demonstrated strong capabilities in controlling robots directly via low-level numerical actions without high-level primitives. However, existing benchmarks report only episode-level success rates, which show neither how these agents succeed nor where they fail. In this paper, we present a systematic action-level analysis of frontier agents on direct robot control across 12 manipulation benchmarks. We introduce AgentRoboCritic, an intent-grounded critique framework in which the policy agent states a local intent alongside each action, and a critic evaluates the action's outcome and failure mode against this intent. Our action-level analysis demonstrates the promising capabilities of frontier agents on direct robot control, including closed-loop recovery from local execution failures, reliable semantic grounding across diverse scenes, and strong zero-shot manipulation without task-specific adaptation. It also identifies their current limitations, including task-relevant physical execution bottlenecks such as grasping and alignment, and uneven recovery across failure modes. Finally, we validate our critic on a 400-action human-annotated benchmark and release a dataset of 12,844 annotated actions alongside a fine-tuned open-source critic, Muse-Glimmer-30B, whose failure-mode accuracy rises from 32.58% to 74.24%, enabling low-cost action-level analysis.
comment: 28 pages. Project page: https://adu2021.github.io/blog/AgentRoboCritic/
☆ Represented but misread: Post-training changes how language models read out what they represent
Jochen Laubrock
Does post-training change what a language model represents, or how this representation is read out into an answer? We compared linear probes with the models' own answers, analyzed with signal detection theory, in six open-weight base models and their post-trained versions. For judgments of whether German texts were written by a human or an AI, probes and word n-gram classifiers separated the classes well, but the answers did not and often ordered the texts inversely. Post-training lowered the sensitivity of the answers on average ($Δd' = -0.34$ across model families; -0.29 across three prompts). It also changed the use of a stated base rate: Base models shifted their log-odds by less than the stated change in prior odds, most post-trained models by more. In two models with stage-wise checkpoints, the change occurred during supervised fine-tuning, model stitching implicated the early layers, and from mid-depth on internal states at positions after the text, not those of the text, transferred the change between checkpoints. Our corpora cannot be shared, so we add a case reproducible from public data, the truth of negated statements. Here, post-training acted in the opposite direction: Probes decoded truth at or near ceiling in every model, the answers of several base models did not use it, and post-training repaired them; by mid-depth the repair resided at the positions after the statement; below it, the representation of the statement contributed, strongly in one of four models. In a training study, adding 2,000 correctly labeled authorship judgments of news texts to otherwise identical fine-tuning data improved the answers on our newspaper and textbook corpora and inverted them on the chats, where they followed a news-trained probe of the base model. Post-training thus changed how a judgment is read out of what a model represents, and a small training signal can set its direction.

☆ Hybrid Retrieval for Government Scheme Search in English and Marathi
Aishwarya Gunjal, Kanchan Khemnar, Pawan Poojary, Pranav Kulkarni, Raviraj Joshi, Manish Khodaskar
Retrieving government schemes requires matching citizens' information needs to formal administrative descriptions, often across languages and terminology. We present a bilingual search system for Maharashtra government schemes and public services that combines BM25 retrieval with sentence-embedding similarity through equal-weight score fusion. The system indexes aligned English-Marathi scheme descriptions and supports multilingual, Marathi-focused, and translation-mediated semantic configurations. Evaluation on manually curated bilingual queries compares BM25, dense retrieval, and hybrid retrieval using IndicSBERT and MahaSBERT. BM25 provides a strong baseline with a mean reciprocal rank (MRR) of 0.864. Hybrid retrieval increases MRR to 0.872 with IndicSBERT and 0.879 with MahaSBERT, whereas the corresponding dense-only systems achieve 0.663 and 0.655. MahaSBERT-based fusion also increases precision from 0.781 to 0.808 while matching BM25 recall at 0.946. These results support the complementary use of lexical and semantic signals in this collection, while showing that improvements over a strong lexical baseline are modest and metric-dependent. The study provides an application-focused analysis of hybrid retrieval for bilingual public-service information access.
☆ AutoMaster: Autonomous Post-Training by Making the Same Model a Better Teacher
Guoxin Chen, Shuang Sun, Fanzhe Meng, Xinyu Tang, Ruihua Song, Wayne Xin Zhao
Task success alone does not specify which behaviors post-training should reinforce. Even successful teacher trajectories can contain unsupported assumptions, unproductive repetition, or superficial verification. We propose Skill-FT (Skill-Guided Fine-Tuning), which uses natural-language skills to suppress undesirable habits and elicit underrepresented effective behaviors during teacher rollouts, then fine-tunes a target model on the resulting trajectories. We introduce AutoMaster, an autonomous post-training system in which an LLM agent develops these interventions through trajectory analysis, skill design, data selection, and feedback from rollouts and target-model evaluation. Across Terminal, Search, and software engineering tasks with 4B and 9B target models, AutoMaster improves mean performance over Vanilla SFT on successful unguided teacher trajectories by 1.42-8.99 percentage points, with the teacher, task pool, training set size, and SFT recipe held fixed. Together with the ablations and trajectory analyses, these results suggest that behavioral shaping can make a fixed model a more effective teacher. We further find that retaining skill instructions during SFT yields higher mean performance than removing them across all six settings, despite their absence at evaluation and identical supervised outputs. These findings suggest that preserving skill context as part of a training demonstration can support better behavioral internalization.
comment: Website: https://aweai-team.github.io/AutoMaster/ Code: https://github.com/RUCAIBox/AutoMaster Models: https://huggingface.co/collections/RUC-AIBOX/automaster
☆ GLiDE: A Fast, Calibrated Decision Model Distilled from Reasoning Teachers
Sahibzada Allahyar, Julia White, Dhruv Atreja, George Hurn-Maloney, Urchade Zaratiana
Decision models enable fast, confidence-aware decisions by scoring candidate answers in a single forward pass, without generating text or intermediate reasoning. Yet reasoning can substantially improve decision quality, raising the question of whether its benefits can be retained without the cost of generating it. We investigate this question with \textbf{GLiDE}, a family of decision models spanning 0.4B to 27B parameters. Building on Qwen hybrid language models, GLiDE enables bidirectional interactions between the context, question, and candidate answers by removing causal masking from full-attention layers. To transfer reasoning capabilities into single-pass inference, we combine soft-target distillation from multiple teachers, including a reasoning model, with progressive post-training on diverse decision tasks. We further explore parameter-free recurrent depth to increase inference-time computation without generating tokens. Across five model sizes, GLiDE lies on the performance--parameter Pareto frontier of the Hugging Face \DI{}, with each model outperforming evaluated alternatives of equal or smaller size. \dmtwo{} (27B) achieves a hidden-set score of 71.52, with an expected calibration error of 2.0\% and a median latency of 92.9\,ms on a single GPU. Further checkpoint averaging and parameter-free recurrent depth yield GLiDE~27B, which reaches 73.37 with an expected calibration error of 2.1\%.

☆ Multimodal Error Detection in Chest X-ray Reports: A Large Language Model Reading Image Evidence
Yanda Cheng, Bo Wen, Yuhao Lu
Rad-Linter checks chest X-ray reports against image-derived evidence to flag potential errors for review. A pretrained image classifier supplies scores for nine pathologies, and a 1.5B-parameter language model, fine-tuned with LoRA on 1,816 examples, checks their consistency with the report. We compare it with a keyword-and-negation rule baseline given the same reports and scores. Evaluation uses controlled report-score pairings from OpenI, with consistency labels derived from automatic report labels and score thresholds. Each counterfactual quad crosses two real reports with two real score vectors from other patients, creating two consistent and two inconsistent pairs. Every report and score vector occurs in both conditions, so either input alone permits only 50% accuracy. On 131 quads, Rad-Linter achieves 98.5% pair accuracy versus 80.5% for the rule baseline, and classifies all four pairs correctly in 94.4% of quads versus 56.5%. The checkers perform similarly on omission items, including unmentioned and negated findings; larger differences occur on the remaining item types, which require interpreting how findings are described. The same language model without fine-tuning remains near 50% accuracy. On a second set of 181 quads that also includes reports on which the automatic labelers disagree, pair accuracy is 93.4% versus 75.3%. These controlled results show that, given the same image-derived scores, a trained language model detects report-score inconsistencies more accurately than the tested rule baseline, with the advantage concentrated in interpreting how findings are worded.
comment: 34 pages, 4 figures, including supplementary material
☆ MemFolder: Structure-Guided Memory Folding for Efficient Agent Memory Management
Xuyang Liu, Chenwen Lu, Junxi Wang, Siyu Ren, Xinying Lin, Siteng Huang, Hongli Sun, Linfeng Zhang, Honggang Chen
Long-term agents accumulate large external memory stores as interactions unfold, increasing the costs of storage, retrieval, and response generation. Efficiency is constrained by two coupled factors: a growing record count increases retrieval overhead, while longer retrieved contexts increase prefill computation and decoding-time attention costs. Existing methods prune or consolidate records, but pruning can discard useful evidence, whereas consolidation may produce fewer yet longer retrieval units. We introduce MemFolder, a structure-guided memory folding framework that sequentially reduces record count and context length. The framework comprises two core components that share memory groups, using cross-record structure to guide both evidence consolidation and compact representation. RecordFolder grounds entity mentions, progressively selects representative yet complementary anchors, and combines semantic and entity affinity with temporal chaining to consolidate compatible records into fewer retrieval units. ContextFolder encodes the original records within these groups into compact text and uses verification feedback to revise encodings when key facts cannot be recovered. Experiments on the three text-based memory benchmarks LoCoMo, PersonaMem, and LongMemEval and the two multimodal benchmarks M3-Bench-web and M3-Bench-robot demonstrate the strong performance of our methods. Notably, on Mem0, RecordFolder retains 99.0% of the original average performance across the three text-based benchmarks with 50% fewer records, exceeding the strongest baseline by 1.9 percentage points in performance retention. Combined with ContextFolder, existing record compression methods further shorten their output records while retaining most of their performance.
comment: Code is available at https://github.com/xuyang-liu16/MemFolder
☆ Democratizing Cross-Project Knowledge for Transforming Water Governance and Climate Resilience: an Agentic AI Ecosystem
Oriol Alàs, Ian Palacín, Iván Cester, Edgar Rubión
EU-funded projects produce extensive evidence in the form of deliverables, reports, and case studies. However, this knowledge is typically scattered across projects and document structures, making it difficult to locate relevant, contextual information when making decisions. This paper introduces an Agentic AI Ecosystem that consolidates heterogeneous project documentation into a domain-aware knowledge base and provides adaptive access through an agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation pipeline. Documents from three EU-funded projects (IMPETUS, NATALIE, and InnWater) are reconstructed into hierarchical section trees and indexed with structured metadata. Retrieval is supported by two domain-specific embedding models fine-tuned on domain data (water and climate). On top of this knowledge base, a ReAct-style controller iteratively orchestrates retrieval actions by allocating retrieval budget across domain collections and accumulating evidence until a stopping criterion is met. In a retrieval benchmark over the corpus, the proposed approach improves Mean Reciprocal Rank by 26.7% (relative) compared to a single-domain embedding baseline, indicating more consistent retrieval of relevant evidence to support cross-project knowledge synthesis and climate-resilient decision-making.

☆ HiSA: Sparsifying the Hidden Dimension of RoPE Attention in LLMs
Ambroise Borbely, Mathieu Salzmann, Olivier Verscheure
Attention in large language models (LLMs) scores every query against every key with a full dot product. We investigate whether this dot product must be computed completely for attention to be effective. We propose Hidden Sparse Attention (HiSA), which keeps the query and key projections intact but only computes a partial dot product, following a static, parameter-free rule set by the token positions: attention becomes sparse in the hidden (feature) dimension, an axis much less explored than token sparsity, rather than over tokens as in almost all sparse attention methods. Based on the structure of rotary position embeddings (RoPE), we decompose keys and queries into parts which can be omitted when computing attention. By training LLMs from scratch with HiSA, we find that this partial dot product does not hurt but improves performance compared with the complete product while computing only about a third of the query-key products. During inference HiSA improves decoding speed and shrinks memory footprint. We also extend HiSA to FlashAttention-3 kernels for training and inference. Finally, we motivate our approach by showing that dense attention learns to suppress the attention score parts that HiSA drops. Code is available at https://github.com/ambroise-b/hisa.
☆ Beam Model Technical Report
Reflection Team, Adam Abdulhamid, Osama Abuelsorour, Yasmin Alameddine, Carlo Alfano, Duarte Alves, Brandon Amos, Selvaraj Anandaraj, Arseni Anisimovich, George Antoniadis, Ioannis Antonoglou, Sheila Babayan, Neeraj Bahl, Märt Bakler, Vidhisha Balachandran, Cameron Banks, Thibaut Barroyer, Navroop Bath, Irwan Bello, Jeremy Berman, Fernanda Bernaldo, Mrityunjay Bhadauria, Sanyam Bhutani, Tolga Bolukbasi, Anudhyan Boral, Joseph Brennan, Ethan Brooks, Sam Buchanan, Anton Bulyenov, John Cai, Catherine Cang, Eason Chan, Elijah Chanakira, Nathan Chandrasekaran, Hao Chen, Hong-You Chen, Jeffrey Chen, Saurav Chhatrapati, Ting-Rui Chiang, Brian Tiger Chow, Aakanksha Chowdhery, Timothy Chung, Rob Claire, John D. Chenyu Li, Haoran Li, Rongzhao Li, Michelle Light, Boyan Lin, Hai Lin, Xiayue Charles Lin, Michael Linden, Ollie Liu, Wei Liu, Zichang Liu, Brian Lui, Amr Mahdi, Raunak Manjani, Kobe Martin, Lucas Sahni, Adrian Samatan, Simon Sandrew, Adriel Saporta, Rohan Savargaonkar, Vlad Savinov, Nick Schank, Jonathon Schwartz, Tim Seah, Ahmad Sharif, Miriam Shlimovich, Hamid Shojanazeri, Yusuf Sindi, Teak Sittipunt, Kurt Smith, Nicolas Sonnerat, Joe Spisak, Raunak Srivastava, Richard Steigerwald, Adam Stein, Stu Stewart, Arnaud Stiegler, Jingtong Su, Alicia Sun, Zoey Sun, Jakub Sygnowski, Douglas Teixeira, Chetan Tekur, Zachary Tellman, Helen Thatcher, Romal Thoppilan, Anil Tolwani, Konstantin Topaloglou-Mundy, Dmitry Ulyumdzhiev, Meet Vadakkanchery, Christopher Vanderschuere, Balaji Varadarajan, Rohan Varma, Balaji Veeramani, Andreas Verdelis, Risto Vuorio, Tom Walshe, Allen Wang, Ewen Wang, Taiyi Wang, Zach Wentz, Peter Wiese, Travis Wilson, Jason Winters, Sam Wiseman, Arvin Wu, Jin Wu, Kai Wu, Kenneth Xu, Mira Yadav, Xianjun Yang, Yao-Yuan Yang, Xiaolan You, Renault Young, Christina Yu, Biao Zhang, Gavin Zhang, Mingrui Zhang, Shenao Zhang, Jiesi Zhao, Mengjun Zhao, Xingchen Zhao, Mike Zhou, Tong Zhou, Zhenpeng Zhou, Tina Zhu
Beam is a sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model with 501.3B total parameters and 23.4B active parameters, specifically built and optimized for coding, reasoning, and agentic workloads. Beam delivers frontier level reasoning, while requiring 3-4x less inference compute than many models of its class. Pretrained on 23.8T diverse, curated, high-quality tokens, Beam features a 1M-token context window achieved through a novel two-stage midtraining phase, built on verifiable, correctness-anchored data. Post-training leveraged a large-scale, fully asynchronous reinforcement learning (RL) campaign across 10.5K GB300 GPUs, generating over 100M rollouts over 4 weeks of training. We believe this is one of the largest scale RL runs conducted by any open lab to date. Across our evaluation suite, capabilities continued to improve as we increased RL compute, with no sign of a plateau. Supported by deep hardware-model co-design, FP8/NVFP4 quantization, and a 1.93B-parameter speculative decoding drafter (DFlash 2), Beam maximizes serving throughput with efficient, flexible deployment. Averaged across conversation turns and harm categories, Beam refuses or pivots on 88.9% of harmful requests while complying with 81.5% of harmless requests, achieving the highest refusal/pivot rate on harmful requests among open-weight models on the Pareto frontier.
☆ One Framework to Diagnose Them All: A Stratified Benchmark for Bengali LLM Hallucination EACL 2027
Asif Shahriyar Sushmit, Shefayat E Shams Adib, Abdullah Al Jubaer Gem, Ahmed Shafin Ruhan, MD. Nazmuddoha Ansary, Biplab Kumar Sarkar, Syed Rifat Raiyan, Ajwad Abrar, Abdullah Bin Hasan, Adib Sakhawat, M. Sakeef Hossain, Ahmed Alfey Sani, Arefin Mizan, Sourab Chakma, Quazi Adibur Rahman Adib, Wassie Haque, Sanjay Kumar Sen, Saad Tahmid, Mohammad Mamun Or Rashid
Hallucination research in low-resource languages fragments easily: each newly named failure mode prompts its own dataset, spending scarce annotators on incomparable resources. We argue for consolidation and test it at community scale. Alikbachan is a Bengali hallucination-detection benchmark stratified along three crossed axes: a human-validated cultural-distance band, six task types, and a five-family, 32-mode hallucination taxonomy. It comprises a harvested 2,516-item test set (Phase 1) and a quota-constructed, gold-labeled 5,000-item set (Phase 2). The stratification is diagnostic. Four zero-shot LLM judges vary by over 30 macro-F1 points across strata, and the item-level predictions of 99 Phase-1 and 32 Phase-2 competition systems reproduce the profile: discourse-level omission defeats judges and most teams alike, whereas only a minority over-flag negative controls. The judges' verdict probabilities rank items well (ROC-AUC 0.74-0.95) but are overconfident (ECE 0.04-0.29). An open competition on Alikbachan drew 222 teams and 3,566 submissions, and a single-prompt 32B judge reaches the 57th percentile. Of the 25 teams in both phases, 24 regress on Phase 2 (mean $-17.6$ points) while every zero-shot judge improves, and Phase-1 rank barely predicts Phase-2 rank (Spearman $ρ=0.35$), so a Phase-1 leaderboard alone would have crowned a different winner.

☆ Language-Oriented Semantic Modeling for Target Language Extraction SC 2026
Mehmet Sinan Yıldırım, Ke Zhang, Shuai Wang, Haizhou Li
Target Language Extraction (TLE) aims to isolate speech in a target language from multilingual mixtures. Existing approaches primarily rely on acoustic modeling with language identity labels, which provide only coarse categorical guidance. In this work, we propose a language-oriented semantic modeling framework that augments acoustic extraction with language-specific semantic representations. A pre-trained speech semantic model is adapted via language-aware prompt conditioning and parameter-efficient fine-tuning to generate target-language-focused semantic representations from the mixture. {These capture high-level semantic structure of the acoustics and they} are integrated into the extraction model to provide structured semantic guidance beyond identity labels. Experiments on the CommonVoiceMix dataset show that incorporating semantic representations consistently improves over acoustic-only baselines, achieving up to 1.16 dB SI-SDR gain.

☆ BADAS-3.0: Real-Time Driving Perception and Planning from One Camera
Roni Goldshmidt, Hamish Scott, Lorenzo Niccolini
We ask how far a driving model can get with one camera and real-time latency. BADAS-3.0 is a 1B-parameter video encoder with four small heads. From two seconds of one forward camera, it predicts the current speed, the metric path just driven, a collision-risk score and six candidate futures over 6.4 seconds, in under 50 ms. Given the car's current speed, BADAS-3.0 has 44% lower minADE$_6$ over five trajectory benchmarks than the 34B Alpamayo 2 Super, which reads six cameras and runs over 100x slower. The gain comes from camera rigs that neither model was trained on. On the rig they share, the two are on par. Existing benchmarks are mostly nominal driving, so we add two open-loop safety benchmarks, one of real dashcam collisions and near-misses and one of Euro NCAP scenarios. On both, BADAS-3.0 has a higher pass rate than every published planner we tested.
☆ Twice-Told Critiques: Claim-Level Homogenization of Peer Review in the LLM Era
Ilya Gusev
Peer review relies on several readers finding different problems. We measure critique overlap in conference and journal reviews. At ICLR, measured overlap rose from 12.0% in 2026 to 21.3% in 2027, in reviews from the same stage of the review process, with year-on-year rises at NeurIPS, ICML and the F1000 and PLOS journals. A third ICLR review of a paper added 5.46 new problems to the other two in 2027, against 6.12 in 2026. We apply MELD, a detector of LLM-generated text, to all reviews. Within a paper, two reviews that MELD flags overlap more than two unflagged reviews: by 3.0 points at ICLR in 2024-2025, by 4.5 points in 2026-2027, and by 5.4 points across five journal sources. These results provide evidence that LLM-flagged reviews of one paper are less distinct from each other than unflagged reviews, and suggest that the diversity of critiques among reviews of a single paper is falling.
☆ What If Chain-of-Thought Is Not Enough?
Marc Jansen
Reasoning capabilities in large language models have evolved from prompting-based methods to internalized model behaviors. In this paper, we investigate whether the internal thinking model of an LLM can be deliberately changed and whether such a change is beneficial. Instead of relying on Chain-of-Thought, we train models to follow Question--Option--Criteria (QOC), a structured decision process that decomposes a task into explicit questions, competing options, and evaluation criteria. We present a three-generation development pipeline that ranges from a basic QOC decision core to richer answer synthesis and statistically enriched QOC reasoning. We then evaluate the resulting models with respect to both internal consistency and output quality. Across the reported experiments, QOC-based fine-tuning improves answer quality relative to non-fine-tuned base models and yields particularly strong gains once models are large enough to execute the underlying QOC computations reliably. The results suggest that the internal thinking model of an LLM is trainable and that structured alternatives to Chain-of-Thought can provide a promising path toward more reliable, interpretable, and task-adapted decision support.
☆ dLoop: Looped Diffusion Language Models via Recurrent Latent Refinement
Handa Li, Pengxiang Li, Mingyu Cao, Xilu Wang, Li Shen, Da Li, Lu Yin
Diffusion language models (DLMs) support parallel generation, yet they often require many denoising steps because only a fraction of token predictions are sufficiently reliable to be committed at each step. Existing acceleration methods primarily optimize confidence learning or commitment policies. However, these approaches operate on the outputs of a single denoising forward pass, in which information from the prompt, previously committed tokens, and uncommitted positions may not yet be fully integrated. Consequently, some predictions remain under-refined when the commitment decision is made, and modifying the commitment policy alone cannot improve their underlying representations. We introduce dLoop, which restructures a pretrained diffusion language model into a Prelude-Loop-Coda architecture and recurrently applies a parameter-shared intermediate block to the same partially masked state. Rather than advancing the denoising process, each additional loop provides further latent computation, allowing information distributed across uncommitted positions to continue interacting and become more fully integrated before commitment. Experiments on GSM8K, MATH, MBPP, and HumanEval show that dLoop achieves the highest or tied-highest accuracy on every benchmark. With tokens per forward pass (TPF) adjusted for loop computation, it outperforms Fast-dLLM in both accuracy and TPF on all four benchmarks and dParallel on three, and is more accurate than d3LLM on all four, while d3LLM attains higher TPF; no compared method surpasses dLoop in both. Mechanistic analyses show that extra loops mainly help on slow, unstable trajectories and that updates at uncommitted positions drive changes in token decisions and later generation. Pre-commitment latent computation thus makes predictions more reliable, offering a route to DLM acceleration distinct from forcing confidence through the training loss.
☆ STAGE: Spatio-Temporal Augmented Graph Expansion for Weakly Supervised Cross-Tweet Event Causality Discovery
Hieu Minh Duong, Todd Gary, Eugene Levin, Long Nguyen
Causal relations in disaster-related social media of- ten span independent posts, making cross-tweet causal discovery difficult to supervise and model at scale. We propose STAGE, a framework that separates scalable cross-tweet weak-supervision construction from spatio-temporal relational learning over event occurrences. Starting from local causal seeds, STAGE retrieves semantically related event occurrences across tweets and uses a causal scorer to construct weakly labeled cross-tweet pairs. These pairs supervise a multi-head Graph Attention Network over semantically enriched event representations and a spatio- temporal context graph. We evaluate STAGE on California Wildfire and Hurricane Harvey using human-gold cross-tweet test sets and 15 baselines. Compared with local-only supervision, the constructed cross-tweet supervision improves STAGE AUPRC by 0.340 on Wildfire and 0.107 on Hurricane. STAGE achieves in-domain AUROC of 0.885 on Wildfire and 0.710 on Hurricane. Cross-disaster experiments reveal asymmetric transfer, highlighting sensitivity to dataset-specific spatio-temporal shifts. These results demonstrate the value of scalable cross-tweet supervision combined with relational event modeling for causal learning across fragmented disaster reports.
☆ TwinLab-Bench: Evaluating AI Scientists in Agentic Twins of Real Labs
Yang Wan, Linchao Zhu
AI agents are increasingly expected to act as AI scientists, which requires three increasingly open abilities: carrying out a given computational workflow correctly, choosing the next measurements from a given design space, and designing their own experiments. Existing benchmarks mainly evaluate the first two abilities, in a terminal or in a closed loop that selects from predefined candidates. Evaluating the third ability requires reliably executing an experiment that an agent designs, with readings grounded in a real laboratory. We introduce TwinLab-Bench, which evaluates AI scientists on self-designed experiments in 52 tasks from laboratories in the physical sciences, the life sciences, and engineering. The benchmark supports more than 100 experimental action types, and the agent's report is scored by comparison with a hidden answer taken from real laboratory records. Each task has its own TwinLab agent, an agent that serves as the environment and executes the experiments that the tested agent designs, reading the raw materials of the laboratory and calling calculators that are built from its records. Compared with a language model that sees only the task description, the TwinLab agent almost eliminates readings that deviate from the real records and inconsistent answers to a repeated request, and closed-book probes control for answers recalled from memory. We find that these tasks remain hard for frontier models: the strongest, Claude Fable 5.2, averages a score of only 45.0, and we conduct an empirical study of where it fails.
comment: https://twinlab-bench.github.io/ contains tasks and code. 33 pages, 6 figures, 14 tables. LLM-agent benchmark for autonomous experimental design in digital twins of real labs (physics, chemistry, wet-lab biology, materials, engineering). 13 agents evaluated: Claude Fable 5.2 45.0, Opus 5.5 40.2, GPT-6.1-sol 31.7, plus DeepSeek, Qwen, Kimi, GLM and others
☆ Frontier models need only one text to unmask its human or AI author
Atrey Desai, Deniz Böloni-Turgut, Ty Brassington, Minjoon Choi, Jenna Russell, Rishanth Rajendhran, Bradley Emi, Hila Gonen, Hal Daumé, John Wieting, Marzena Karpinska, Mohit Iyyer
Given a single text and a simple zero-shot prompt (no web access, tools, candidate lists, or writing samples), frontier models can answer "who wrote this?" at three increasingly specific levels: (1) whether an AI or human wrote it, (2) if an AI, which lab, and (3) if a human, often exactly who. The latest models (Claude Fable 5.2, GPT-6.1 Sol, Gemini 4 Carbon) match trained detectors like Pangram while also explaining their predictions in natural language, and Opus 5.5 names the correct lab for 98% of texts generated by models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google. Most strikingly, Fable 5.2 correctly names the author of 39% of public peer reviews published in Oct-Nov 2026 (78% on a subset of reviews from Nature), and identifies the username behind 69% of comments from active Redditors (with >500 posts over the last year). Accuracy depends on exposure to an author's writing in pretraining data and on reasoning effort, and simply asking a model how familiar it is with someone predicts whether it can identify their writing. For anyone who has published enough writing online, frontier models present a greater threat to anonymity than commonly assumed, and defenses are limited.
☆ Learning to Think with Images Faithfully via Adaptive Data Selection
Yu Zhao, Jiaxin Zhang, Rohit Saxena, Fazl Barez, Pasquale Minervini, Yiwen Guo
Thinking with images (TwI) empowers vision-language models (VLMs) to overcome perceptual bottlenecks by actively cropping and zooming into fine-grained visual details, promising both higher accuracy and interpretable, region-grounded reasoning. However, our analysis shows that the improved accuracy of TwI models does not guarantee faithful tool use; they frequently invoke tools without actually relying on the zoomed region, undermining the reliability of tool-assisted reasoning. We trace this unfaithful behaviour to the training data: when questions are answerable without zooming, the model can answer correctly while ignoring the returned evidence, and this problem is exacerbated during training as the model becomes better at answering without tools. To keep zooming useful throughout training, we adapt the training data to the model's evolving capability and train on questions where correct zoomed evidence can still help the model answer correctly. This selection is supported by a data synthesis pipeline that localises small objects and constructs verifiable questions about their fine details, and complemented by an adaptiveness reward that favours tool-free answers when zooming is unnecessary. The experimental results show that our method substantially improves reasoning faithfulness and achieves state-of-the-art performance on visual search benchmarks (+11.6% on V*, +7.9% on HR-Bench 4K, and +9.4% on HR-Bench 8K). On multimodal hallucination benchmarks, our model also outperforms the compared TwI baselines. These results suggest that improving TwI faithfulness does not sacrifice accuracy and that both can be learnt from the right training data without directly supervising the reasoning process.
☆ Can World Models Predict Rice Growth? Toward Agricultural World Models
Wenkai Ji, Zeteng Li, Yinkun Guo, Jie Zhou, Liang He, Zhenong Jin
World models can imagine how a physical scene evolves over the next few seconds, but growers need to know how a crop will develop over weeks to months: will this rice plant have headed a month from now, and how will it look at harvest? We introduce agricultural world modeling, the task of predicting how the same plant will look at a requested horizon of days to months, from one photo of it, its cultivar, and its camera view. In preliminary analysis, even state-of-the-art image generators get this wrong: they often grow the plant too little or give it the color and form of the wrong growth stage. We argue that they do not work out the plant's developmental state, which the photo reveals only indirectly. We therefore propose AgroWM, which reasons about development before it draws growth. Its reasoning module, PhenoClock, works out the current growth stage, predicts the future one, and writes a Growth Script with numeric targets for height, plant area, and color; its generation module, the Rooted Renderer, then edits the photo to follow this script, adding new organs and colors to the photographed plant. Because pixel scores can prefer an unchanged copy of the photo, we instead check each forecast's traits and developmental timing against the plant's own later photos. On rice, 54.6% of the forecasts of AgroWM are matched to the target stage (Stage R@1), against 34.8% for GPT Image 2.5, 24.0% for Qwen Image 2.1, and 47.4% for the strongest domain-specific baseline, the same backbone fine-tuned without Growth Scripts; 9 to 20 weeks ahead, it still reaches 46.4%, against 35.7% for that baseline. On maize, a crop with a different body plan and stage scale, the same design again scores best on all four measures.
☆ RL Can Only Reinforce What It Sees Succeed
Ruben Sanchez-Corcuera, Unai Zulaika, Aritz Bilbao-Jayo, Miguel Fernandez-de-Retana
When a familiar rule stops working, an agent has to weigh what pre-training taught it against what it now observes. We ask how outcome-reward reinforcement learning (RL) changes this balance, in a puzzle world where both sides are known: small language models trained from scratch on games played by a Bayesian player, so that the prior and the right answer are exact. Base models hedge as their corpus did, and RL turns that hedging into decisions, in whichever direction the reward favours. But RL can only reinforce what it sees succeed. When the right action is rare, each run either learns it everywhere or not at all, and which runs take off is predicted before training by one count: the expected number of groups of attempts in which the action wins. About 35 such groups give even odds. The count holds for new and repeated problems and for groups of 4 to 256 attempts, and across learning rates, optimizers, GRPO variants and model sizes from 1.3M to 85M parameters, which move the threshold but not what is counted.

☆ Same Judgement, Different Scores: Numerical Scoring Topology in LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation EACL 2027
Doyun Kim, Chanwoo Kim, Jiwoo Kum, Minsoo Song, Chanjun Park
LLMs are increasingly used as automated evaluators to reduce the cost and time required for human annotation. However, the role of scoring-scheme design in the reliability of LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation remains underexplored. To address this problem, we introduce numerical scoring topology, which characterizes the structural design of a scoring scheme in terms of its scale width, granularity, offset, symmetry, and boundary placement. Building on this definition, we conduct the first systematic study of how numerical scoring topology influences zero-shot LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation across diverse benchmarks and judge models. Our results show that numerical scoring topology systematically affects judge scores, calibration, and robustness, particularly in judgment-intensive evaluations. Shifted and narrow ranges tend to induce some of the largest score deviations across many settings. We further find that stronger and larger judge models generally achieve higher discriminative validity and greater robustness to scoring-topology changes. These findings suggest that numerical scoring topology should be treated as an integral component of LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation and highlight the need for more principled and standardized scoring protocols.

☆ Understanding MoE Decisions Beyond Gradients
Zhen Xu, Zitao Zhang, Kevin Zhao, Xuefeng Liu
Understanding a language model's decision requires knowing how its internal computations affect its preference between answers. Gradient-based methods estimate this influence by predicting the effect of changing those computations. In mixture-of-experts (MoE) models, replacing one expert can change which experts later layers select. Those switches can alter the answer scores in ways that derivatives do not capture. We introduce a predictor that uses the model's own expert outputs and selection rules to estimate these extra effects, then adds them to a second-order prediction. Across questions from three benchmarks, our method reduces the error in predicting answer-score changes by 60\% on Qwen3.6 and 47\% on Gemma4, relative to a multistep baseline using gradients and second derivatives.
☆ AltRA-Test: An Alternative Annotator Test for LLM Judges Without Shared Annotators
Nagham Omar, Rotem Dror, Almog Gueta, Avigdor Gal, Roi Reichart, Nitay Calderon
Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as judges and graders to replace human annotators, raising the question of when such replacement is statistically justified. To support such decisions, the Alternative Annotator Test (Alt-Test) was introduced for dense settings where all items are annotated by the same annotators. However, in many large-scale annotation settings, items are annotated by different subsets of annotators. We therefore propose the Alternative Annotator Test with Resampled Annotations (AltRA-Test), a statistical test that does not require that all items are annotated by the same annotators. AltRA-Test uses a bootstrap that resamples items, draws k human annotations for each item, and substitutes one of them with an LLM annotation. This produces paired all-human and mixed human-LLM panels. A replacement is supported when the inter-annotator agreement (IAA) of the mixed panel is statistically non-inferior to the IAA of the all-human panel, i.e., when the LLM preserves the signal that the human annotators share. We establish that AltRA-Test is a consistent test of asymptotic level alpha. We then evaluate it through simulations and real-world experiments in which we test 18 LLMs and 5 prompting techniques on 20 datasets across text, image, audio, and video. Across these evaluations, about 100 items often suffice for the test. In simulations, where we know whether the LLM is a better or a worse annotator than the humans, AltRA-Test makes more correct replacement decisions than existing baselines. In real-world data experiments, LLMs pass the test in about half of the setups, depending mainly on the task. Since replacement is not always justified, we encourage researchers to test it for their task.
☆ SheetAlign: Grounded Interaction and Stage-Isolated Learning for Spreadsheet Agents
Yuxiang Wang, Junhao Gan, Jianzhong Qi
Spreadsheets are widely used for data analysis and supporting decision-making. Automating spreadsheet analysis while protecting sensitive data and controlling deployment costs motivates locally deployed open-weight large language models (LLMs). Meanwhile, complex spreadsheet tasks require both task reasoning and workbook understanding, while predefined operation interfaces impose additional tool-selection and invocation demands. We introduce SheetAlign, a spreadsheet manipulation framework with stage-isolated policy learning, organized around where to operate, what to change, and how to implement transformations. Task-guided inspection and API grounding anchor code generation in workbook observations and available operations, while stage-isolated learning optimizes program generation and review using edit-progress rewards. At inference, execution consistency selects programs through agreement among workbook states. On SpreadsheetBench, our grounded task interface improves Qwen3-4B's accuracy from 13.5% to 29.2% on average without training. With learning and execution consistency, SheetAlign achieves 32.8% accuracy on average on SpreadsheetBench and 30.1% on Domain-Spreadsheet, outperforming comparable baselines. The code will be made publicly available upon publication.

☆ Complete-SVD: A Functional Norm Approach to Low-Rank LLM Compression
Alper Kalle, Theo Rudkiewicz, Mohamed-Oumar Ouerfelli, Mohamed Tamaazousti
Post-training low-rank compression of neural networks must not only approximate the original weights but also preserve the model's input-output behavior. Recent output-aware methods measure the compression error beyond the compressed layer, at the model output, weighting logit changes by the observed next token or by the model's predicted probabilities. We propose Complete-SVD, a method for large language models that minimizes the $L_2$ distance between the logits of the original and compressed models, weighting all logit changes equally. Linearizing the network around the original weights, together with a Kronecker-factored approximation, reduces this objective to a weighted SVD with a closed-form solution. Its output-side factor is intractable to compute; we estimate it without bias from random probes, at a cost of $K$ backward passes per sequence, independent of vocabulary size and sequence length. At matched settings with uniform ranks and WikiText-2 calibration, Complete-SVD achieves the lowest C4 and PTB perplexity in every setting, and at ratio 0.7 under LEMS rank allocation. Replacing the $L_2$ distance by the Kullback-Leibler divergence (KL-SVD), with the same estimation framework, lowers calibration perplexity in most settings and raises downstream accuracy, but raises cross-corpus perplexity. Complete-SVD also improves IFEval instruction-following accuracy over the strongest prior output-aware methods throughout.

☆ Evaluating safeguarding in multi-turn conversations with AI assistants
Charlotte Peart, Franziska Sofia Hafner, Luc Rocher
Conversational AI assistants are now used for personal and emotional support. Developers report improvements in how new models respond to sensitive conversations, yet users and civil society continue to raise concerns about their behaviour in real-world, repeated interactions that safety evaluations rarely capture. Often, evaluations present signs of distress clearly and in a single message, even though people may reveal their struggles gradually in otherwise ordinary conversations. We worked with three community mental health support workers to test if models provide the same level of safeguarding when cues are shared all at once or gradually over time. We designed nine scenarios in which a user shares cues related to depression, psychosis, or domestic abuse. For each scenario, the support workers established a gold standard response, ranging from signposting support to pausing the everyday task to address safeguarding. We presented five cues either together in one message or dispersed across a conversation. Across more than 7,400 conversations, we evaluated six widely used models from five developers. GPT-5.5, Grok 4.3, DeepSeek V4, and Llama 4 Maverick met the support workers' standards in just 26-59% of conversations when cues were concentrated, and only 3-6% when dispersed. Claude Sonnet 5 and Claude Opus 5 showed a smaller gap, meeting the standards in 68-97% of concentrated conversations and 59-87% of dispersed conversations. Even the best-performing model, Claude Opus 5, fell short of the gold standard in 13% of conversations when cues were dispersed. Safety evaluations centred on explicit, legible concerns can overstate how well safeguards protect people when signs of distress emerge gradually or indirectly through ordinary conversations. For claims of improved safety to be taken seriously, developers must design and assess safeguards around how people actually communicate.
☆ Xing-Audio-NVVASR: Scaling and Refining Supervision for Joint Recognition of Speech and Non-Verbal Vocalizations
Pengyu Xu, Gang Liu, Genliang Zhao, Hongjie Chen, Jian Kang, Jie Lian, Jie Li, Yongxiang Li
Joint recognition of speech and non-verbal vocalizations (NVVs) is hindered by scarce event-rich training data and inaccurate or missing event labels. We present Xing-Audio-NVVASR, an end-to-end recognizer trained through progressive data mining and iterative supervision refinement. Guided by unified acoustic criteria, two heterogeneous models cross-audit annotations, flagging disagreements for teacher reannotation. To uncover shared omissions that model agreement may conceal, complementary breath detectors screen the entire corpus for human review. Human-confirmed labels accumulate across rounds and are preserved during subsequent refinement. Over five rounds, we refine annotations for 170,000 utterances from public corpora and incorporate 630,000 mined utterances. The resulting corpus of approximately 800,000 utterances is used to train a single 1.7B-parameter model that transcribes speech with inline tags for six NVV classes. Across four human-annotated benchmarks, Xing-Audio-NVVASR ranks first among nine models in both composite score and six-class Macro-F1. It achieves a mean composite score of 64.46, surpassing the strongest comparison model, StepAudio 3 Chat Preview, by 6.03 points, and exceeds the annotation teacher's breathing F1 by 14.40 to 31.57 percentage points. We plan to release the model weights, inference code, four evaluation benchmarks, annotation guidelines, and evaluation code.

☆ Policy Improvement-Guided Replay for Continual Reinforcement Learning
Qin-Wen Luo, Haoxuan Gao, Sheng Ren, Xiang Chen, Sheng-Jun Huang
Continual reinforcement learning (CRL) requires models to acquire new capabilities while retaining performance on previous tasks. A stability-oriented perspective common in conventional continual learning emphasizes knowledge preservation and interference mitigation. Complementing this perspective, we revisit CRL for vision-language models through policy improvement, a fundamental principle of RL, to preserve acquired performance gains. Using the performance difference lemma, we characterize historical performance changes through the current policy's expected advantages on historical task distributions, motivating replay of historical prompts with the current policy. To allocate limited replay memory, we propose Contrastive Likelihood Replay (CoLiR), which contrasts likelihood gains on correct responses from the final task policy with likelihood reductions on incorrect responses from the initial policy. Our analysis links correct-response likelihood gains to reference-policy advantages under an idealized KL-regularized optimal update and provides an endogenous-reward interpretation for general policy updates. CoLiR estimates scores from finite training rollouts and cached log-likelihoods, selects prompts at task boundaries, and replays them uniformly during subsequent training, with KL regularization restricted to replayed prompts. Memory construction only requires a small amount of additional cross-policy scoring but no additional response generation. Across three Qwen3-VL model sizes on MRCL, CoLiR achieves the highest mean final accuracy and mean task accuracy among the compared methods while maintaining competitive new-task performance.
☆ Energy estimates of open-weight large language models across stated food weights: a dose-response evaluation
Donato Riccio, Fabrizio Maturo, Iulia Brezeanu
We tested whether energy estimates of open-weight large language models (LLMs) scale proportionally with an exactly stated food weight, and whether computing energy density times weight removes the error. Under a prespecified protocol, seven open-weight LLMs estimated the energy of 168 randomly drawn foods from the USDA Food and Nutrient Database for Dietary Studies at nine stated weights, from 0.25 to 4 times the default amount. The primary outcome was the elasticity of the estimate with respect to weight (1: proportional scaling). A two-step estimate multiplied each model's own stated energy density by the weight in code. All seven models compressed their estimates, with elasticities from 0.548 to 0.979 (every 95% CI below 1); four compressed severely (0.548 to 0.709). These four overestimated a quarter of the default amount by 47% to 130% and underestimated four times it by 20% to 34%. Supplying the density restored near-proportional scaling in three of the four, and the two-step estimate cut the typical error factor at these extremes from 1.47-2.43 to 1.20-1.38. Compression persisted at full precision and grew in five of seven models under a plain question. The models hold the energy densities needed for proportional scaling and answer near a typical serving, reproducing the flat-slope syndrome of dietary recall. Accuracy at usual portions does not reveal it. Food-logging tools should compute the energy of weighed entries as density times weight, and evaluations should test fixed foods across stated weights.

☆ The Depth of a Prediction: Where Language Models Resolve a Word Predicts Human Reading Time
Donato Riccio, Fabrizio Maturo
Surprisal from a language model's final layer predicts how long people read a word, yet the model computes its prediction across many layers. We read each word's prediction at every layer with the tuned lens and summarize this layerwise surprisal trajectory by its resolution depth, the depth around which its change is centered. On eight English eye-tracking and self-paced reading corpora (81,957 words) and ten GPT-2, OPT and Pythia models, words that a model resolves later, and the words after them, are read more slowly than equally surprising words. Resolution depth improves held-out prediction beyond final-layer surprisal, entropy and the best single layer by 0.26 millinats per word (pooled p = 0.001), about a third of entropy's gain, and each model family gains, GPT-2 and Pythia through one corpus each. The net gain varies between corpora (random-effects mean 0.07, p = 0.37); against layer-shuffled depth, which adds as many coefficients, it is positive in seven of eight corpora (random-effects mean 0.23, p = 0.016), and untrained networks show no gain under the logit lens. Pooled over corpora, the effect holds with corrected word probabilities and beyond first-to-last-layer divergences, and a whole-trajectory functional regression adds no significant information beyond resolution depth.

☆ When Sounds Turn Heads: Recovering Event Timing That Audio LLMs Fail to Say
Jongbhin Woo, Jaewook Jeong, Joon Son Chung
Audio LLMs can know when a queried sound occurs but fail to say it. We find a few attention heads that track the queried sound: they shift when the query changes, follow the sound when it moves, and removing or blocking them often hurts the model's timestamps. Yet the answer does not express what they track. Intervals read directly from these heads outperform the model's original answer and recover events that its timestamps miss. To bring this timing into the answer, we introduce head-guided decoding, which needs no training. It keeps the model's decision on whether the sound is present and rescores its candidate timestamps with a prior from these heads. Heads selected on development data transfer unchanged to new recordings and free-text queries, where head-guided decoding improves the timestamps of three audio LLMs, scores above their head readout on every benchmark, and, in most settings, outperforms random-head guidance. On recordings released after the models were trained, head-guided decoding raises mIoU from 0.07-0.36 to 0.48-0.56. These results suggest that poor temporal answers in audio LLMs partly reflect a failure to say rather than to hear, and that the model's own attention can close part of this gap.

☆ Web search in Latin America: language models use less local and diverse news than search engines
Francisco W. Kerche, Franziska Sofia Hafner, Felix M. Simon, Luc Rocher
AI platforms are becoming a major gateway to news and current events. This shift risks amplifying existing geographical biases that favour narratives from wealthier countries over those from the Global South. This is particularly relevant in Latin America, which accounts for the largest share of AI usage in the Global South. We examine how information about Latin America changes when users shift from conventional search engines to LLM-based search. We construct a dataset of 1,901 current events across nine Latin American countries, craft realistic queries, and submit them to three search engines and four language models. We vary query language and user location to represent local, diaspora, tourist, and foreign user scenarios. Across 49,207 requests and 8,834 unique domains, language models return a less diverse set of news sources than search engines. Across user types, the share of domestic news returned by language models is inconsistent. For local users, while search engines already return only 41.3\% local news, language models return a further 3.6 to 11.6 percentage points less. Taken together, our findings suggest that LLM-based search can reproduce and, in some settings, amplify geographical hierarchies in the reach and visibility of news sources. We release an open dataset to support multilingual and geographically diverse evaluation of LLM-based search, and highlight implications for more equitable information systems design.

☆ Many Solutions, One Answer: Search Order Shapes the Default Solutions of Language Models
Qinhan Hou, Jing Tang
When a constraint puzzle has several correct answers, language models tend to give the same one.We study four open-weight models from three families on symbolic puzzles and multi-solution ZebraLogic variants with exactly known solution sets.Repeated samples concentrate on one default solution far beyond uniform sampling, and different models often share it.Yet models usually produce even rarely sampled solutions when added clues uniquely identify them.On held-out symbolic puzzles, we compare visiting entities in name order with visiting those with the fewest initial candidates first. Both searches try values in the stated order. Where their pre-specified predictions differ, the latter matches a larger share of responses in all four models, by 26 to 40 percentage points.Reversing the stated value order or specifying a search order shifts responses towards the solution that the new order favours, on both testbeds. ☆ The Linguistic Structure of Dependency-Conditioned Embeddings
Bach Phan-Tat, Tung Kieu, Kris Heylen, Dirk Geeraerts, Stefano De Pascale, Dirk Speelman
Dependency-based distributional models capture syntactically informed lexical similarities, but the geometric organisation of dependency-conditioned lexical representations remains underexplored. We investigate the global and local geometry of lemma-dependency relation embeddings using four representations trained on WikiText-103: two count-based PPMI configurations and two Skip-Gram with Negative Sampling (SGNS) configurations. At the global level, we analyse dependency-relation centroids, control for lexical identity through within-lemma centring, and examine their alignment with part-of-speech (POS) distributions. At the local level, we evaluate cross-relation geometric consistency, cross-method agreement, lexical similarity, and polysemous usages. Results show that global geometry is structured strongly by context POS across all representations. Locally, broader lexical similarity structures are partially preserved across dependency relations, while immediate neighbourhoods vary substantially, highlighting different usages. PPMI-based representations exhibit greater cross-relation consistency than SGNS-based representations, with cross-method agreement varying by relation. These findings reveal systematic linguistic organisation at multiple geometric scales and its sensitivity to representation choices.
☆ Verifiable Logic in Medical LLMs: A Critical Review of Agentic Clinical Reasoning Optimization and Neuro-Symbolic Grounding
Youssef ElNahas, Adel Ammar, Soumaya Chaffar, Wadii Boulila
Clinical large language models are usually judged by whether the final answer is right. Yet a model can reach a fluent, plausible output through invalid reasoning that static benchmarks do not detect, and confident hallucination, causally invalid inference, and spurious demographic shortcuts can pass conventional evaluation. This critical review covers four paradigms in turn: how clinical hallucination arises and why static metrics miss it; the agentic hallucination cascade, in which multi-agent and tool-augmented systems propagate and amplify early reasoning errors; process reward modeling as step-level supervision; and neuro-symbolic grounding, in which formal logic engines, ontology reasoners, and knowledge graphs place verifiable constraints on neural output. Within their encoded domains, systems with symbolic veto layers offer guarantees that hold relative to their encoded knowledge, and they report large gains on the safety measures they use. These results come from single studies, mostly in simulated or small-sample settings, and are not directly comparable with neural baselines. Process reward models remain bounded by the statistics of their training data. We propose a conceptual framework that maps each agentic failure mode to a symbolic intervention point, and we argue that clinical AI safety depends substantially on system architecture. Because current regulation emphasizes performance and does not require verification of reasoning steps, we argue that responsible certification should also audit reasoning trajectories alongside accuracy on static benchmarks.
☆ One Schedule Does Not Fit All: Adaptive Inference Schedules for Diffusion Language Models
Rabimba Karanjai, Linsey Pang, Hemanth Hegadehalli Madhavarao, Richard Williamson, Uma Kona, Prakhar Mehrotra
Diffusion language models typically use a fixed denoising schedule across workloads. We show that the cheapest schedule that preserves quality depends on the task, model, and sampler. We propose Task-Calibrated Denoising Allocation (TCDA), a deployment rule with an explicit error guarantee. Given candidate samplers, paired calibration data, a utility tolerance, and guardrails against degeneration, TCDA uses simultaneous one-sided bootstrap bounds to certify which candidates meet both the utility and guardrail criteria. It deploys the cheapest certified candidate or falls back to the full schedule. The probability of deploying an infeasible schedule is asymptotically at most alpha. Candidates include early prefix decoding, state-reusing continuation, reduced time grids, and confidence-threshold decoding. Across seven datasets and two model families, TCDA certifies reductions in denoiser evaluations by factors of 3.4 on XSum and 2.0 on WMT14 for ELF-B; 8.0 on WMT14 and 4.1 on GSM8K for LLaDA-8B; and 1.33 on OpenWebText for both ELF-M and ELF-L. A validation protocol fixed before generation confirmed every selection on fresh seeds. Across 500 calibration splits, false certification remained below 2% in every setting at a nominal level of 5%. Certified schedules vary across tasks and model scales. Trajectory measurements suggest that when decoded tokens commit is more informative for schedule selection than the remaining latent discretization error. We also find that the ELF reference sampler draws its time grid at random, so reproducible schedule comparisons for unconditional generation require fixing the grid.
☆ HALTT: A HAte Lexicon Through Time
Dario Franzese, Farah Benamara, Véronique Moriceau, Viviana Patti
Automated hate speech detection degrades over time as everyday vocabulary is weaponized into covert slurs while retaining original benign meanings (polysemy retention). Existing lexical semantic change detection (LSCD) frameworks struggle with this phenomenon due to a lack of standardized, sense-delineated diachronic resources. We propose a multi-stage framework to construct and validate a diachronic lexicon of semantically shifted hate speech terms. Filtering > 20,000 candidates, our pipeline combines historical (1770-1964) and modern (2001-2024) corpus frequency grounding, masked definition-level scoring to mitigate LLM temporal bias, and a dual-persona reasoning prompt paradigm. We release HALTT, a grounded lexicon of 19 polysemous terms with literal and derogatory senses, analyze shift typologies, and establish evaluation protocols for downstream diachronic benchmarking.
☆ TableRoute: Capability-Aware Routing for Scalable Table Question Answering
Yuxiang Wang, Junhao Gan, Jianzhong Qi
Table question answering with large language models remains difficult to scale: tables range from compact to million-token sizes, while questions require precise grounding of columns, values, and computations. Existing methods often specialize in a fixed inference strategy, which is brittle across different table scales and model capabilities. Full-table reading is efficient for compact inputs but suffers from irrelevant context. Table reduction helps long inputs but depends on reliable evidence localization, while execution is required when tables exceed the model context window and remains sensitive to schema and value grounding errors. We propose TableRoute, a capability-aware routing framework with evidence-conditioned inference for scalable TableQA. TableRoute first generates validated question--table evidence and identifies the required operations, then routes each instance among these three strategies according to serialized table length and calibrated estimates of how reliably the base model handles these operations. The validated evidence supports the selected route by grounding columns, values, and conditions. Across WikiTQ, WTQ-L, BirdQA, and TableBench, TableRoute consistently improves accuracy through complementary routing and evidence conditioning across table scales, while reducing online token cost. Code will be made available upon publication.
☆ Anchor and Check: Deterministic Cross-Prompt, Cross-Model Reconciliation for Auditable LLM Decisions
Sivakameswara Reddy Chejarla
Pipelines that let a large language model (LLM) make discrete decisions need to know which decisions they may act on unattended, and a record that explains each one. We study a small, fully specified reconciliation contract: commit the target model's greedy answer (the anchor), draw one or two greedy checks that reach the answer by a different route (an alternate prompt, or a model from another family), and let a pure function return one of five outcomes, of which only Agree is acted on. On 48,000 model-item evaluations over 6,000 questions, with every method given access to verbalized confidence as a tie-breaker, the main finding concerns where calls come from: pooling another family's samples raises semantic entropy's AUROC by 1.7 to 4.0 points at every budget, and a same-model temperature check is the weakest design. As complete pipelines, the point estimates of the three-call contract and pooled semantic entropy over 3 or 5 samples differ by less than one point, with intervals containing zero. As detectors of the anchor's errors, the contract is ahead of pooled entropy with 3 and 5 samples under both tie-breaker conventions; with 10 samples the point estimates differ by less than one point and the sign depends on the convention. Under the strict Agree gate it automates 70% of items and still acts on 16% of errors. What the contract adds is a fixed committed answer, an explicit outcome and a decision replayable from its record; generation itself was not reproducible on the hosted API.

☆ What Does Anti-Hallucination Training Buy? Saying Less, Cheap Pruning, and the Rest
Lingkai Bu, Qian Gao
Preference training is the standard recipe for reducing object hallucination in vision-language models, and each new method reports a lower CHAIR score. We ask what that reduction consists of. Every trained system is compared with its base model's caption truncated to the same object recall, which removes the part of the gain that comes from saying less; the remainder is compared with what a deliberately weak, frozen, eleven-feature post-hoc probe fitted on 500 annotated images can reproduce. All criteria were specified in the analysis code and frozen before the outputs they judge existed. For eight public methods on LLaVA-1.5-7B, the probe reproduces at least two thirds of what training removes beyond saying less for 7 of 8 methods on COCO, 8 of 8 on AMBER and 6 of 8 on Object HalBench, with RLAIF-V the consistent exception; a later public method released for both LLaVA-1.5 and Qwen2.5-VL (SENTINEL) meets the same bar on both. On our own DPO checkpoints the accounting holds on Qwen2.5-VL but fails on InternVL2.5 on COCO (2 of 4 training designs). At matched recall, the base model plus the probe beats truncation for 8 of 9 LLaVA-1.5 systems on COCO and 9 of 9 on AMBER, on the two newer backbones, and when realized as actual sentence deletion. The same measure prices training signals: DPO on the model's own samples labelled by the probe or by an object-level CLIP score recovers 0.70 and 0.87 of the beyond-brevity gain of ground-truth-labelled DPO on Qwen2.5-VL (the probe 0.73 on InternVL2.5), both beat length-matched random labels, and CLIP labels buy more than probe labels on COCO. Human judgements show that the sentences the probe deletes are wrong more often than those truncation would delete; a human check of the CHAIR evaluator's precision falls below our pre-set bar, which we report as a limitation.
☆ Efficient and Robust Drafting with Multi-Token Prediction
Filippo Galgani, Ziwei Ji, Omri Homburger, Ganesh Jawahar, Michelle Ramanovich, Ivan Korotkov, Luciano Martins, Utku Evci, Edouard Yvinec, Pooja Consul, Vivek Kumar, Tal Schuster
Speculative decoding has emerged as a critical technique for accelerating Large Language Model (LLM) inference by decoupling generation into a drafting phase and a parallel verification phase. A crucial challenge is therefore designing a drafter that is both lightweight and accurate enough to approximate the predictions of the target model. We present a highly optimized Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) architecture that extends existing backbone LLMs to efficiently produce high-quality drafts. Built around a single shared drafting head, our approach features three core architectural optimizations: target activation injection to anchor semantic planning, zero-copy KV-cache cross-attention to bypass prefill compute and reduce memory overhead, and an efficient clustered vocabulary projection to reduce the cost of full-vocabulary decoding. Testing these improvements with the Gemma 4 model family, our G-MTP shows out-of-the-box speedups for both large server-side setups (up to 3.1$\times$) and smaller models targeting memory-constrained on-device environments (up to 2.3$\times$ on H100 and up to 2.2$\times$ on a smartphone GPU). In addition, we present parameter-efficient fine-tuning recipes for G-MTP that keep the drafter aligned with the backbone LLM under domain shift, requiring up to 1.83$\times$ fewer target forward passes than tuning only the backbone.
☆ Language models represent more about emphasis than they can tell you
Aarne Talman, Stergios Chatzikyriakidis
Prosodic prominence can largely be predicted from the words of a sentence, but part of it depends on how the speaker reads it. We study how much of prominence language models recover from text, whether they can report it when asked, and what the speech signal adds. On read English speech labelled for word-level prominence, we compare frozen and fine-tuned text models, prompted large language models (LLMs) and speech models on the same words. All text models end within about one point of each other, at best 68.8% on the 3-way task, and largely make the same errors. With the prompts we tested, LLMs do no better than a word-majority baseline, even with 500 examples. Linear probes find more in their representations. Speech models reach 84.7%. We argue that text determines only part of prominence, and that the rest has to be heard. LLMs encode what the text determines, but prompting does not bring it out: they represent more about where the emphasis falls than they tell you.
☆ RouteVerify: Routing Interventions for Step-Level Verification in Mixture-of-Experts Models
Yucheng Du, Ryan A. Rossi, Xiyang Hu
Routing states in mixture-of-experts (MoE) models can distinguish correct from erroneous reasoning steps. If changing routing also changes a model's step-level verification judgment, it is tempting to conclude that the judgment follows what routing encodes. We test this inference with RouteVerify, a controlled intervention study that replaces selected internal activations from one solution trace with activations from another, while keeping the evaluated input unchanged. Across nine MoE checkpoints, we compare three intervention interfaces: router logits, MoE outputs, and post-MoE residual states. We vary the layers and token positions at which activations are replaced. We measure changes in a verification margin that quantifies the model's preference for INCORRECT over CORRECT. We also evaluate the same input with activations from correct versus erroneous steps to test whether the resulting judgment depends on the source step's correctness. We report four linked results. First, probes on router logits separate correct from erroneous steps in eight checkpoints (AUROC 0.80-0.88). Second, replacing router logits across all routed layers changes the judgment in all nine checkpoints, and by more than a norm-matched random replacement. Third, this change rarely depends on whether the source step is correct. At one layer, routing from erroneous steps and from correct steps produces indistinguishable margins in all nine checkpoints. Under all-layer routing, their difference is resolved and positive in only one. Fourth, the same difference is positive for local residual replacements in eight of nine checkpoints, so the measure does detect such dependence where it exists. Correctness is therefore readable from routing, and routing replacements move the judgment, but the movement rarely follows correctness.

☆ Closing Performance Gap for Diffusion Language Models under Low Inference Budgets
Hao Wu, Sipeng Chen, Jingwen Fu, Yuwang Wang
Diffusion language models offer an architectural route to low-budget generation by updating multiple tokens in parallel, which can reduce the number of sequential decoding steps relative to token-by-token autoregressive decoding. However, realizing this advantage at low budgets is challenging, as generation quality often declines when the number of denoising and refinement steps is reduced. Editable diffusion models address this quality loss through post-draft refinement, but useful intermediate results may still be lost before final submission. We introduce CaRR (Commitment-aware Refinement and Recovery), a training-free framework which improves submission quality from a fixed editable checkpoint through updated-context refill and trajectory-based recovery within its existing forward schedule. Token Commitment Remasking (TCR) remasks selected native pure replacements and refills them on the next scheduled edit forward using the updated context. Submission Commitment Recovery (SCR) uses the resulting refinement trajectory to recover supported candidates when the final response does not satisfy task-specific output requirements. Both modules operate within the native mask/edit schedule without additional backbone forwards. Experiments on code generation and mathematical reasoning show consistent gains in average accuracy over native ME-DLM across four inference budgets, with the largest gains under tight budgets. At one-eighth of the full scheduled forward budget, CaRR improves the six-metric average accuracy by 7.44 pp, including gains of 11.37 and 10.05 on MBPP and MBPP+.

☆ Verification Behavior Across Five Qwen2.5-Instruct Checkpoints
YaJie Yin
We evaluate five Qwen2.5-Instruct checkpoints (0.5B to 32B) under four prompting conditions using three fixed tasks per information condition and five generations per task. Two AI raters scored 600 trajectories. Under the coordinated instruction, both raters recorded strict declarations of insufficiency in 11/15 cases at 7B, 0/15 at 14B, and 5/15 at 32B. The runner sent a standard follow-up when a request detector fired. The three checkpoints received at least one follow-up in 15, 12, and 15 cases, respectively. On information-sufficient tasks at 3B through 32B, scored format markers appeared in 60/60 protocol trajectories and 0/60 baseline trajectories. ☆ PosteriorBench: Evaluating Behavior Posterior Sampling in Language Models
Zhiyang Xun, Daniel D. Johnson, Sarah Schwettmann, Neil Chowdhury
Rare language model behaviors can be difficult to study because obtaining them through ordinary sampling can require a very large number of samples. This motivates the behavior posterior sampling problem: given a language model and a behavior of interest, is there an algorithm that efficiently samples from the model's distribution over transcripts that exhibit this behavior? We introduce PosteriorBench, a benchmark for comparing algorithms by their compute cost and by how close their samples are to the true posterior in forward and reverse KL divergence. PosteriorBench contains 21 tasks on Qwen3.5-0.8B with behavior probabilities from $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-1}$, each with exact posterior samples from a corpus of one billion labeled responses. We find that no method we evaluate is both efficient and faithful: simple methods like prompting are cheap but plateau at a high KL divergence from the posterior, fine-tuning on posterior samples is often cost-prohibitive, and several training methods from prior work do not perform better. Building a posterior sampler that can cheaply approximate the true posterior remains an open problem, and we hope PosteriorBench can measure progress towards this objective. Code and data are available at https://github.com/TransluceAI/PosteriorBench.
☆ VR-SparseQuant: Accurate FP4 Quantization with 2:4 Sparsity on Vera Rubin
Samir Khaki, Wei-Chen Wang, Song Han, Ryan Hanrui Wang
In recent years, GPUs have incorporated increasingly specialized support for low-precision computation, from Hopper's FP8 to Blackwell's FP4 Tensor Cores. Precision formats have evolved alongside this hardware, from MXFP4 and NVFP4 on Blackwell to E2M1 FP4 with direct UE5M3 block scaling on Vera Rubin, which we term as $\boldsymbol{VRFP4}$. These advances help serve larger language models at lower cost, but four-bit activation quantization can substantially degrade model quality. We introduce VR-SparseQuant, which uses Vera Rubin's elementwise 2:4 sparsity to recover accuracy while retaining native FP4 execution. By allowing two sparse streams to overlap, we use the same FP4 value budget as dense quantization to refine activations with larger errors. Offline scale-ratio calibration avoids runtime search, while a shared-weight GEMM accumulates both streams efficiently. On Nemotron-3-Nano-30B-A3B, VR-SparseQuant reduces WikiText-2 perplexity from $\boldsymbol{6.85}$ to $\boldsymbol{5.57}$ versus dense RTN W4A4. Including activation preparation, our native Rubin kernels for VRFP4 achieve up to $\boldsymbol{3.1\times}$ GEMM and $\boldsymbol{2.7\times}$ MoE speedup over BF16. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study of adaptive activation quantization using Vera Rubin's elementwise 2:4 structured sparse FP4 instructions.
