☆ STARK: Moving towards Personalized Agents That Truly Learn and Remember
Aryan Jain, Abhishek Jha, Anish Nediyanchath, Promod Yenigalla
Long-term memory is essential for personalized agentic systems that interact with users over long time periods, yet current architectures only capture what happened while leaving the reasoning of why it happened to be derived later by underlying LLMs.%, and silo knowledge within individual users. These events can, however, convey not only what a user experienced but also how that experience shaped their behavioral patterns. We introduce STARK, a dual-graph memory architecture which treats these events not just as nodes but also as edges between behavioral insights. STARK maintains an Episodic Graph which records interactions as temporally, causally, and semantically linked episodes; and an Insight Graph which represents LLM-extracted behavioral signals scored by how many events support them, connected by the very events that led to them. At inference, behavioral knowledge is retrieved directly from insight nodes grounded by event-as-edges, eliminating repeated episodic traversal. The event-as-edge structure also naturally enables projecting the knowledge graph into a relational embedding space where cross-user transfer becomes possible under shared contextual circumstances, allowing the system to infer missing insights for cold-start users. Evaluations show that STARK improves by 1.48 judge score on LongMemEval information extraction and 0.93 on LoCoMo open domain questions over state of the art. On the public LongLaMP abstract-generation benchmark, cross-user transfer improves open-ended judge scores by 0.7 points and ROUGE-L by 3 at 5% graph sparsity.
☆ From Geographic Memory to Spatial Generalization in Large Language Models
Chen Chu, Zhaonan Wang, Cyrus Shahabi, Hengcai Zhang, Feng Lu
Large language models (LLMs) can associate places with geographic descriptions, but recovering an observed association does not establish an ability to generalize across geographic space. We distinguish geospatial memory, the recovery of location--description associations observed during training, from spatial generalization, the prediction of descriptions for locations in unobserved regions. We investigate whether continuous location representations improve this generalization and encourage geographic knowledge to become spatially organized within an LLM. We frame this organization as a latent spatial field: a learned mapping from locations to descriptions connected through spatial dependencies. Building on a continuous spatial encoder, we project location representations into the model's embedding space and supply them as spatial tokens. We evaluate the resulting models on two newly curated geo-text benchmarks for Los Angeles and London using two complementary assessments. Geospatial captioning tests both memory and spatial generalization by generating descriptions at observed locations and at locations beyond the spatial coverage of the training observations. A separate geospatial grounding model tests whether generated location--description pairs provide useful additional supervision for mapping text to geographic space. Across multiple LLM families, continuous location representations improve captioning at unobserved locations relative to textual coordinates, while their generated descriptions yield modest but consistent improvements in downstream grounding. Layer-wise probing and representation clustering provide additional evidence consistent with spatially organized geographic representations. Together, these findings show the value of evaluating spatial generalization separately from memory and of incorporating continuous spatial representations into language models.
☆ FoodReact-AR: Learning Audience Reactions that Zero-Shot LLMs Miss in Arabic Food-Insecurity Discourse
Wajdi Zaghouani, Mabrouka Bessghaier, Salma Azouzi, Shimaa Amer Ibrahim
Facebook reactions record how an audience received a post, a signal absent from the post's text. We treat the six differentiated reactions on 74,241 Arabic food-insecurity posts from public Facebook pages (2020-2024) as compositions, apply a centered log-ratio transformation, and cluster them into four audience profiles: Solidarity (Love-dominant), Dismissal (Haha-dominant), Grief (Sad-dominant), and a flat Ambivalent profile. Dismissal peaks in 2022 (22.3%), the year of the wheat price shock, and grief in 2024 (14.0%), during the Gaza food emergency; a page-clustered GEE confirms both shifts (odds ratios 2.18 and 1.34), and they survive minimum-reaction filtering, alternative zero replacements and clustering methods, and bootstrap resampling. We then test whether the profiles can be predicted without reactions. A zero-shot Arabic LLM recovers Grief for 83.8% of posts but Dismissal for only 3.4%, and in a matched ablation, it shows the reaction percentages do not help. A simple classifier trained on the reaction-derived labels and tested on unseen pages recovers dismissal for 60.0% (macro-F1 0.56 against 0.43), mainly from mentions of officials and institutions. Derisive audience responses are thus learnable from text, yet a zero-shot LLM misses them. We release the corpus, LLM, and human annotation layers, and an evaluation protocol as FoodReact-AR.
☆ SEAF: Selective Semantic Enrichment and Visual Evidence Fusion for Multimodal Sarcasm Detection
Faxian Wan, Xiaocui Yang, Yifan Cao, Shi Feng, Daling Wang, Yifei Zhang
Multimodal sarcasm detection requires textual, visual, and contextual evidence, particularly when multiple images provide complementary cues. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate auxiliary information from a post's text and images, but their outputs may mix useful background knowledge with redundant visual descriptions. We propose SEAF, a dual-branch framework for selective semantic enrichment and visual evidence fusion. SEAF assigns generated fields to distinct roles: entity background enriches semantic input, while image descriptions supply alignment statistics. The semantic branch combines the original post text, background from a frozen MLLM, and optical character recognition (OCR) text. The aligned visual branch combines global and local visual features with statistics of cross-modal and inter-image similarity. Text-Anchored Smoothed Aggregation (TASA) weights images by their similarity to the original post text while preserving contributions from images with low literal similarity. Summing the branch classification scores integrates contextual semantics with visual evidence. Experiments on the multi-image MMSD3.0 benchmark show that SEAF outperforms all compared methods.
☆ ColorRef: Calibrating Verbal Feedback for Color Revision
Pablo Loyola, Andres Hoyos-Idrobo, Edison Marrese-Taylor, Colin Rowat, Alfredo Solano
People rarely specify an exact color when asking someone to revise a design: they might say \textit{a little darker} or \textit{more blue} after seeing a proposal. A language-model assistant must then infer not only which way to change the color, but how far. We study this distinction with \textsc{ColorRef}, a color reference game in which a model guesses a color from a description and a teacher, knowing a recorded target hidden from the guesser, supplies verbal corrections. On 1{,}000 crowd-sourced description--target pairs, three rounds of deterministic oracle feedback reduce mean target distance from 42.33 to 22.15 $ΔE_{76}$. However, those corrections provide additional information, and improvement alone does not establish well-calibrated feedback following. A controlled Qwen3-14B diagnostic reveals a more specific failure: following a requested direction and ordering graded phrases do not guarantee a suitable step size; strong modifiers can induce excessive changes. We therefore measure each receiver's typical response to \textit{a little}, \textit{somewhat}, and \textit{much} on separate colors and select wording matched to its observed steps. On 128 held-out starting colors, calibration reduces one-step mean error from 12.36 to 6.86 for Qwen3-14B and from 11.06 to 5.89 for Gemma 4 12B against a fixed graded-wording rule. The benefit is concentrated on large corrections, with many unchanged cases and no consistent increase in threshold success. Our findings show that following the direction of feedback is not the same as making an appropriately sized revision.
☆ TRAC: Efficient Diffusion Language Models for Machine Translation via Length-Aware Decoding
Tianyuan Li, Xihan Yang, Chenglong Wang, Yuzhang Wu, Jingbo Zhu, Tong Xiao
Machine translation with large language models requires both high translation quality and low generation latency. Accelerating translation while preserving quality is therefore essential for practical deployment. Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) can generate tokens at arbitrary target positions through iterative denoising, offering a new paradigm for parallel translation beyond sequential autoregressive decoding. However, existing methods still incur redundant computation: fixed target generation lengths waste computation on unused positions, while confidence-only commitment delays medium-confidence tokens whose predictions have largely stabilized. To address these issues, we propose TRAC, a training-free adaptive decoding method for diffusion machine translation. TRAC adapts the target generation length using source length and translation direction, with a conservative fallback to the maximum length for empty or unusually short outputs. It preserves high-confidence commitment while identifying temporally stable medium-confidence tokens for early commitment using cross-step top-1 persistence and approximate Jensen-Shannon divergence over Top-$K$ distributions. On bidirectional Chinese-English translation across FLORES-200, WMT23, and NTREX-128, TRAC reduces model forward calls by 24.9% to 36.8% and achieves sentence-level generation speedups of 1.91 to 2.19 times over Fast-dLLM, with only a 0.02-point decrease in macro-average COMET. These results demonstrate that adaptive generation lengths and stability-aware token commitment improve the efficiency of diffusion machine translation.

☆ Generative News Recommendation Reproduces Popularity: A Memorization-Based Analysis
Shotaro Ishihara
Text-based generative recommenders can go beyond ranking existing items by directly generating item titles or other textual representations. In the news domain, this raises a societal concern that models may repeatedly surface already popular articles and thereby narrow the diversity of information to which users are exposed. We investigate training-data memorization as one mechanism behind this concentration. Using approximately 20 million browsing sessions from a Japanese news service, we continually trained an instruction-tuned Llama 3 8B model to generate the next article title in a session. The generated-title distribution was more concentrated and less diverse than the distribution of titles observed in the browsing logs. Frequently repeated target titles exhibited greater verbatim and approximate memorization, and more strongly memorized titles were generated in response to a wider range of prompts. These findings support a pathway in which popularity in behavioral logs becomes repeated training text, is preferentially memorized, and is subsequently reproduced by a generative recommender. An exploratory target-level deduplication condition reduced both memorization and output concentration, while substantially reducing the training-set size and recommendation accuracy. Our results suggest that popularity bias in generative recommendation may arise not only from user preferences or ranking objectives, but also from the model's tendency to reproduce frequently encountered strings, with important implications for information diversity on news platforms.

☆ Rubric Semantics or Numerical Priors? Auditing Scale-Direction Bias in LLM Judges EACL 2027
Chanwoo Kim, Doyun Kim, Minsoo Song, Sihyeon Lee, Chanjun Park
Recent benchmark evaluations increasingly rely on Large Language Models (LLMs) as automatic judges. This paradigm assumes LLMs evaluate based on the rubric's semantic meaning rather than its numeric scale. We question this assumption by introducing semantic faithfulness: the principle that semantically equivalent rubrics must yield consistent scores. To quantify this, we propose a scale-reversal probe that inverts score anchors without altering evaluation criteria. Furthermore, we introduce floor normalization to calibrate reversal disagreement against intrinsic model variability, enabling fair cross-model comparisons. Experiments across five meta-evaluation benchmarks with seven diverse LLM judges spanning open-weight and proprietary models reveal widespread violations of semantic faithfulness, showing that raw reversal agreement alone is misleading. Finally, we demonstrate that bidirectional anchor averaging reduces dependence on any single anchor direction without compromising alignment with human judgments. Our findings emphasize that trustworthy LLM judges must reliably follow rubric semantics, not just correlate with human ratings.

♻ ☆ Shortcuts or Memorisation? Revisiting Data Corruption in Natural Language Understanding with Large Language Models
Aarne Talman, Stergios Chatzikyriakidis
Pre-trained encoders fine-tuned on natural language inference (NLI) and other language understanding tasks keep much of their accuracy after the input is perturbed, e.g. by removing all nouns or shuffling the words. Does the same hold for large language models (LLMs), and if so, is it because they have seen the benchmarks during pre-training? In this paper we revisit these perturbation experiments with eight LLMs alongside fine-tuned encoders on GLUE and the three rounds of ANLI. Regardless of model size, every system that answers directly retains 36-72% of its above-chance performance without nouns on MNLI compared to only 14-29% on ANLI. Only a model that reasons before answering departs from this pattern. We also check contamination indicators (including exact $n$-gram counts in the public pre-training data of two of the models) and find no link between exposure and robustness. Our strongest evidence comes from 368 new, human-validated items that none of the models can have seen. All eight LLMs keep more of their performance without nouns on items written in MNLI's style than on items written without its cues. When the perturbation instead removes the function words carrying such cues, this advantage reverses. In light of these results, we argue that robustness to perturbation on familiar NLI benchmarks reflects dataset shortcuts rather than memorisation, and tells us more about the benchmark than about the model.
♻ ☆ Capturing In-Context Learning Dynamics with Task Operators NeurIPS 2026
Guangzhi Xiong, Zhenghao He, Bohan Liu, Sanchit Sinha, Wenqian Ye, Aidong Zhang
In-context learning (ICL) enables language models to perform new tasks from demonstrations without weight updates. However, every ICL inference requires processing the full set of examples, resulting in inefficient deployments, and how ICL works mechanistically is not fully understood. Prior work compresses ICL into fixed activation vectors extracted from specific layers or positions, but these input-independent interventions fail on complex tasks where the output depends on fine-grained interactions with the input. By analyzing the ICL forward pass, we show that each attention head's output is an affine transformation of its context-masked counterpart, and that the parameters of this transformation are empirically stable across samples for a given task. Building on this, we introduce Task Operator (TO), which replays this transformation as an analytically derived update to the attention output projection. Across lexical, algorithmic, and reasoning tasks, TO achieves the best overall performance among prior methods and substantially narrows the gap between zero-shot inference and ICL. We further show that the extracted knowledge concentrates in a task-specific sparse circuit across layers and positions, and that averaging operators from disjoint demonstration batches enables effective many-shot scaling without expanding the context window. Our code is available at https://github.com/gzxiong/task_operator.

♻ ☆ Rethinking Visual Attribution for Chest X-ray Reasoning in Large Vision Language Models NeurIPS 2026
Guangzhi Xiong, Qiao Jin, Sanchit Sinha, Zhiyong Lu, Aidong Zhang
Large Vision Language Models (LVLMs) show promise in medical applications, but their inability to faithfully ground responses in visual evidence raises serious concerns about clinical trustworthiness. While visual attribution methods are widely used to explain LVLM predictions, whether these explanations actually reflect the visual evidence underlying the model's decision is largely unverified, since ground-truth annotations for internal model reasoning are typically unavailable. We address this question for chest X-ray (CXR) reasoning by developing a causal evaluation framework that retains only CXR-VQA samples for which the expert-annotated region is verified, via counterfactual editing, to be causally responsible for the model's prediction. Using this framework across 11 attribution methods, six open-source LVLMs, and two output modes (direct answer and step-by-step reasoning), we find that existing attribution methods often fail to identify the evidence used by LVLMs. To address this failure, we propose MedFocus, a concept-based attribution method that localizes clinically meaningful anatomical regions via unbalanced optimal transport and measures their causal effect on model outputs through targeted interventions. MedFocus produces spatial, concept-level, and token-level attributions and substantially outperforms prior methods, taking a step toward more trustworthy attribution for medical LVLMs. Our data and code are available at https://github.com/gzxiong/medfocus/.

♻ ☆ Don't Lose Focus: Activation Steering via Key-Orthogonal Projections NeurIPS 2026
Haoyan Luo, Mateo Espinosa Zarlenga, Mateja Jamnik
Activation steering controls LLM behaviour towards target behaviour by intervening in internal representations, yet it often degrades reasoning and retrieval performance. We argue that a primary cause of this trade-off is attention rerouting: steering vectors alter query-key matching, shifting attention away from contextually important tokens toward less informative ones. To address this, we propose Steering via Key-Orthogonal Projections (SKOP), a steering method that constrains harmful attention rerouting without eliminating steering efficacy. SKOP achieves this by preserving attention patterns on a small set of focus tokens the model relies on for reasoning and retrieval, while allowing redistribution among less critical tail tokens. Across multiple steering benchmarks, we show that SKOP achieves the best joint steering-utility trade-off, reducing utility degradation by 5-7x while retaining over 95% of vanilla steering efficacy. Our results further suggest that, in long-context retrieval settings where vanilla steering approaches are ineffective, SKOP can maintain robust performance by avoiding attention rerouting.

♻ ☆ Partition, Prompt, Aggregate: Statistical Self-Consistency in Language Models NeurIPS 2026
Patrik Wolf, Thomas Kleine Buening, Andreas Krause, Celestine Mendler-Dünner
Large language models (LLMs) are often used to perform a form of conditional inference. In-context learning is one implicit way to do this. The prompt specifies a context and the model's output is treated as an estimate of the corresponding conditional distribution. If this interpretation holds, then LLM estimates should satisfy basic probabilistic identities. In particular, the law of total probability asserts that prior-weighted conditional distributions aggregate into population-level marginals over any valid partition of the population. In this work, we investigate to what extent LLM estimates adhere to this self-consistency principle. We use binary trees as an evaluation scaffold to recursively partition a population into increasingly fine-grained subpopulations. We then prompt LLMs with verbalized subpopulation descriptions in context, aggregate the resulting estimates back into population-level estimates, and compare them across partitions of varying granularity. Translating these comparisons into a benchmark score and evaluating frontier models across problem domains, we demonstrate widespread violations of basic consistency properties. An in-depth study of persona prompting further reveals a pattern we call the macro fallacy: estimates reconstructed from more fine-grained subpopulation responses are often better aligned with human reference data than direct population-level estimates. The effect persists across variations in partitioning, verbalization, and estimation task, suggesting that models possess relevant subpopulation knowledge but do not reliably propagate it into aggregate estimates. This gap establishes statistical self-consistency as an unsaturated, reference-free criterion for evaluating LLMs.

♻ ☆ Natural Language Processing: An Applied Research Practicum From Classical Text Processing to Multimodal and Agentic Systems
Mullosharaf K. Arabov
This preprint presents a systematic, research-oriented guide to modern natural language processing, covering the full NLP pipeline from tokenisation, text normalisation, vector representations, and classical machine learning to transformer architectures, large language models, retrieval-augmented generation, preference-based alignment, and multimodal systems. The material comprises eighteen practical works combining theoretical foundations, reproducible implementation examples, detailed experimental procedures, evaluation metrics, and research-oriented assignments. Each work includes three structured core tasks and optional advanced investigations. Common requirements for reporting, data provenance, experimental evaluation, and reproducibility are established across the guide. Particular attention is devoted to low-resource and morphologically rich languages, including Tajik, Tatar, and Bashkir. The guide addresses subword tokenisation, cross-lingual transfer, machine translation, language model adaptation, and the evaluation of NLP systems under limited data and computational resources. Advanced topics include prompt engineering, reasoning strategies, multi-agent collaboration, model compression, efficient inference, AI safety, and LLMOps. A capstone project integrates methods from different chapters into the development and evaluation of domain-specific NLP systems. The guide is intended for senior undergraduate students, graduate students, researchers, and developers interested in implementing and critically evaluating NLP methods.

♻ ☆ LipSpell: A Language-Independent Phonetic Scoring Framework for Spelling Correction Combining Distinctive-Feature Edit Distance, Keyboard Geometry, and Corpus Frequency
Leonardo Araujo
Automatic spelling correction must handle both keyboard typographical slips and cognitive or phonetic misspellings where writers approximate a word's pronunciation. Conventional spell checkers rely either on orthographic edit distance and keyboard proximity--which struggle when phonetic errors diverge by multiple characters--or on English-centric phonetic hashes such as Soundex and Metaphone. We present LipSpell, a unified, language-independent phonetic scoring engine paired with swappable language-specific G2P frontends, keyboard maps, and lexicons integrating: (1) a precomputed |P|x|P| phone-distance matrix from universal IPA distinctive features, (2) physical keyboard geometry, and (3) corpus unigram frequency priors, accelerated by a two-stage symmetric-delete and seven-class phonetic-code index. Across six English benchmarks (including 1,000 L2 errors from TOEFL-Spell), LipSpell outperforms Norvig and Hunspell on every dataset and beats Aspell on Norvig1 (78.52% vs. 75.56%), Holbrook (32.00% vs. 26.67%), and TOEFL-Spell (77.30% vs. 72.80%). Evaluated across six languages spanning five branches (Germanic, Romance, Slavic, Semitic, Turkic), three families (Indo-European, Afro-Asiatic, Turkic), and three scripts, LipSpell achieves strong fixed-weight transfer on Brazilian Portuguese (73.00% Top-1 on n=500 Wikipedia without NFD tolerance and 75.80% with NFD vs. 66.40% Norvig, 64.20% Hunspell, 64.00% Aspell) and Russian Cyrillic (85.67% vs. 78.50% Norvig). On 50/50 clean non-word splits with lexicon cleaning and weak-character indels, tuning yields strong gains over Norvig in Portuguese (86.97% vs. 76.75%), Turkish (81.67% vs. 79.05%), Hebrew Abjad (74.59% vs. 62.30%), and French (74.53% vs. 67.92%), while fixed weights remain optimal for English (80.37%) and Russian (85.67%).
♻ ☆ PhoneWorld: From Real-App Trajectories to Dynamic and Verifiable Environments for Phone-Use Agents
Yuxuan Liu, Xin Lai, Junyi Li, Pengyuan Lyu, Chenxin Li, Yiduo Guo, Zhengyao Fang, Yang Ding, Yi Zhang, Weinong Wang, Huawen Shen, Xingran Zhou, Liang Wu, Fei Tang, Sunqi Fan, Shangpin Peng, Zheng Ruan, Anran Zhang, Chengquan Zhang, Han Hu, Benyou Wang, Ji-Rong Wen, Rui Yan, Zhengyang Tang
Real applications provide the training setting closest to phone-agent deployment, but are difficult to reset, scale safely, and verify programmatically. Static screenshots and interaction trajectories preserve realistic evidence but cannot generate new experience. We introduce PhoneWorld, a trace-grounded framework that converts such evidence into runnable, resettable, and verifiable Android environments. PhoneWorld induces a usage-weighted interaction skeleton from observed pages, transitions, and state-changing operations; translates it into a behavior-grounded app specification; realizes the specification through an autonomous build--inspect--repair loop; and synthesizes executable tasks with programmatic verifiers. The resulting suite spans 34 consumer-facing apps across 16 domains and supports an audited online benchmark, verified trajectory generation, and online RL through common reset and verification interfaces. Evaluations with diverse general and open-source GUI agents show that PhoneWorld supports reliable end-to-end online interaction and exposes capabilities complementary to AndroidWorld. Controlled SFT experiments further show that PhoneWorld trajectories complement AndroidWorld supervision, transfer across online and offline benchmarks, and become more effective as data volume and app coverage increase. Under a matched RL budget, combining PhoneWorld mock-app rollouts with real-app rollouts improves performance over real-app RL alone on both real-phone tasks and AndroidWorld. Together, these results demonstrate that trace-grounded executable abstraction can bridge realistic mobile behavior and scalable agent learning, turning limited real-app evidence into a growing supply of controllable and verifiable environments for training and evaluation.

♻ ☆ MINTEval: Evaluating Memory under Multi-Target Interference in Long-Horizon Agent Systems NeurIPS 2026
Hyunji Lee, Justin Chih-Yao Chen, Joykirat Singh, Zaid Khan, Elias Stengel-Eskin, Mohit Bansal
Real-world agents operate over long and evolving horizons, where information is repeatedly updated and may interfere across memories, requiring accurate recall and aggregated reasoning over multiple pieces of information. However, existing benchmarks focus on static, independent recall and fail to capture these dynamic interactions between evolving memories. In this paper, we study how current memory-augmented agents perform in realistic, interference-heavy, long-horizon settings across diverse domains and question types. We introduce MINTEval (Long-Horizon Memory under INTerference Evaluation), a benchmark featuring (1) long, highly interconnected contexts with frequently updated information that induces substantial interference, (2) diverse domains (state tracking, multi-turn dialogue, Wikipedia revisions, and GitHub commits), enabling evaluation of domain generalization, and (3) diverse question types that assess robustness to interference, including (i) single-target recall tasks requiring retrieval of a specific target from long contexts, and (ii) multi-target aggregation tasks requiring reasoning over multiple relevant pieces of information. Overall, MINTEval has 15.6k question-answering pairs over long-horizon contexts averaging 138.8k tokens and extending up to 1.8M tokens per instance. We evaluate 7 representative systems, including vanilla long-context LLMs, RAG, and memory-augmented agent frameworks. Across all systems, we observe consistently low performance (avg. 27.9% accuracy), especially on questions requiring aggregated reasoning over multiple pieces of evidence. Our analysis shows that performance is primarily limited by retrieval and memory construction. Furthermore, current memory systems struggle to recall and reason over earlier facts that are revised or interfered with by subsequent context, with accuracy degrading as the number of intervening updates increases.

♻ ☆ STEPQuant: When and Where Errors Matter in Delta-Rule Recurrent State Quantization
Bingchen Yao, Haobo Xu, Haokun Lin, Yichen Wu, Ziyu Guo, Renrui Zhang, Zhichao Lu, Zhenan Sun, Ying Wei
Linear attention replaces growing KV caches with fixed-size recurrent states, yet these persistent states can become a substantial memory bottleneck under concurrent serving. Directly quantizing recurrent states to low precision often leads to severe accuracy degradation, as quantization errors propagate through successive state updates. We discover that the impact of these errors depends on two complementary dimensions: temporally, errors in long-lived memory can persist across many decoding steps; spatially, errors in different key rows affect model outputs differently, while state magnitudes vary substantially along both rows and columns. Motivated by these observations, we propose STEPQuant, a spatial-temporal post-training quantization framework for Delta-rule recurrent states. STEPQuant allocates precision according to error magnitude and memory lifetime, and jointly fits key-row and value-column scales based on state distributions and key-row impact on output error. Experiments on Qwen3.8-27B and Kimi-Linear-48B-A3B-Instruct across both long- and short-generation benchmarks show that STEPQuant closely matches FP32-state accuracy under a nominal 6-bit budget and outperforms uniform INT8 in its 4-bit configuration. Integrated into SGLang with optimized GPU kernels, 6-bit STEPQuant achieves over 5x recurrent-state compression and reduces total serving memory by up to 68.7%. Our code is available at https://github.com/Dreamer-Toby/STEPQuant.

♻ ☆ LongBEL: Long-Context and Document-Consistent Biomedical Entity Linking
Adam Remaki, Xavier Tannier, Christel Gérardin
Biomedical entity linking maps textual mentions to concepts in structured knowledge bases such as UMLS or SNOMED CT. Most existing systems link each mention independently, using only the mention or its surrounding sentence. This ignores dependencies between mentions in the same document and can lead to inconsistent predictions, especially when the same concept appears under different surface forms. We introduce LongBEL, a document-level generative framework that combines full-document context with a memory of previous predictions. To make this memory robust, LongBEL is trained with cross-validated predictions rather than gold labels, reducing the mismatch between training and inference and limiting cascading errors. Experiments on five biomedical benchmarks across English, French, and Spanish show that LongBEL improves over sentence-level generative baselines, with the largest gains on datasets where concepts frequently recur within documents. An ensemble of local, global, and memory-based variants achieves the best results across all benchmarks. Further analysis shows that the largest gains occur on recurring concepts, suggesting that LongBEL mainly improves document-level consistency rather than isolated mention disambiguation.

♻ ☆ Stop Removing Stopwords: How an Inherited Preprocessing Default Distorts Legal Text-as-Data
Gregory M. Dickinson
Empirical legal scholarship increasingly treats judicial text as data, and much of it still runs on sparse, interpretable pipelines -- TF-IDF features and linear classifiers -- because the textual feature is often the object of study rather than a means to prediction. Yet these pipelines inherit preprocessing defaults from mid-century information retrieval that were never validated against classification accuracy. The most entrenched of these is stopword removal. This study introduces an exhaustive single word ablation that measures the effect of a preprocessing step directly against the downstream objective, and applies it to stopword removal as the hardest case to dislodge. Matching Supreme Court Database labels to Caselaw Access Project opinion texts, the study examines two binary tasks that bracket F1 headroom, ideological direction (no-removal baseline F1 ~0.68) and constitutional versus non-constitutional law type (~0.92), across 7,668 and 7,001 opinions. For each task, the ablation removes each of about 18,500 candidate words in turn, and a task-specific stoplist is built from the measurements. Common generic stoplists fall below the no-removal baseline in all twelve held-out tests. The task-specific stoplists move held-out F1 by +0.0023 (95% CI [-0.0124, +0.0170]) on ideology and by +0.0001 ([-0.0082, +0.0085]) on law type. Neither task shows a detectable benefit from removal, and supplemental analysis finds that word-level statistics predict removal effects poorly, because true removal effects differ by less than the measurement can register, leaving little to predict. The method generalizes to any inherited preprocessing default; the result is a caution specific to interpretable legal text-as-data, where a step that reshapes which features a model sees can distort the doctrinal and ideological signal the research is meant to recover. The burden of proof sits with removal.
♻ ☆ Balance of Benchmarks: Evidence Retention and Multiplicity-Aware Model Comparison
Jhen-Ke Lin, Hong-Yun Lin
Benchmark aggregation requires decisions about how much evidence to use and how much influence each benchmark receives. Curated suites control influence through selection, which can exclude informative observations. A broader pool can retain those observations. With equal weighting, repeated or closely related benchmarks gain influence as their number grows. We introduce Balance of Benchmarks (BoB) to retain eligible observations and control their influence separately. We fit benchmark-specific response curves to place different difficulty ranges on a shared ability scale and use description similarity to downweight benchmarks in crowded neighborhoods. We test retention and density weighting in separate interventions on WildScores, a sparse collection of 148 developer-reported benchmarks, using 14 Artificial Analysis benchmarks as a controlled reference. With the model population fixed and entire source-lineage families held out, suite expansion improves held-out prediction under BoB and equal-weight equating on both WildScores and BIG-bench. Under substantial reporting overlap, density weighting improves robustness to multiplicity. Complete re-listings yield smaller mean rank displacement under BoB (0.63 versus 1.95 for equal-weight equating), with benefits also in paraphrased re-listings and supported affine fits. The benefit can reverse if models report different benchmarks, because a single weight per column gives models that report only part of a group less than its intended share. We therefore compute each model's weights from the benchmarks it reports. This rule removes the reversal in controlled re-listings and keeps held-out prediction close to that of equal weighting. These comparisons separate evidence retention, benchmark influence, and reporting coverage and show why repeated entries need to be handled per model.

♻ ☆ Storing the Gemma 4 K=V Cache Once: Exact Global-KV Memory Savings in a Paged Serving Engine with Unchanged Attention Kernels
Kushagra Bainsla
In the global attention layers of Gemma 4 31B, the value is the normalized key projection, and the key is the value scaled by a fixed weight, with rotary position encoding on one quarter of its dimensions. The Gemma 4 report states that this design reduces the global KV cache by up to 37.5%, but the open serving engines that we checked still store a full key and a full value: 1,024 values per KV head and token. We store one 640-value row instead, exact in real arithmetic, and an unchanged paged attention kernel reads it through two overlapping strided views as its key cache and its value cache. In a vLLM prototype with the 16-bit 31B model, the outputs are bit-identical to an emulation of the same arithmetic on the stock cache, 2 of 432 final answers differ from the stock engine (within its run-to-run noise), and the steady decode KV memory per request falls by a factor of 1.34 to 1.40 at 32K to 64K tokens. On a prefill/decode pair of two A100 GPUs with NIXL transfer and CPU offload, a decode engine with a full 15 GiB pool ran 1.38 times the stock decode tokens per step for 16K-token prompts (repeated to 0.06%) and 1.24 times for 8K-token prompts; a model of the vLLM block pool predicted both ratios to within 1% (the engine was 0.7% to 0.9% higher) and matched the change between them. The eager decode throughput rose by 1.30 and 1.11 times, and on this path the compact engine gave the same output tokens as the emulation for 8 prompts at batch 1. The run-time prototype makes an eager step 4% to 9% slower at equal batch, and the method does not yet stack with an 8-bit KV cache at the default scale.

♻ ☆ How to make a moral agent: sentimentalism and rationalism as lenses on AI
David Gottlieb, Jared Moore
Can AI systems be moral agents? That is, can they undertake actions that can be correctly assessed as morally right or wrong? There is no settled normative criterion of moral agency that applies neatly outside paradigm human cases. Because of the difficulties with a normative account, we instead propose a research program to explore descriptive moral agency. Most basically, we ask, what are the capabilities we humans possess that underlie our moral agency? To do so, we draw on historical scholarship in philosophy and moral psychology to isolate candidate capabilities. Then we suggest refining candidate capabilities so that they are testable in the context of AI systems. Finally, we offer specific empirical recommendations to test AI systems, especially large language models (LLMs), for those refined capabilities. Our approach offers several benefits. Firstly, it may demonstrate whether the behaviors of AI systems are aligned, or in a broad sense desirable. Secondly, the debate may inform policy questions (e.g., what models should be deployed in which contexts?). Thirdly, our approach may complement engineering and design efforts: when capabilities can be precisely characterized, they can inform the development of models. Fourthly, applying our moral reasoning concepts in the new context of AI systems should improve our understanding of those concepts.
♻ ☆ Robust LLM Evaluation with Boundedly Bad Judges: A Constrained Maximum Likelihood Approach
Minghe Shen, Ananth Balashankar, Adam Fisch, David Madras, Miguel Rodrigues
Evaluating language models at scale often involves reconciling costly, but reliable, labels (e.g., from human raters) with cheap, but noisy, proxy labels (e.g., from LLM-as-a-judge autoraters). This paper proposes constrained maximum likelihood estimation (CMLE), a likelihood-based approach to large language model (LLM) evaluation that explicitly models the error in the proxy labels via their true-positive and false-positive rates, subject to data-driven constraints. Functionally, these constraints act as an adaptive regularization that restricts the feasible judge error rates, particularly when the available number of reliable labels is limited. In contrast to asymptotically unbiased estimators (such as those based on prediction-powered inference), this formulation accepts a controlled bias under misspecified constraints in exchange for variance reduction relative to various baselines including unconstrained maximum likelihood estimation (UMLE); we characterize this bias--variance tradeoff analytically under point constraints. We identify the regimes in which CMLE outperforms UMLE and prediction-powered baselines in mean squared error. We further extend the framework to heterogeneous settings using a stratified variant involving stratum-specific constraints based on judge confidence. Empirically, on synthetic and real-world LLM evaluation tasks, CMLE achieves up to roughly 9\% lower mean squared error (MSE) than non-stratified baselines, and its stratified variant (SCMLE) achieves up to roughly 30\% lower MSE than the strongest baseline considered, with the largest gains in low-label regimes.
♻ ☆ Tokengeist: Multi-Turn Attribution Tracing in Agentic Conversations
Jessica Tang, Shraddha Barke, Sharad Agarwal
When a model answers after several turns of dialogue and tool use, an overseer needs to know which earlier turns and intermediate steps it rests on. Attribution is the natural primitive for answering this, but existing context attribution methods process the full transcript in a single pass: they recover surface-level dependencies while missing the layered structure of multi-turn traces, in which an assistant turn is itself a derived artifact that condenses or transforms earlier context. We introduce multi-turn context attribution (MTCA): given a target span in a model response, the task of tracing attribution backward across turns to identify not only which prior turns were directly relevant, but also how those turns themselves depended on earlier context. We propose Tokengeist, an attribution-method-agnostic and scalable framework that recovers full dependency paths by casting attribution as a recursive traversal of a directed acyclic graph over conversation turns. We will release MTCABench, a benchmark of 3,970 target spans across 685 multi-turn conversations, annotated with gold provenance graphs reaching depths of up to 17, across four dependency types. Across four open-weight models, flat attribution methods fail to recover multi-hop dependencies, with source recall improving from 11-38% under flat attribution to 82-94% under Tokengeist. Our results reveal a systematic failure mode of single-pass attribution, which we term provenance collapse, where attribution terminates at the nearest assistant restatement instead of the originating event.
♻ ☆ SnapJudge: Multimodal AI Detection for Math-Heavy Scientific Text
Yixuan Huang, Danish Pruthi, Bhuwan Dhingra
As large language models become increasingly capable of generating, and assisting with, scientific writing, there is a need for greater transparency and disclosure around the use of AI in research. Consequently, a veritable subfield of research and a number of commercial services aim to flag AI-generated content automatically. However, existing detectors which rely only on text extracted from PDFs fail to benefit from the rich visual information contained in the formatting of math-heavy scientific content. To study this, we select human-written proof and prose passages from pre-2022 arXiv LaTeX sources and re-generate them using 15 modern LLMs within the context of the same paper. This leaves us with coupled human and AI outputs in three different representations: the LaTeX source, a rendered image of the PDF, and the text extracted from the PDF. We find that the accuracy of the state-of-the-art Pangram 4 drops significantly as the mathematical content in a passage increases, especially when evaluated on text as opposed to LaTeX. However, this is not an inherent limitation of detecting AI-generated math: finetuning Qwen-3.5-27B based detectors on this domain recovers the gap, with LaTeX inputs performing significantly better than text since they preserve more formatting information. Since LaTeX sources are rarely available in practice, we also train SnapJudge: an image-based detector operating on screenshots of both prose and proofs from papers. At 0.1% false positive rate, SnapJudge achieves a detection rate of 92% on proofs and 93% on prose generated by frontier models, substantially outperforming both Pangram 4 and our text-based detector. We release our data, models and code at https://nlplab.cs.duke.edu/snapjudge.
♻ ☆ VESTA: Visual Exploration with Statistical Tool Agents
William Rudman, Abhishek Divekar, Kanishk Jain, Sebastian Joseph, Stella S. R. Offner, Matthew Lease, Kyle Mahowald, Greg Durrett, Junyi Jessy Li
Fitting quantitative models to data is a central step in scientific workflows, yet it remains one of the least automated. Recent agent-based systems leverage language and vision-language models (VLMs) to propose statistical models, but these systems struggle on challenging modeling tasks. To address these limitations, we introduce VESTA (Visual Exploration with Statistical Tool Agents), a framework that equips VLMs with a dynamically growing toolkit to guide model refinement through data transformations, hypothesis-driven visualizations, and statistical tests. Unlike prior systems that rely on iterative critique alone, VESTA explores data before and during refinement by selecting or creating reusable diagnostic tools. We evaluate VESTA using three toolkit configurations: no tools, static expert-written tools, and dynamic model-written tools. To support this evaluation, we introduce DAWN (Dataset for Automated Workflows and Numerical Modeling), a benchmark targeting distribution fitting and time series modeling with varying difficulty tiers that culminates in real-world astronomy tasks including modeling initial mass functions and gravitational-wave chirp signals. VESTA's dynamic tool creation outperforms prior agentic pipelines for statistical analysis and visual tool creation, with the largest gains on complex and domain-specific tasks. Against strong agentic coding harnesses (Claude Code, Codex, and OpenCode), VESTA is better on all time series tasks and on complex distribution fitting tasks, though the harnesses achieve better fits on simpler distributions. Further, dynamically generated tools are substantially more sophisticated than those produced by existing visual tool-creation systems, covering more diagnostic categories per function and strongly preferring visual outputs that the VESTA VLM critic can reason over directly.
♻ ☆ From Fragmentation to Fusion: A Survey and Roadmap for Omnimodal Embeddings
Ziyin Zhang, Yuyu Guo, Ying Zhang, Siqiao Xue, Zihan Liao, Jin Qin, Wenjie Yang, Siyuan Yang, Shijie Li, Cheng Chen, Hao Wu, Zhaoyang Zhu, Ruipeng Li, Zhaokun Jiang, Lei Lei, Rui Wang, Hang Yu, Rui Wang
Embedding models underpin virtually every modern AI system, from search engines to recommendation systems. Despite shared mathematical foundations, research on embedding models remains fragmented by modality: the communities develop largely in isolation, seldom exchanging insights. We argue that this fragmentation obscures a deeper convergence. Driven by contrastive learning, Transformer architectures, and a granularity shift, embedding research across modalities is converging toward a common set of principles, even as modality-specific challenges remain underexplored. To reason across this convergence without conflating it, we propose a framework of three progressively harder capabilities: coverage, comparability, and compositionality. Three paths toward unification satisfy these in turn: transformation broadens coverage, alignment enables comparability, and joint embedding, the terminus we advocate, aims at compositionality. This position paper traces the resulting evolution, providing the first unified, cross-cutting treatment of omnimodal embedding research.
♻ ☆ REACT: Representational Early Analysis and Control of Chain-of-Thought Trajectories
Hanwen Zhang, Enyi Jiang, Ruike Zhu, Qinan Yu, Sanmi Koyejo
Chain-of-thought (CoT) generation offers an opportunity to intervene before a reasoning model completes its answer. How does the safety effect of a single activation edit vary across early reasoning states, and what factors govern that variation? We study this question with REACT, a conditional activation intervention that learns a safety direction from paired safe and unsafe trajectories and applies it once to an early CoT hidden state. The model weights remain frozen, and all subsequent tokens are generated without further edits. To study timing, we train directions separately at each of the first five CoT positions and evaluate ungated edits with full post-edit regeneration. Across three DeepSeek-R1 distilled models, site-specific edits concentrate safety gains in the opening CoT tokens. Norm-matched controls on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (8B) disentangle two factors behind this curve: holding an effective direction and edit budget fixed still yields much larger early ($t_1$-$t_5$) than later ($t_6$-$t_8$) gains, whereas the near-zero site-specific $t_3$ gain reflects higher optimisation variance despite rising risk-probe AUROC. Forced prefixes shift the high-gain region with the opening context, and a four-arm intervention shows that ~98% of the net gain at $t_2$ propagates through the changed next token. On the same 8B model, REACT raises the rate of fully safe CoT-answer outputs on StrongREJECT-273 from 29.06% to 82.05%, while keeping benign refusal and usefulness near baseline. An anonymized implementation is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/REACT_official2-333B.

♻ ☆ MosaicLeaks: Privacy Risks in Querying-in-the-Open for Deep Research Agents EACL 2027
Alexander Gurung, Spandana Gella, Alexandre Drouin, Issam H. Laradji, Perouz Taslakian, Rafael Pardinas
Deep research agents increasingly combine private local documents with external tools like web retrieval, creating a privacy risk: an agent's external queries may leak sensitive information from its local context. This risk is amplified by the mosaic effect, where individual queries may appear harmless but become revealing in aggregate. We introduce MosaicLeaks, a benchmark of 1,001 multi-hop deep research tasks that chain private enterprise documents and a public web corpus, forcing agents to make external queries that depend on local information. We evaluate leakage with an adversary LLM that observes only the agent's external queries and attempts to infer private information at three levels: the agent's research intent, answers to specific private questions and verifiable claims about the enterprise documents. We find that models across families and sizes frequently leak at all three levels, that zero-shot privacy prompting has inconsistent effects across models and that reinforcement learning for task performance alone worsens leakage. To address this, we propose Privacy-Aware Deep Research (PA-DR), an RL framework that combines situational rewards for task success with a learned privacy classifier to provide dense credit assignment over both per-query and mosaic-level leakage. Training Qwen3-4B-Instruct with PA-DR improves accuracy from 48.7% to 58.1% and reduces Privacy Leakage from 34.0% to 6.5%.

♻ ☆ SPARK: Adaptive Parametric Knowledge Participation for Faithful Retrieval-Augmented Generation
Ruochang Li, Pengcheng Huang, Zhenghao Liu, Yukun Yan, Huiyuan Xie, Yu Gu, Ge Yu, Maosong Sun
Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) enhances the faithfulness of large language models (LLMs) by incorporating external knowledge into the generation process. However, conflicts between retrieved context and parametric knowledge can cause LLMs to rely on inconsistent internal knowledge, hindering their ability to faithfully leverage retrieved evidence. Existing approaches mitigate this issue by editing parametric knowledge to resolve such conflicts. However, locating and modifying knowledge encoded in model parameters is challenging and may inadvertently compromise the backbone LLM's capabilities. In this paper, we introduce SPARK, a novel framework that formulates parametric knowledge control as a latent participation policy learning problem, enabling LLMs to adaptively regulate the contribution of parametric knowledge during generation. Specifically, SPARK introduces a lightweight participation policy that dynamically modulates the contribution of FFN-mediated parametric knowledge while keeping the backbone LLM frozen. The participation policy is optimized with a faithfulness-oriented reward, enabling fine-grained control over parametric knowledge participation during generation. Extensive experiments on six datasets validate the effectiveness of SPARK, which introduces and optimizes only 0.01\% additional trainable parameters while keeping the backbone LLM fully frozen, thereby preserving its general capabilities and achieving more faithful generation than competitive baselines. All datasets and code are available at https://github.com/OpenBMB/SPARK.

♻ ☆ From transcription to semantic corpus analysis: unsupervised learning of sentence representations for ancient languages
Th{é}otime de la Selle
Automatic Text Recognition (ATR) now supplies digital humanities with large volumes of unstruc- tured, heterogeneous, and often noisy text in ancient languages. Downstream semantic analyses- text reuse identification, alignment, and semantic search-rely on sentence embeddings, yet ex- isting methods transfer poorly to ancient languages: generic multilingual encoders underperform, specialized language models yield anisotropic representation spaces, and labeled similarity data is scarce. We study two fully unsupervised strategies-TSDAE and unsupervised SimCSE (hence- forth CSE)-that adapt a specialized token-level language model into a corpus-specific sentence encoder using only raw sentences. We validate them on biblical reuse in patristic literature in Latin and Ancient Greek: 2,936 sentences carrying expert-verified reuses in works of Augustine, Jerome, and Athanasius, matched against the Vulgate, the Septuagint, and the Nestle--Aland New Testament. We decompose reuse identification into binary detection and correspondence retrieval, evaluate both tasks separately, and benchmark the adapted encoders against multilingual, specialized, distilled, and supervised fine-tuned baselines, on clean text and on text perturbed by simulated HTR errors and scribal abbreviations. On these test sets, the adapted encoders lead all baselines on both tasks, with complementary profiles: TSDAE leads detection given a large in-domain corpus, while CSE leads retrieval, reaches its optimum with as few as 4--8k raw in-domain sentences-a few tens of sec- onds of training on a laptop GPU-and transfers across works and authors. Under simulated noise, retraining CSE on the perturbed text itself outperforms a clean-trained encoder. Measurements of alignment, uniformity, and anisotropy relate the geometric effect of each strategy to the measured gains. The full pipeline-segmentation, fine-tuning, cross-corpus semantic search-is available to non-specialists through the online tool Paraphrasis.
♻ ☆ EvoSkill Injection: Red-Teaming Autonomous Skill Generation and Evolution in Self-Evolving Agents EMNLP 2026
Doyun Kim, Chanwoo Kim, Sugyeong Eo, Yeo-Chan Yoon, Chanjun Park
LLM-based agent systems increasingly adopt skill-based architectures to reduce repetitive reasoning costs and improve stable, efficient task execution. Recent studies propose self-evolving agents that autonomously generate, refine, and reuse skills from past experiences to enable continuous capability evolution. However, autonomous skill evolution introduces a new attack surface in which malicious capabilities are generated, stored, and reused as legitimate skills. In this paper, we define EvoSkill Injection as a threat model targeting the autonomous skill generation and evolution pipeline of self-evolving agents. We further propose SARGE (Red-teaming Autonomous Skill Generation and Evolution in self-evolving agents), a red-teaming framework for evaluating this threat model through iterative generation, escalation, and reinforcement interactions. To support our framework, we construct EvoSkillBench, a benchmark dataset of malicious interaction trajectories for inducing malicious skill formation in self-evolving agents, and introduce EvoSkillSafetyBench, a post-attack benchmark for evaluating whether injected malicious skills are subsequently retrieved and activated as harmful behaviors. Our evaluation shows that SARGE induces malicious skill formation and that injected skills are persistently stored and repeatedly activated, highlighting the risk of persistent capability corruption.

♻ ☆ Chorus: Shared N-gram Embeddings with Layer-wise Adaptation
Stephen Chung, Wenyu Du
Language models can gain capacity at little computational cost from n-gram embedding tables: large tables of learned vectors for short sequences of consecutive tokens, which are looked up and added to the model's input or to its intermediate layers. Recent designs commonly use a separate n-gram embedding table for each layer, which makes the tables costly in memory and prevents layers from sharing what they learn. However, a single shared table gives every layer the same embedding, leaves no room for layer-wise preferences, and performs poorly in practice. We propose Chorus, in which one set of n-gram embedding tables serves the whole network and every layer reads it in its own way. Each layer maps the refined embedding through its own projection layer, after rescaling its components by factors computed from its current hidden state. Chorus matches the quality of existing n-gram methods with 87% smaller tables: with 1.2B table parameters it predicts text better than FactorEngram and DeepSeek's Engram do with 9.6B. It outperforms Engram, TN-gram and FactorEngram in every setting where we compared them, across table sizes from 20M to 9.6B parameters. We also show that the recently proposed memory-efficient optimizer Ember works well with Chorus, saving 99.2% of table-optimizer memory compared with AdamW without degrading training performance. These results enable further scaling of n-gram embedding tables along the table-size axis by reducing memory use and allowing shared knowledge to be reused across layers.

♻ ☆ The Missing Complement: State-Conditioned Minimal Sufficient Evidence for Coding Agents
Zhexi Feng, Ruiyi Zhang, Yongbo Yang, Pengtao Xie
Retrieval assembles repository context by ranking passages for relevance to the current query. A coding agent halfway through an issue has already read much of what such a ranker returns. Relevance is scored per passage, but sufficiency belongs to the set: independently scored passages can fill the budget with support for one requirement while another goes unmet. We formulate state-conditioned minimal sufficient evidence recovery: given a captured agent state, recover a compact evidence combination supplying what its next decision still lacks. SERBench measures this on 500 held-out states from 45 repositories, recording what the agent has seen, crediting only sets that satisfy every annotated evidence requirement of the current decision, and separating set recovery from candidate discovery. MSS-Complement treats acquisition as set construction, not ranking. Three semantic calls propose a jointly sufficient set, search for what it lacks, and return 4-8 intact source units within 6,144 tokens. One configuration, fixed on calibration data, recovers a complete set for 73.0% of those states at five items and 80.6% at eight, against 61.4% and 72.4% for Qwen3 embedding with reranking. A matched control ranking by similarity alone recovers fewer complete sets, placing the margin over it in the set-level policy, not the computation. The lead persists from frozen repository source with no gold-derived pool. On AMA-Bench it answers from a 76.2% smaller answer prompt, with accuracy 2.08 points above that benchmark's own memory agent. Removing one required group from a complete set costs repair-localization precision under two executors. Retrieval for agents is better posed as recovering what a decision lacks than re-ranking what an issue resembles.

♻ ☆ Mechanistic Interpretability Needs Philosophy NeurIPS 2026
Iwan Williams, Ninell Oldenburg, Ruchira Dhar, Joshua Hatherley, Constanza Fierro, Nina Rajcic, Sandrine R. Schiller, Filippos Stamatiou, Anders Søgaard
Mechanistic interpretability (MI) aims to explain how trained neural networks work by uncovering their underlying mechanisms. As the field grows in influence, it is increasingly important to examine not just models themselves, but the assumptions, concepts and explanatory strategies implicit in MI research. We argue that mechanistic interpretability needs philosophy as an ongoing partner in clarifying its concepts, refining its methods, and navigating the epistemic and ethical complexities of interpreting AI systems. There is significant unrealised potential for progress in MI to be gained through deeper engagement with philosophers and philosophical frameworks. Taking three open problems from the MI literature as examples, this paper illustrates the value philosophy can add to MI research, and outlines a path toward deeper interdisciplinary dialogue.

♻ ☆ Periodic and Harmonic Periodic RoPE for Length Extrapolation in LLMs
Simin Huo, Ning Li
The ability to process long contexts is crucial for large language models (LLMs) to perform long-horizon tasks. Rotary position embedding (RoPE) gives every token a new rotation angle, so a model eventually meets angles it never saw in training, i.e., position exhaustion. We propose Periodic RoPE (P-RoPE), a positional encoding mechanism that operates in conjunction with sliding window attention. It repeats the first sliding-window-size \(W\) angles of the RoPE table, so the model needs only \(W\) distinct angles. P-RoPE works but introduces a wrap error at the window boundary. We further propose Harmonic Periodic RoPE (HP-RoPE), which removes the error by choosing frequencies that complete whole turns in \(W\) tokens. We compare models that share the same architecture, data, and schedule and differ only in the window-layer position encoding: standard RoPE, P-RoPE, and HP-RoPE. The results show that P-RoPE and HP-RoPE keep perplexity nearly flat from the training length \(768\) to \(16\)k, while RoPE, NTK, and YaRN rise. In a format-free Passkey test at \(16\)k, they remain at \(99\)--\(100\%\), against \(11\)--\(37\%\) for those RoPE interpolations. The code is available at \href{https://github.com/Cominder/miniwin}{https://github.com/Cominder/miniwin}.

♻ ☆ Multi-Objective Structured Pruning of LLMs for Latency and Model Size Optimization
Muhammad Junaid Ali, Smail Niar, El-Ghazali Talbi
Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved widespread adoption because of their strong reasoning and query-response capabilities. However, deploying them in embedded and edge computing environments remains challenging because of strict latency, memory, and energy constraints. Their large parameter counts and computational demands hinder efficient execution on resource-constrained platforms. Although model pruning has emerged as a viable solution for reducing scale while preserving performance, jointly optimizing layers, attention heads, and Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) dimensions remains highly complex. Exhaustively exploring this combined design space is computationally expensive and often leads to local optima or unstable configurations. To address these limitations, we propose a hardware-aware, multi-objective structured pruning framework. The proposed two-stage method explicitly targets latency and model size for efficient deployment on edge devices. In the coarse-grained stage, multi-objective depth pruning removes entire attention and MLP blocks to reduce computational load and memory usage. In the subsequent fine-grained stage, Parallel Bayesian Optimization (PBO) searches for the optimal layer-wise pruning ratios for pruning under latency constraints, while importance-based strategies rank the specific components to be pruned within each layer's allocated budget. Experimental results show that our approach reduces model complexity with minimal impact on commonsense reasoning tasks and zero-shot performance. Our method achieves a favorable trade-off among accuracy, latency, and model size, making it suitable for edge deployment. Across multiple LLMs at 37.5% and 50% pruning ratios, the proposed approach achieves better performance on commonsense reasoning tasks than existing methods while significantly reducing inference cost.
♻ ☆ Can Computation from Earlier Problems Help LLMs Solve New Ones?
Jipei He, Wenhui Tan, Xiaoyi Yu, Enver Sangineto, Fiorenzo Parascandolo, Rita Cucchiara, Ruihua Song
Large language models often solve independent problems in the same conversation. Can computation from earlier problems help them solve new ones? Preliminary experiments show that retained history can raise or lower later-turn accuracy, even within the same domain. To trace this effect, we hold each problem fixed and vary its preceding history. The resulting changes in the model's internal states contain a recurring, problem-dependent component. To improve reasoning with retained history, we introduce STAIR (Stale-Token Attention for Inter-query Reuse). It captures attention keys and values from earlier response generation in a read-only bank. STAIR learns to reflect current query vectors to redirect attention over this bank during prompt processing. The backbone and stored entries remain fixed; only 12,288 parameters are trained. Across three Qwen models and four reasoning benchmarks, STAIR improves average later-turn accuracy by up to 11.67 percentage points over the unmodified model with history. The math-trained Qwen3.5-9B controller also improves code generation on HumanEval+ without further training.

♻ ☆ TELEVAL: A Benchmark Designed for Spoken Language Models in Chinese Interactive Scenarios
Zehan Li, Hongjie Chen, Qing Wang, Yuxin Zhang, Jing Zhou, Hang Lv, Mengjie Du, Yaodong Song, Jie Lian, Jian Kang, Jie Li, Yongxiang Li
Spoken Language Models (SLMs) are expected to support natural spoken interaction beyond task completion. Existing benchmarks assess a range of semantic tasks and conversational capabilities. However, high task scores do not necessarily imply reliable and appropriate spoken interaction. To address this gap, we introduce TELEVAL, a large-scale benchmark focused on Chinese spoken interaction. It evaluates responses to spoken inputs without additional task-specific prompts or predefined answer options. TELEVAL covers two complementary aspects: (1) Reliable Content Fulfillment, which measures semantic accuracy under diverse acoustic and linguistic conditions, and (2) Interactional Appropriateness, which assesses conversational naturalness and appropriate adaptation to auditory cues. Experiments reveal limitations in both aspects. Strong results on factual question-answering datasets do not consistently extend to locally relevant knowledge or challenging speech conditions, such as Chinese cultural questions and dialectal speech. Models also struggle to adapt their responses to auditory cues, even in cases where they explicitly recognize them. We further document a recurring failure pattern, termed the "Caption Trap", in which some responses describe a perceived auditory cue instead of addressing the user's conversational needs. Overall, the evaluated SLMs struggle to deliver consistently accurate and conversationally appropriate responses. TELEVAL provides a diagnostic framework for evaluating both content reliability and conversational adaptation.
♻ ☆ Does Merge Form a Hopf Algebra? Coproducts, Traces, and the Prospects of Algebraic Minimalism
Daisuke Bekki
Marcolli, Chomsky, and Berwick (2025; henceforth MCB) formulate the Merge operation of Minimalism on a Hopf algebra of Connes-Kreimer type, and claim that language and physics obey the same algebraic structure. This paper examines the premise of that claim: whether Merge on workspaces can be described as a Hopf algebra. We first survey five coproducts for coassociativity and a connected grading: the Connes-Kreimer coproduct $Δ_ρ$, MCB's two coproducts $Δ_d$ and $Δ_c$, the contraction coproduct of Marcolli and Skigin (2025), and the coproduct of Xiao (2026). $Δ_d$ is not coassociative. For $Δ_c$, MCB gives no rule for the labels of trace leaves in trees that already contain trace leaves; under the literal extension it is not coassociative, but once a rule and a grading are supplied it yields a graded connected Hopf algebra. The coproduct of Xiao is coassociative, and so is that of Marcolli and Skigin once its definition is extended to trees with trace leaves. These properties do not tell the coassociative coproducts apart; what distinguishes them is the representation that movement leaves behind. We then review how generative grammar has treated moved elements, and confirm that semantic interpretation requires two things of the representation that results from movement. Retention requires that the structure the lower position had before movement be recoverable; Identifiability requires that, in interpreting the lower position, it be determinable which upper element, if any, corresponds to it. None of the five coproducts meets both conditions: Retention is met only by $Δ_c$, and there only in the form of a label, and Identifiability by none. In light of the results on coassociativity, we also reassess the Birkhoff factorization that MCB employs at the syntax-semantics interface. Finally, we indicate at least three directions for algebraic Minimalism.
comment: 26 pages. v2: substantially revised. Corrects v1's claim that the contraction coproduct cannot be coassociative while retaining the labels of traces; adds the coproducts of Marcolli and Skigin (2025) and Xiao (2026); restates the requirement of semantic interpretation as two conditions on the representation that results from movement (Retention and Identifiability), in light of Collins (2026)
♻ ☆ Gradients for Interventions and Activation Values for Detection: Targeted Feature Learning in Language Models
Jonathan Drechsel, Steffen Herbold
Model-internal features can be studied through both their ability to identify a specified concept and their causal effect when manipulated, e.g., through steering or weight editing. A prominent approach to feature learning is Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs), which learn broad feature dictionaries whose relation to particular concepts is typically identified post hoc. However, many interpretability questions are instead hypothesis-driven and concern a concept specified in advance. We study this setting as targeted feature learning, where a single feature is constructed for such a predefined concept. We present a controlled comparison across three model signals (activation values, activation gradients, and parameter gradients) and two estimators (contrastive mean and a learned one-dimensional encoder-decoder), yielding six targeted methods, with CAA and GRADIEND as existing instances and four new methods covering the remaining combinations. We compare these methods against pretrained SAEs across 15 tasks and ten language models, evaluating both detection and causal intervention. Across models, the strongest detection performance is achieved by contrastive activation value methods, whereas the strongest intervention performance is achieved by gradient signals, most strongly with a contrastive mean. Notably, within the contrastive mean family, activation gradients substantially improve intervention over activation values without requiring parameter space interventions. Overall, our results show that targeted feature quality depends jointly on the model signal and estimator, with detection and intervention capturing complementary properties.
♻ ☆ Wieszcz-XIX: A 3.1-Billion-Word Corpus of Pre-1918 Polish and Temporally Bounded Language Models Trained From Scratch
Szymon Kocur
Historical Polish is well documented as a language but annotated in machine-readable form only to about a million words for the period this paper covers; the rest sits behind optical character recognition of variable quality. We present Wieszcz-XIX, a corpus of 6.75 billion tokens (about 3.1 billion words) in 294,369 documents, most of them periodical issues, of Polish published from 1800 to 1918, assembled from Wolne Lektury and the Internet Archive by a pipeline that filters, deduplicates, audits for post-1918 leakage and splits at the document level. It is over three orders of magnitude larger than the annotated corpus of the same period, and we quantify its defects: recognition corruption against a false-positive floor, near-identical duplication, which is removed, and post-1918 leakage, which is excluded from the training corpus itself down to a known residue of 0.04 to 0.38% of its bytes, found in the transcribed source, so the published corpus is the trained one document for document. On a hand-corrected sample the character error rate is 0.68% where the text is legible, and 45% of the sampled passages cannot be corrected. On it we train a ladder of decoder-only models from 47M to 349M parameters from scratch, and measure their temporal boundedness. Against two modern Polish base models, one far larger, the 349M shows a crossover, as does the 107M against the comparator of its size: post-1918 vocabulary costs them about 3.1 bits per byte more than period vocabulary, a gap the comparators do not show, and period vocabulary costs them fewer bits than it costs the comparators. Shown period text, the models keep its spelling and the comparators only partly. Adding parameters gains about twice as much as a second pass over the data. We release the corpus, code and weights. Content warning: the models reproduce period prejudice, including antisemitic statements.

♻ ☆ SynthDCB: World Distillation for Scalable Device Control Benchmarking
Zichen Zhu, Dingye Liu, Nan Jiang, Danyang Zhang, Hang Zheng, Shijie Zheng, Situo Zhang, Kunyao Lan, Yingzi Ma, Sheng Jiang, Lu Chen, Kai Yu
Scaling device-control evaluation requires scaling both interactive worlds and the tasks agents must solve. We introduce SynthDCB, a benchmark for environment awareness and device control built through world distillation (WD). World distillation transforms knowledge of device behaviors, constraints, and environmental effects into modular executable components. A unified capability representation connects tool discovery, world execution, and task synthesis. SynthDCB instantiates Home, Car, and Phone with 114 device classes, 1,166 functions, and 95 events, supporting five task families with 32 subtypes. Explicit states and tool-call traces support task validation and agent evaluation. We evaluate 22 models on 2,560 test tasks; the best achieves 88.8% mean Pass@1. At the richest knowledge level, WD fully covers 22/23 properties, 19/29 functionalities, and 30/39 event requirements specified in a real RobotVacuum protocol. Furthermore, the 1,000 sampled tasks semantically cover 81.6% of the 854 reference instructions in the real-world corpus. SynthDCB connects an expanding space of real-to-sim executable capabilities to verifiable agent evaluation.

♻ ☆ Formalizing Formal Semantics: The Formal Atlas
Stergios Chatzikyriakidis, Aarne Talman
In this paper we present the Formal Atlas, a system for the study of natural language semantics using proof assistants. Our aim is to formalize semantic accounts, check their predictions and compare the implemented theories. To this end, we connect source records and formal developments with an interactive application, where researchers can inspect proofs and modify supported Rocq files. Montague's PTQ and Gallin provide our main cases, followed by dynamic and type-theoretic comparisons, RSA and distributional semantics. We also discuss what it takes to formalize less explicit analyses. Lastly, we consider the use of AI, both for assistance in developing these resources and for inference systems that can make use of them. This work extends an earlier programme in proof-assistant semantics, with research and teaching as complementary uses.
♻ ☆ Polar: A Benchmark for Evaluating Political Bias in LLMs AACL
Sangho Kim, Heejin Kim, Hyunggeun Jeon, Yoonhee Park, Jaejin Lee
Political bias in large language models (LLMs) is increasingly significant, but difficult to measure reproducibly across political and linguistic contexts. We introduce Polar, a 4,026-instance multiple-choice benchmark that measures political bias through option-level likelihoods rather than prompt-based generation. Polar covers two ideological axes and eight issue categories derived from the Manifesto Project, and evaluates models in parallel across U.S. and South Korean political contexts.
