☆ Learning through Discernment: when a small language model learns from user feedback on the job, and when it decays
Sirisilp Kongsilp
Small language models on devices could keep improving by learning, overnight, from their own daily work. Without a true outcome, however, something must decide which attempts are worth learning from, and both the model's own judgment and the user's reaction can be wrong. We study a small model (Qwen3 0.6B and 1.7B) with a LoRA adapter, fine-tuned each night on the attempts a judge accepts, over 127 lives of up to a year on WebShop and $τ^2$-bench. We compare the true outcome, the user's stated verdict and the model's own judgment with a separate, frozen "feedback sense" that reads the user's reaction, built in three versions. Judging itself, the model improved and then decayed, over a year and whatever its adapter. A sense that took polite give-ups for success collapsed 3 of 5 lives; one built for discernment, which first asks whether the user gave a clear verdict, kept 4 of 5 near the oracle for two weeks, then faded on clean but scarce lessons, to the level of self-judging by day 60. When the user cannot see the outcome ($τ^2$-bench), the sense read reactions correctly, yet 80% of its lessons were failures the customer had thanked. Across 97 lives, the share of failed attempts among the lessons predicted the outcome (Spearman $ρ$ = -0.81). In our experiments, learning from users held up only where the judge kept failures out and the user could see the outcome; lopsided lessons may also narrow the model.
comment: 34 pages, 11 figures. Code and data: https://github.com/kratai-desci/research/tree/discernment/discernment
♻ ☆ RPMem: Learning Long-Term Recurrent Parametric Memory Across Sessions for LLM Agents
Fanyu Zhao, Ruike Cao, Liang Dong, Fugen Yao, Jian Xu, Guanjun Jiang, Yinsheng Li, Yifei Zhao, Han Zhang
Long-running LLM agents require memory that persists and evolves across sessions. Text-based memory retrieves and reconstructs past interactions at every query, making long-horizon performance increasingly dependent on retrieval quality and contextual reasoning as histories grow. Parametric memory encodes experience directly into model computation, but existing approaches provide limited support for cross-session memory evolution. Their coupling to a specific backbone further restricts memory reuse after model replacement. We introduce RPMem, a two-stage architecture that compiles each session into a model-independent latent memory through forward computation and selectively integrates it with retained memory via a task-trained recurrent gate. The consolidated memory is then mapped to backbone-specific low-rank adaptation (LoRA) parameters, allowing the encoding capability to transfer when the backbone is replaced. Evaluation across three long-term memory benchmarks and five diverse backbones demonstrates broad generalization with near-constant update cost and memory footprint. With Qwen3-8B on PERMA, RPMem reaches 85.52%, outperforming the strongest parametric and text-based baselines by 5.32 and 12.98 percentage points, respectively. Ablations validate the complementary roles of session compilation and cross-session consolidation, while dynamics analyses reveal that the gate acquires task-specific memory integration strategies. These results establish RPMem as a lifecycle-independent parametric memory framework that maintains evolving cross-session memory that remains reusable across backbone replacements. Our code is available at https://github.com/Quark-Medical/rpmem/tree/main.

♻ ☆ MultiAgentBench: Evaluating the Collaboration and Competition of LLM agents
Kunlun Zhu, Hongyi Du, Zhaochen Hong, Xiaocheng Yang, Shuyi Guo, Zhe Wang, Zhenhailong Wang, Cheng Qian, Xiangru Tang, Heng Ji, Jiaxuan You
Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities as autonomous agents, yet existing benchmarks either focus on single-agent tasks or are confined to narrow domains, failing to capture the dynamics of multi-agent coordination and competition. In this paper, we introduce MultiAgentBench, a comprehensive benchmark designed to evaluate LLM-based multi-agent systems across diverse, interactive scenarios. Our framework measures not only task completion but also the quality of collaboration and competition using novel, milestone-based key performance indicators. Moreover, we evaluate various coordination protocols (including star, chain, tree, and graph topologies) and innovative strategies such as group discussion and cognitive planning. Notably, gpt-4o-mini reaches the average highest task score, graph structure performs the best among coordination protocols in the research scenario, and cognitive planning improves milestone achievement rates by 3%. Code and datasets are public available at https://github.com/MultiagentBench/MARBLE.

♻ ☆ MARBLE: An Agentic Benchmark for Spatial Reasoning and Visual Abstraction NeurIPS 2026
Yulun Jiang, Naël Ouerghemi, Yekun Chai, Maria Brbić, Michael Moor
A central challenge for multimodal language models (MLLMs) in real-world tasks is to abstract visual observations into structured spatial states and reason over how those states change under actions over long horizons. Existing multimodal benchmarks often focus on static question answering, where answers can be directly extracted from images, or assume symbolic states are available and bypass the visual-to-spatial abstraction process. We present MARBLE, a diagnostic benchmark for spatially grounded multimodal reasoning and planning across three agent environments: CUBE, MAZE, and BLOX. MARBLE spans 2D and 3D spatial puzzles under geometric constraints, dynamic layouts, and spatial transformations, with difficulty systematically controlled along two axes: observation format and planning horizon. Extensive evaluations show that frontier MLLMs struggle on MARBLE, with the strongest model GPT-5.4 achieving only a 7.3% average success rate across the three environments in the hardest setting. Pairwise ablations on the open-weight models identify visual-to-spatial abstraction as the major bottleneck: when models must infer spatial states from images instead of receiving human-annotated states, success drops from 50.7% to 3.7% for Gemma-4-31B and from 87.9% to 6.5% for Kimi-K2.6. The results indicate that models often fail to construct precise spatial states from visual observations before planning, a task that is straightforward for humans. Overall, MARBLE reveals the gap in reliable visual-to-spatial abstraction for frontier MLLMs and provides a controlled testbed for measuring progress in spatially grounded multimodal reasoning and planning.

♻ ☆ Improving Cross-Lingual Token Representations by Adding a Pinch of SALT
Guillem Ramírez
Cross-lingual sentence encoders enable scalable transfer across hundreds of languages, powering applications such as translation mining and zero-shot learning in low-resource settings. Although trained for sentence-level alignment, they are increasingly also applied to token-level tasks such as hallucination detection and sequence tagging, exposing a mismatch between training and usage. We propose SALT, a lightweight post-training method that improves token representations by injecting span-level supervision into existing sentence encoders. Across five multilingual token-level benchmarks, SALT achieves the best overall results on four of them, outperforming alternative fine-tuning strategies and competitive encoders. It also improves sentence-level performance on cross-lingual retrieval and classification tasks. These results demonstrate that span-level supervision is an effective signal for improving both token and sentence representations.
♻ ☆ Mixtures of In-Context Learners ACL 2025
Giwon Hong, Emile van Krieken, Edoardo Ponti, Esmeralda S. Whitammer, Pasquale Minervini
In-context learning (ICL) adapts LLMs by providing demonstrations without fine-tuning the model parameters; however, it does not differentiate between demonstrations and quadratically increases the complexity of Transformer LLMs, exhausting the memory. As a solution, we propose Mixtures of In-Context Learners (MoICL), a novel approach to treat subsets of demonstrations as experts and learn a weighting function to merge their output distributions based on a training set. In our experiments, we show performance improvements on 5 out of 7 classification datasets compared to a set of strong baselines (up to +13\% compared to ICL and LENS). Moreover, we enhance the Pareto frontier of ICL by reducing the inference time needed to achieve the same performance with fewer demonstrations. Finally, MoICL is more robust to out-of-domain (up to +11\%), imbalanced (up to +49\%), or noisy demonstrations (up to +38\%) or can filter these out from datasets. Overall, MoICL is a more expressive approach to learning from demonstrations without exhausting the context window or memory.

♻ ☆ Extracting Training Data from Diffusion Language Models via Infilling NeurIPS 2026
Yihan Wang, N. Asokan
Memorization in large language models has been studied almost exclusively through prefix-conditioned extraction, a natural choice for autoregressive models. However, diffusion language models (DLMs) can denoise masked tokens at arbitrary positions. Thus, prefix-only probing reveals only one facet of memorization in DLMs and significantly underestimates the risk of training-data extraction. In order to realistically model extractability of training data in DLMs, we introduce infilling extraction, a data-extraction protocol parameterized by an arbitrary binary mask that subsumes prefix-only probing and accounts for the bidirectional inductive bias of DLMs. Instantiating it on LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B across five extraction modes, three training pipelines, and three corpora covering verbatim and partial leakage, we find that mask geometry governs extractability: edge-conditioned masks extract up to three times more verbatim sequences than prefix-conditioned ones, and bidirectional access opens channels inaccessible in autoregressive models. In particular, an adversary who obtains a PII-redacted copy of the training data can use targeted infilling to raise PII recall on fine-tuned DLMs by 2.5-4.5x over prefix-conditioned extraction, substantially narrowing the gap to a scale-matched autoregressive model and, for LLaDA on email addresses, exceeding it under greedy prefix-conditioned extraction. Tunable parameters for decoding measurably affect extraction performance, while a follow-up supervised fine-tuning stage does not eliminate the prior memorization.

♻ ☆ A conceptual framework for ideology in online discourse beyond the left and right
Kenneth Joseph, Kim Williams, David Lazer
Computational social science (CSS) has largely operationalized ideology along a single left/right partisan axis when studying online discourse. This approach obscures how people interpret and engage with more specific ideological formations related to race, climate, gender, and other domains. We introduce a framework that instead conceptualizes ideology as a multi-level socio-cognitive concept network and then explain how this conceptual model of ideology can be linked to the study of online discourse. In doing so, our framework clarifies how ideology manifests in discourse alongside related social processes such as framing, and provides an argument for better understanding of when and why we might study multiple concepts, such as values and beliefs, together in one analysis. More broadly, it bridges methods used to study online discourse with ideology theory, enabling richer analyses of social discourse that benefit both fields. Although primarily theoretical, we provide a concrete operationalization of the framework on a sample of data, allowing us to show one example of how it can be used on real data.

♻ ☆ ThinkSum: Probabilistic reasoning over sets using large language models ACL 2023
Batu Ozturkler, Esmeralda S. Whitammer, Zhen Wang, Nebojsa Jojic
Large language models (LLMs) have a substantial capacity for high-level analogical reasoning: reproducing patterns in linear text that occur in their training data (zero-shot evaluation) or in the provided context (few-shot in-context learning). However, recent studies show that even the more advanced LLMs fail in scenarios that require reasoning over multiple objects or facts and making sequences of logical deductions. We propose a two-stage probabilistic inference paradigm, ThinkSum, which reasons over sets of objects or facts in a structured manner. In the first stage (Think - retrieval of associations), a LLM is queried in parallel over a set of phrases extracted from the prompt or an auxiliary model call. In the second stage (Sum - probabilistic inference or reasoning), the results of these queries are aggregated to make the final prediction. We demonstrate the possibilities and advantages of ThinkSum on the BIG-bench suite of LLM evaluation tasks, achieving improvements over the state of the art using GPT-family models on thirteen difficult tasks, often with far smaller model variants. We also compare and contrast ThinkSum with other proposed modifications to direct prompting of LLMs, such as variants of chain-of-thought prompting. Our results suggest that because the probabilistic inference in ThinkSum is performed outside of calls to the LLM, ThinkSum is less sensitive to prompt design, yields more interpretable predictions, and can be flexibly combined with latent variable models to extract structured knowledge from LLMs. Overall, our proposed paradigm represents a promising approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of LLMs.

♻ ☆ Coherence boosting: When your pretrained language model is not paying enough attention ACL 2022

♻ ☆ How Far Does Online RL Generalize in Mobile GUI Agents? A Controlled Study with AndroidWorld-Generalization
Li Gu, Zihuan Jiang, Zhixiang Chi, Huan Liu, Ziqiang Wang, Yuanhao Yu, Linqiang Guo, Yang Wang
Online reinforcement learning (RL) has advanced mobile GUI agents on AndroidWorld to over 80\% success, but these systems train and evaluate in the same environment, and their training tasks are not released for auditing. How far RL generalizes beyond its training contexts therefore remains unmeasured. We formalize the mobile environment as a Contextual MDP and instantiate a ladder of three progressively distant context shifts as AndroidWorld-Generalization: Unseen Instance, Unseen Template, and Unseen Application, with separation verified manually. In addition, we provide an open RL training system that makes studies at this scale feasible. Applying the identical RL algorithm to two priors of different origin and competence, UI-TARS-7B-SFT at 23.0\% and Qwen3-VL-8B at 52.6\%, we find that RL improves both in distribution but that gains fall monotonically up the ladder (+17.5/+11.1/+4.2 and +10.3/+7.4/+0), with training performance rising throughout while evaluation performance saturates. The decay is algorithm-agnostic, reproduced under PPO, and RL contributes less as the prior strengthens. Few-shot adaptation in the target application recovers +22.9, motivating test-time adaptation as a complement to further RL training. We release the benchmark, training system, recipes, and model weights.
♻ ☆ RABBiT: Rapidly adaptive BOLD foundation model via brain-tuning for accurate zero-shot and few-shot prediction of speech-elicited responses in the brain NeurIPS 2026
Omer Moussa, Mariya Toneva
Language understanding in the brain is context-dependent, varying across experimental stimuli and individuals, which makes it difficult to build computational models that generalize across both. This calls for a foundation model of language-evoked brain activity that can capture shared structure while adapting efficiently to new participants and inputs. We introduce RABBiT (Rapidly Adaptive BOLD foundation model via BraIn-Tuning), a compact audio-to-fMRI encoder designed for accurate zero- and few-shot prediction. A comprehensive evaluation on 324 participants across multiple unseen fMRI datasets shows that RABBiT enables accurate zero-shot prediction of fMRI responses to natural speech across auditory and language-selective regions, surpassing the SOTA foundation model for fMRI and predictions based on group averages. With as little as 10 minutes of participant-specific data, RABBiT further improves performance via parameter-efficient tuning, substantially outperforming per-participant linear models. RABBiT's performance is driven by two key innovations: (1) learned region-specific attention, and (2) a decomposition of brain responses into shared and subject-specific components, combined with a brain-tuned speech backbone. In addition to supporting strong predictive accuracy, the structured, region-specific representations that RABBiT learns enable interpretability. By eliminating the need for extensive per-participant data and model fitting, RABBiT enables scalable population-level analyses of language in the human brain. We make the code available at https://github.com/bridge-ai-neuro/rabbit.

♻ ☆ Ambiguous Examples Help, But Full Data Wins: Revisiting Dataset Cartography for Extractive Adversarial QA EACL 2027
Paul K. Mandal
Dataset cartography argues that training dynamics can reveal regions of a dataset that matter differently for learning: easy-to-learn examples aid optimization, hard-to-learn examples often expose annotation problems, and ambiguous examples contribute strongly to out-of-distribution generalization. A striking implication of the original work is that a model trained on only 33% ambiguous examples can outperform full-data training on several OOD evaluations, including a QNLI reformulation of SQuAD evaluated on Adversarial SQuAD. We revisit this claim in a stricter setting: open-ended extractive question answering, where models must predict answer spans rather than classify question-sentence pairs. For each encoder family, we compute clean-source SQuAD training dynamics, train on full, random, easy-ranked, ambiguous-ranked, and hard-ranked subsets, and evaluate robustness on adversarial-only AddSent and AddOneSent rows. Ambiguous-ranked 33% subsets beat random 33% subsets on exact-match accuracy in all 14 model-evaluation cells and on F1 in 12/14 cells. However, full-data training beats ambiguous-ranked training in 12/14 exact-match cells and 12/14 F1 cells, with mean full-minus-ambiguous gains of 3.40 EM and 4.10 F1. The most thoroughly replicated ELECTRA-small panel is also the main exception to the ambiguous-over-random F1 gains. These results show that clean-source cartography remains useful for reduced-data selection, but its OOD advantage does not carry over as a general replacement for full-data training in extractive adversarial QA.

♻ ☆ Typed Knowledge over Constructive Institutions: A Proof-Relevant Type Theory for Verified Science
Hans-Martin Will
Mathematical proof assistants such as Coq, Agda, and Lean 4 build proof irrelevance into definitional conversion ($p \equiv q$ for $p, q : P$ with $P : Prop$), erasing which derivation established a theorem. While natural for mathematics over fixed axioms, judgmental proof irrelevance is incompatible with verified scientific stores, which must inspect and compare independent derivations of a claim to assess consilience and compute counterfactual retractions when an assay, reagent, or model is invalidated. Moreover, combining definitional proof irrelevance with subsingleton elimination breaks normalization and makes algorithmic conversion non-transitive. We present the type-theoretic and institutional foundations of Eigenius, a verified knowledge substrate for science. First, we formulate a dependent kernel with cumulative universes ($Prop, Set, Type_k$), impredicative $Prop$, inductive families with index-forced subsingleton elimination, and intensional identity types, where definitional equality $\equiv$ is untyped joinability under an orthogonal CRS and proof irrelevance is a witnessed axiom ($irrel : ΠP{:}Prop.\,Πp,q{:}P.\,Id\,P\,p\,q$). Second, we unify universe cumulativity, $Π$-covariant subtyping, and ontology class inclusions in a coercion-free subsumption judgment whose bidirectional fragments ($\mathcal{K}_0, \mathcal{K}_1$) satisfy subject reduction, compile to unification-free checkers ($\mathcal{A}_0, \mathcal{A}_1$), and match declarative typing. Third, we organize external verifiers into a three-level institutional hierarchy with explicit axiom policies (Adopt, Discharge, Relativize), internalize a justification logic with Why-provenance support $supp(j,P)$, and embed Cooper's Type Theory with Records (TTR) for open-world knowledge graphs. Finally, we prove consistency in Grothendieck universes and mechanize the metatheory in Lean 4 with zero sorry axioms.
♻ ☆ Knowledge Localization in Mixture-of-Experts LLMs Using Cross-Lingual Inconsistency NeurIPS 2026
Lucas Bandarkar, Alan Ansell, Trevor Cohn
Modern LLMs continue to exhibit significant variance in behavior across languages, such as being able to recall factual information in some languages but not others. While typically studied as a problem to be mitigated, in this work, we propose leveraging this cross-lingual inconsistency as a tool for interpretability in mixture-of-experts (MoE) LLMs. Our knowledge localization framework contrasts routing for sets of languages where the model correctly recalls information from languages where it fails. This allows us to isolate model components that play a functional role in answering about a piece of knowledge. Our method proceeds in two stages: (1) querying the model with difficult factual questions across a diverse set of languages to generate "success" and "failure" activation buckets and then (2) applying a statistical contrastive analysis to the MoE router logits to identify experts important for knowledge. To validate the necessity of this small number of experts for answering a knowledge question, we deactivate them and re-ask the question. We find that despite only deactivating about 20 out of 6000 experts, the model no longer answers correctly in over 40% of cases. Generally, this method provides a realistic and scalable knowledge localization approach to address increasingly complex LLMs.

♻ ☆ Aligning Language Model Benchmarks with Pairwise Preferences NeurIPS 2026
Marco Gutierrez, Xinyi Leng, Hannah Cyberey, Jonathan Richard Schwarz, Ahmed Alaa, Thomas Hartvigsen
Language model benchmarks are pervasive and computationally-efficient proxies for real-world downstream performance. However, many recent works find that benchmarks often fail to predict downstream utility. While some works have begun diagnosing sources of misalignment, there remain no ways to systematically update benchmarks to align their scores with downstream usage. Towards bridging this gap, we introduce and study \textit{benchmark alignment}, where we use information about downstream model performance to automatically update benchmarks, specifically aiming to update static benchmarks so they generalizably rank models according to new pairwise preferences. Our experiments involving 4576 language models and 6 benchmarks show that reweighting benchmark items can successfully rank unseen models, even generalizing across model scales in most cases. And while naive alignment unsurprisingly requires large numbers of models and benchmark questions, an oracle experiment suggests this could be reduced to as few as 20 well-chosen models. Overall, our work takes a step towards efficiently aligning benchmark development with downstream tasks. All of our code, models, and data are publicly-available: https://github.com/hartvigsen-group/benchalign

♻ ☆ Quicksviewer: An LMM with Early Video Compression using Annealed Gumbel-Softmax
Ji Qi, Yuan Yao, Yushi Bai, Bin Xu, Juanzi Li, Zhiyuan Liu, Tat-Seng Chua
Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) uniformly perceive video frames, creating computational inefficiency for videos with inherently varying temporal redundancy. This paper present **Quicksviewer**, an LMM with new perceiving paradigm that partitions a nonuniform redundant video into varying cubes using Gumbel Softmax, followed by a unified resampling for each cube to achieve efficient video understanding. We further propose a Gumbel-noise annealing strategy that enables stable end-to-end LMM training with the discrete cubing network. This simple and intuitive approach dynamically compress video online based on the momentum of frame differences, significantly reducing spatiotemporal complexity (overall 45$\times$ compression rate), while enabling efficient training with large receptive field. We train the model from a language backbone through three progressive stages, each incorporating lengthy videos on average of 420s/1fps thanks to the perceiving efficiency. With only 0.8M total video samples, our model outperforms contemporary LMMs with the same backbone and comparable parameters amount. An experiment in A/B pretraining with same backbone and training data shows that, w/ cubing achieves lower training loss and higher benchmark accuracy (+2.1) than the fixed partitioning baseline, strictly validating the benefits. Moreover, the front-loaded compression in encoding substantially reduces latency in the computationally expensive prefill and decoding, making it suited to mainstream EPD disaggregated inference deployment. Code: https://quicksviewer.github.io.
♻ ☆ CRAM: Centroid-Routing and Adaptive MoE for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning EMNLP 2026
Jun-Tao Tang, Zhen-Hao Xie, Yu-Cheng Shi, Da-Wei Zhou
Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) unify heterogeneous vision-language tasks under a shared generative framework via instruction tuning, yet real-world deployment demands continuous capability expansion, making Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT) essential. Existing methods either update all tasks with a shared parameter set or allocate dedicated modules for each new task. Shared updates force heterogeneous tasks to compete, causing forgetting of learned capabilities. Conversely, isolated expansion prevents interference but severely limits parameter efficiency over long task streams. To address this dilemma, we propose CRAM (Centroid-Routing and Adaptive MoE). Specifically, by isolating task-specific patterns into independent modules, CRAM mitigates catastrophic forgetting across tasks. To further boost parameter efficiency, we utilize adaptive-rank instantiation to identify the capability gap between existing expert capability and new task demands, and dynamically allocate only the necessary parameters. To ensure stable reuse among tasks, centroid-guided routing recognizes and activates existing experts' capabilities, while an orthogonality penalty confines new updates to task-specific directions, preventing re-learning general capability. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate its superiority over existing methods. Code is available at https://github.com/LAMDA-CL/EMNLP2026-CRAM.

♻ ☆ Gamer: A Generalist Vision-Language-Action Foundation Model for Long-Horizon Real-Time Game Agent
Run Luo, Kuan Zhang, Qi Deng, Yukun Chen, Zhihao Yang, Yufei Zhang, Renrong Hu, Yi Zhang, Guiyu Ma, Tianyu Fan, Longze Chen, Hao Sun, Jiaming Li, Jiaming Yang, Xiaohao Liu, Tao Tan, Minzheng Wang, Hangtian Jia, Shuai Yuan, Lvdi Wang, Xiao Liu, Chao Ma, Yuting Zou, Yinhe Zheng, Xiaobo Xia, Dawei Jiang, Shaoqing Ren
We introduce Gamer, a generalist agent framework built for long-horizon tasks in complex interactive environments. A shared training and deployment pipeline supports interaction with both conventional 2D graphical interfaces and complex 3D simulated worlds. We first continue pre-training on a dataset constructed from tens of thousands of hours of device-level keyboard and mouse interaction. These trajectories span simulated games and embodied environments, allowing the model to learn environmental dynamics across both physical and virtual domains. ♻ ☆ Improving LLM Final Representations with Inter-Layer Geometry NeurIPS 2026
Tom Ulanovski, Eyal Blyachman, Maya Bechler-Speicher
The standard in LLM-based prediction is to use the final-layer representation as the input to a downstream predictor. However, intermediate layers may encode complementary task-relevant signals. Existing approaches therefore either search for the best layer for each task or apply expensive attention-based mechanisms to learn inter-layer aggregation. In this work, we first show that such complexity is unnecessary: a lightweight Graph Neural Network over a fully connected graph of LLM layers is more efficient and achieves significantly stronger predictive performance than existing approaches. We then introduce the Cayley-Encoder, which further improves both efficiency and predictive performance by replacing the fully connected graph with a Cayley graph over SL(2, Zn). These Cayley graphs provide a mathematically grounded topology that is sparse, regular by construction, and has low diameter. This enables effective communication across layers while constraining the aggregation structure and reducing the risk of GNN overfitting. In an evaluation of Cayley-Encoder across 13 tasks and 13 LLMs, Cayley-Encoder consistently outperforms baselines, achieving improvements of up to 48 percentage points in accuracy, while introducing at most 0.1% additional parameters relative to the LLM size. We further show that Cayley-Encoder is effective in few-shot regimes. Finally, we show that Cayley-Encoder outperforms LoRA fine-tuning while operating on the frozen LLM. We conclude with an explainability analysis showing that multiple layers contribute meaningfully to the final prediction, supporting our hypothesis.

♻ ☆ Meta-Learning Preferences for Multilingual LLM Alignment NeurIPS 2026
Jiaying Lin, Seongho Son, Nam Phuong Tran, Long Tran-thanh, Ilija Bogunovic, Debmalya Mandal

♻ ☆ HYPER: Hypothesis-Guided Planning for Evidence-Seeking Retrieval EACL 2027
Hangeol Chang, Changsun Lee, Seungjoon Rho, Junho Yeo, Jong Chul Ye
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) improves Large Language Models (LLMs) by grounding generation in external, non-parametric knowledge. However, when a task requires choosing among competing possibilities, such as a diagnosis among plausible alternatives, topically relevant context is often insufficient to drive the final decision. To address this issue, we propose HYPER: Hypothesis-Guided Planning for Evidence-Seeking Retrieval, a training-free pre-retrieval planning framework that reorients RAG from topic-oriented retrieval to evidence-oriented retrieval. HYPER uses a provisional answer hypothesis as a planning anchor. It then decomposes retrieval into three evidence roles. Support seeks evidence for the provisional answer. Distinction retrieves criteria that separate competing alternatives. Feature grounds salient question features needed for the decision. This planning step steers retrieval toward evidence near the decision boundary, enabling the generator to answer from context that is more directly aligned with the required choice. On MedQA and MMLU-Med, where single-query retrieval falls below no-retrieval chain-of-thought prompting, HYPER achieves the best average accuracy among ten baselines, improving over Simple RAG by 5.9 and 3.6 points, respectively. Its gains transfer to the 14 subjects of MMLU-Pro, at the cost of two additional LLM calls per question. Code is available at https://github.com/hangeol/HypothesisRAG.

♻ ☆ Training-Free Dynamic Upcycling of Expert Language Models NeurIPS 2026
Eros Fanì, Oğuzhan Ersoy
Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable performance on a wide range of specialized tasks, exhibiting strong problem-solving capabilities. However, training these models is prohibitively expensive, and they often lack domain-specific expertise because they rely on general knowledge datasets. Expertise finetuning can address this issue; however, it often leads to overspecialization, and developing a single multi-domain expert remains difficult due to diverging objectives. Furthermore, multitask training is challenging due to interference and catastrophic forgetting. Existing work proposes combining the expertise of dense models within a Mixture of Experts (MoE) architecture, although this approach still requires multitask finetuning. To address these issues, we introduce Dynamic Upcycling MoE (DUME), a novel approach that reuses dense experts trained on different domains to construct a unified MoE model. Our method builds a single multitask model that preserves the capabilities of the original dense experts without requiring additional training. DUME is both cost-efficient and scalable: by leveraging the closed-form solution of ridge regression, it eliminates the need for further optimization and enables experts to be added or removed dynamically. Our extensive experiments on different tasks (in causal language modeling and reasoning settings) with several model sizes (between 115M and 9B parameters) and architectures demonstrate that DUME consistently outperforms baseline approaches. In particular, in a reasoning setting, DUME can also surpass the dense expert model's performance in its domain, achieving 104% of the dense expert's performance on average. Finally, we also show that the DUME model can be fine-tuned to further improve performance in complex reasoning scenarios. Our code is available at: github.com/gensyn-ai/dume.

♻ ☆ Twists and Turns: A Graph-Based Benchmark and Framework for Long-Term Memory in Exploration Tasks
Chongyuan Dai, Yaling Shen, Zirui Song, Junfeng Liao, Jinpeng Hu, Xiuying Chen
Long-term problem-solving processes, such as scientific research and software development, proceed along an evolving trial-and-error trajectory toward a central goal. We term these \textit{exploration tasks}, which require models to remember not only facts but also how solutions are tested against intermediate outcomes and early attempts are continually revised or revived. However, existing long-term memory research focuses on factual recall and updates, overlooking the interconnected nature of exploration. We observe that exploration processes naturally form graphs, with branching approaches linked by revisions and reconsiderations. Motivated by this, we propose \textsc{LExMem}, a graph-guided benchmark designed to evaluate \underline{\textsc{Mem}}ory in \underline{\textsc{L}}ong-term \underline{\textsc{Ex}}ploration tasks. We model exploration processes as graphs and introduce an evolutionary mechanism to increase their structural complexity, yielding 224 dialogues and 2,018 question-answer instances derived from graph structures. Building on the same insight, we propose \textsc{LExGraph}, a graph-based memory framework that incrementally organizes dialogues into interconnected memory nodes and retrieves evidence through hybrid retrieval and neighbor expansion. Extensive experiments demonstrate that \textsc{LExMem} poses a significant challenge to existing retrieval- and memory-based systems, whereas \textsc{LExGraph} achieves superior performance and generalizes effectively to external benchmarks. Codes and datasets are available at https://github.com/mbzuai-nlp/LExMem.

♻ ☆ Reinforcement Learning with Reference-Based Rewards for Language Model Reasoning and Alignment
Shuai Zhao, Yunqiu Xu, Linchao Zhu, Ling Chen, Yi Yang
Reinforcement learning (RL) powers two major lines of large language model (LLM) post-training: reasoning, which elicits deliberate, verifiable problem solving, and alignment, which steers models toward helpful, harmless, and honest behavior. Both hinge on reward design. Reasoning relies on rule-based verification of final answers, whose binary feedback is blind to intermediate derivations, whereas process reward models (PRMs) require step-level annotations too costly to scale. Alignment depends on reward models (RMs) trained on binary preference pairs, making both data collection and model training resource-intensive. We propose RL2R, reinforcement learning with reference-based rewards, as a unified and inexpensive alternative. Using pre-existing or distilled references, RL2R measures semantic similarity with lightweight metrics such as BERTScore between a sampled generation and a reference answer or solution. Similarity preserves ordinal quality signals that binary feedback discards: responses closer to the reference earn higher rewards, and sound derivations receive credit even when the final conclusion is wrong. For mathematical reasoning, augmenting rule-based verification with answer- and step-level similarity provides low-cost process supervision and outperforms answer-only verification. For alignment, a critic-free REINFORCE-style instantiation covers general preference, safety, and confidence tasks on par with RM-based methods. Reference-based rewards thus offer a cost-effective, versatile reward source spanning reasoning and alignment.
comment: The code is at https://github.com/mzhaoshuai/RL2R. This version adds a reasoning part with reference-based rewards compared to the previous version
♻ ☆ Challenging Quadratic Attention$\unicode{x2013}$A Holistic View on the Rise of Alternative Language Model Architectures
Alexander M. Fichtl, Jeremias Bohn, Josefin Kelber, Edoardo Mosca, Georg Groh
Transformers have dominated sequence processing tasks for the past seven years$\unicode{x2013}$most notably language modeling. However, the inherent quadratic complexity of their attention mechanism remains a significant bottleneck as context length increases. We review and distill the recent efforts to overcome this bottleneck, including advances in (sub-quadratic) attention variants, recurrent neural networks, state space models, and hybrid architectures. We critically analyze approaches regarding compute and memory complexity, benchmark results, and fundamental limitations to assess whether the dominance of pure-attention transformers may soon be challenged, which we consider possible, particularly in domain-specific and edge-device applications.

♻ ☆ Ceiling-Clipped Acceptance Histograms Indicate Stranded Speed-up in Block-Diffusion Speculative Decoding
Ephrem Wu
Speculative decoding speeds up generation using an efficient draft model (drafter) to propose tokens that a target model verifies in one pass, preserving the target's output distribution. High-acceptance block-diffusion drafters, such as DFlash and DFlare, fill an entire block in one parallel pass. In many cycles, the target model accepts all draft tokens in the block, so the drafter exhausts its trained horizon before verification ever fails. We call this unrealized acceptance stranded speed-up. Evaluating a drafter by its per-prompt or per-cycle mean committed length alone hides this phenomenon, whereas the acceptance histogram exposes this bottleneck as a spike in the ceiling bin, the fraction of cycles that accept the entire block. We recommend reviewing the acceptance histogram before spending more training compute. Naively widening the block at inference could reduce acceptance for drafters with bidirectional attention, since, once the block outgrows its training size, the drafter's bidirectional attention shifts its distribution even at early positions, which empirically degrades acceptance there. We therefore post-train the drafter to a longer block with a short curriculum that emphasizes the newly exposed positions, a method we call DBloom. We continue training the pretrained DFlash and DFlare drafters at the original block size of 16, before expanding them to 24 (B16 to B24) on Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-4B. Compared to the original B16 drafters, this technique raises per-prompt committed length by up to +1.7 tokens and decode speed-up by a median of +10.2% versus +5.7% without the continuation. On high-ceiling benchmarks, the speed-up rises to +11.9%. On Gemma-4-12B-IT and GPT-OSS-20B, expansion also raises committed length on every benchmark. Against JetSpec, a tree drafter, DBloom has higher committed length by point estimate on every benchmark at tree budgets up to 64 nodes.
♻ ☆ S-GRADES -- Studying Generalization of Student Response Assessments in Diverse Evaluative Settings LREC 2026
Tasfia Seuti, Sagnik Ray Choudhury
Evaluating student responses, from long essays to short factual answers, is a key challenge in educational NLP. Automated Essay Scoring (AES) focuses on holistic writing qualities such as coherence and argumentation, while Automatic Short Answer Grading (ASAG) emphasizes factual correctness and conceptual understanding. Despite their shared goal, these paradigms have progressed in isolation with fragmented datasets, inconsistent metrics, and separate communities. We introduce S-GRADES (Studying Generalization of Student Response Assessments in Diverse Evaluative Settings), a web-based benchmark that consolidates 14 diverse grading datasets under a unified interface with standardized access and reproducible evaluation protocols. The benchmark is fully open-source and designed for extensibility, enabling continuous integration of new datasets and evaluation settings. To demonstrate the utility of S-GRADES, we evaluate three state-of-the-art large language models across the benchmark using multiple reasoning strategies in prompting. We further examine the effects of exemplar selection and cross-dataset exemplar transfer. Our analyses illustrate how benchmark-driven evaluation reveals reliability and generalization gaps across essay and short-answer grading tasks, highlighting the importance of standardized, cross-paradigm assessment.

♻ ☆ EasyOPD: An Easy-to-use On-Policy Distillation Framework for Large Language Models
Jie Sun, Mao Zheng, Mingyang Song, Qiyong Zhong, Gengsheng Li, Zhepei Hong, Chang Wu, Pengfei Liu, Junfeng Fang, Xiang Wang
Conventional language-model distillation often relies on fixed teacher-generated data, which may not cover the states encountered by an evolving student policy. On-policy distillation (OPD) instead collects teacher or evaluator supervision on student-generated rollouts. However, existing OPD methods differ substantially in supervision form, tokenizer compatibility, teacher access, and supervision granularity, leading to fragmented implementations that are difficult to reproduce and extend. We present \textsc{EasyOPD}, an on-policy distillation framework built on verl, a distributed reinforcement-learning framework for large language models. \textsc{EasyOPD} separates user-side configuration, method-specific supervision logic, and verl-based execution. Its method modules connect to the shared backend through extension boundaries for loss construction, rollout metadata, reward processing, tokenizer alignment, and teacher-side computation. We instantiate representative methods for three OPD settings -- cross-tokenizer OPD, on-policy self-distillation, and step-wise OPD. Experiments on reasoning, code-generation, scientific-knowledge, and tool-use benchmarks show that these implementations can be executed through the same verl-based backend while retaining their method-specific objectives and task-dependent performance profiles. We release \textsc{EasyOPD} with runnable YAML configurations, documentation, and an installable demonstration package and video.

♻ ☆ Distill What You Trust: Reliability-Aware Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation
Jie Sun, Mao Zheng, Mingyang Song, Zeyuan Liu, Gengsheng Li, Houcheng Jiang, Yilin Cheng, Bichuan Feng, Yuchen Cai, Junfeng Fang, Xiang Wang
Multi-teacher on-policy distillation allows a student to learn from complementary specialists on its own trajectories. Domain-routed approaches, however, select one teacher per example and keep it fixed throughout the response. This design both depends on domain labels that mixed training corpora often lack and cannot adapt teacher selection when the expertise required changes within a trajectory. We observe that each specialist deviates more from a shared reference on in-domain prompts than on out-of-domain prompts, on average. Based on this observation, we propose \textbf{TrustMOPD}, which replaces example-level teacher selection with label-free, token-level supervision allocation. At each student-generated prefix, TrustMOPD measures this displacement in next-token preferences, calibrates its magnitude across teachers, and uses the resulting scores as proxies for local reliability to weight teacher-specific distillation losses. Evaluated across mathematics, code, and instruction following, TrustMOPD closes 91.5\% and 98.0\% of the overall-score gap between the initial student and oracle-routed teachers when trained on \textsc{SingleCap} and \textsc{MultiCap}, respectively, compared with 54.4\% and 54.5\% for the strongest label-free baseline in each setting. On \textsc{SingleCap}, it approaches label-based MOPD without using domain labels. Our code is available at https://github.com/sunjie279/TrustMOPD-.

♻ ☆ Embedding Cosine Measures Text Length Before It Measures Homogenization
Fengming Liu, Mingyu Shu
Mean pairwise cosine similarity between transformer embeddings is a standard measure of whether online discourse is converging under generative AI. When the embeddings are mean-pooled outputs of an encoder never trained for sentence similarity, this measure mostly tracks text length. In 6.06 million Reddit comments, an uncentered DeBERTa-v3 measure had suggested that only conservative discourse broke toward homogeneity after late 2022. Truncating the same comments from 10 to 150 words raises their mean cosine by eight times the reported break. A placebo that swaps every comment for a random one of equal length reproduces 81% of the asymmetry, and a fixed-content simulation reproduces 78%. The leading principal direction carries 99.9% of raw DeBERTa homogeneity, and once length is handled the asymmetry disappears under every encoder. On 575,000 Stack Exchange answers the measure reports a post-ChatGPT homogenization of technical sites that the placebo fully reproduces, although answers grew longer. A census of 31 studies locates the risk where anisotropic encoders meet time-series or length-unequal designs. A reverse control on a well-known experiment shows that centering with renormalization can reverse a genuine effect. We propose a low-cost diagnostic protocol. Corrected measures show that the two political camps converged between 2019 and 2022 and stopped converging before ChatGPT. Cross-camp replies engage their targets more closely than same-camp replies.

♻ ☆ MoE--LoRA Methods for Multilingual Adaptation: A Systematic Empirical Study EACL 2027
Nikit Srivastava, Joel Dag, René Speck, Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo
Routed low-rank adapters offer a parameter-efficient means to improve the performance of large language models (LLMs) across tasks. Hence, it is assumed that they can also be used to improve the support of low-resource languages in LLMs, which currently serve English far better than the thousands of other languages spoken worldwide. However, this assumption has never been verified empirically under a shared multilingual evaluation protocol. We address exactly this gap by comparing eight low-rank adaptation (LoRA) methods on a frozen base model, using 146 training runs across 200 unique languages and more than 10,000 evaluations. Our results suggest that a reduced-scale search can identify viable configurations of LoRA methods but does not reliably predict their relative performance, as the top-ranking configuration is never best at scale. LoRA reliably improves language-model fit on translation data, consistently degrades reading comprehension, and benefits low-resource languages most. These findings suggest that LoRA methods should be regarded as targeted tools rather than general-purpose means for improving the multilingual capabilities of LLMs.

♻ ☆ A Survey on Collaborative Mechanisms Between Large and Small Language Models
Yi Chen, Haiquan Wang, JiaHao Zhao, HaoHao Han
Large Language Models (LLMs) deliver powerful AI capabilities but face deployment challenges due to high resource costs and latency, whereas Small Language Models (SLMs) offer efficiency and deployability at the cost of reduced performance. Collaboration between LLMs and SLMs emerges as a crucial paradigm to synergistically balance these trade-offs, enabling advanced AI applications, especially on resource-constrained edge devices. This survey provides a comprehensive overview of LLM-SLM collaboration, detailing various interaction mechanisms (pipeline, routing, auxiliary, distillation, fusion), key enabling technologies, and diverse application scenarios driven by on-device needs like low latency, privacy, personalization, and offline operation. While highlighting the significant potential for creating more efficient, adaptable, and accessible AI, we also discuss persistent challenges including system overhead, inter-model consistency, robust task allocation, evaluation complexity, and security/privacy concerns. Future directions point towards more intelligent adaptive frameworks, deeper model fusion, and expansion into multimodal and embodied AI, positioning LLM-SLM collaboration as a key driver for the next generation of practical and ubiquitous artificial intelligence.
♻ ☆ Circuit Hypernetworks for Quantum-Augmented Diffusion Language Models
Xiaoqiang Wang, Mengyang Xiong, Jun Dai, Bang Liu
Improving pretrained language models with limited adaptation data calls for computational modules that are both effective and scalable. Parameterized quantum circuits offer expressive transformations for language modeling, yet existing quantum-enhanced language models apply the same predefined circuit design to every token in an input sequence, regardless of its content. This limitation is particularly acute in masked-diffusion language models, where token representations evolve across iterative denoising steps while the circuit design remains fixed. We introduce HyperQ, a quantum-augmented masked-diffusion language model that dynamically synthesizes token-specific quantum circuits. Built upon an IQP-inspired architecture, HyperQ employs a lightweight circuit hypernetwork to generate rotation angles and measurement bases conditioned on each token's hidden state. The resulting quantum expectation values are projected back into the frozen language-model backbone through a residual connection. To support wider quantum registers, HyperQ bounds circuit interaction degree, maintaining per-qubit gradient scale and readout cost independent of register width. Across six zero-shot benchmarks, scaling HyperQ from 16 to 64 qubits improves average accuracy from 47.65 to 54.30, surpassing the frozen backbone's 49.59 by 4.71 points while using only one tenth of the fine-tuning data used by classical baselines. We further execute the generated circuits on an IBM 156-qubit superconducting quantum processor, demonstrating their feasibility on physical quantum hardware.

♻ ☆ Polar: A Benchmark for Evaluating Political Bias in LLMs AACL
Sangho Kim, Heejin Kim, Hyunggeun Jeon, Yoonhee Park, Jaejin Lee
Political bias in large language models (LLMs) is increasingly significant, but difficult to measure reproducibly across political and linguistic contexts. We introduce Polar, a 4,026-instance multiple-choice benchmark that measures political bias through option-level likelihoods rather than prompt-based generation. Polar covers two ideological axes and eight issue categories derived from the Manifesto Project, and evaluates models in parallel across U.S. and South Korean political contexts. Across 38 LLMs, measured bias varies systematically with political context, issue category, model group, and presentation language. All models lean left-progressive on U.S. political content, but show more centered and mixed patterns on South Korean content. Translation experiments further show that presentation language alone can shift measured bias. These findings highlight the need for multilingual and cross-contextual evaluation of political bias in LLMs. The code and dataset are publicly available on GitHub (https://github.com/mcrl/Polar) and Hugging Face (https://huggingface.co/datasets/thunder-research-group/Polar).

♻ ☆ Multimodal Information Fusion for Chart Understanding: A Survey of MLLMs -- Evolution, Limitations, and Cognitive Enhancement
Zhihang Yi, Jian Zhao, Jiancheng Lv, Tao Wang
Chart understanding is a quintessential information fusion task, requiring the seamless integration of graphical and textual data to extract meaning. The advent of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) has revolutionized this domain, yet the landscape of MLLM-based chart analysis remains fragmented and lacks systematic organization. This survey provides a comprehensive roadmap of this nascent frontier by structuring the domain's core components. We begin by analyzing the fundamental challenges of fusing visual and linguistic information in charts. We then categorize downstream tasks and datasets, introducing a novel taxonomy of canonical and non-canonical benchmarks to highlight the field's expanding scope. Subsequently, we present a comprehensive evolution of methodologies, tracing the progression from classic deep learning techniques to state-of-the-art MLLM paradigms that leverage sophisticated fusion strategies. By critically examining the limitations of current models, particularly their perceptual and reasoning deficits, we identify promising future directions, including advanced alignment techniques and reinforcement learning for cognitive enhancement. This survey aims to equip researchers and practitioners with a structured understanding of how MLLMs are transforming chart information fusion and to catalyze progress toward more robust and reliable systems.
♻ ☆ Unlocking Continuous Diffusion Language Models via Discrete Stochastic Localization
Yunshu Wu, Jiayi Cheng, Longxuan Yu, Partha Thakuria, Rob Brekelmans, Evangelos E. Papalexakis, Greg Ver Steeg
Continuous diffusion is a natural framework for non-autoregressive generation but has generally lagged behind masked discrete diffusion models (MDMs) on discrete sequence generation. We argue that continuous diffusion can effectively model discrete sequences through the joint design of noise parameterization and token representation geometry. We introduce \emph{Discrete Stochastic Localization} (DSL), a continuous-state framework that combines a stochastic localization channel with unit-sphere token embeddings, resulting in a Bayes-optimal denoiser that depends only on the noisy state, without additional timestep conditioning. A single trained denoising network then supports multiple sampling methods, from continuous denoising to masked diffusion, random-order autoregressive generation and hybrid continuous-then-discrete generation. Parallel generation in this continuous-state framework, therefore, need not imply synchronized denoising: some tokens can establish context earlier while others remain open to refinement. Fine-tuning a pretrained MDLM checkpoint with DSL substantially improves generation quality, as measured by MAUVE, on OpenWebText across all step budgets from $T{=}128$ to $T{=}1024$. The same checkpoint supports random-order autoregressive sampling, and a hybrid continuous-then-discrete sampler using as few as $T=48$ total steps, without distillation or further training.
♻ ☆ Know Before You Fetch: When Does Confidence-Based Retrieval-Budget Allocation Pay Off?
Zhe Dong, Fang Qin, Manish Shah
Adaptive retrieval-augmented generation uses a signal from the reader, such as its confidence when answering without retrieval (closed-book), to decide whether and how much to retrieve. We compare such methods not with always retrieving many passages but with the best simple policy under the same cap on average passages per query: a fixed depth or a random mixture of fixed depths. Policies allocate zero, one or five passages and are scored on held-out questions. Seven settings of about 2,000 questions combine TriviaQA, Natural Questions and MS MARCO, Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-32B readers, and two retrieval sources: per-question passage pools and a 3.5-million-passage Wikipedia index. Allocation by confidence could gain in three ways. It rations a scarce budget: at a cap of half a passage per query it beats the best simple policy in all seven settings, by 0.010 to 0.075 in accuracy. It avoids harmful retrieval: at caps of one passage or more, substantial gains appear only on Wikipedia, where passages often overwrite known answers, most robustly for Qwen3-8B on TriviaQA (+0.051, also under exact match and F1). It barely helps to choose depth: closed-book confidence ranks which queries a first passage helps but ranks the choice between one and five passages little better than chance, and with passage pools a fixed depth of two or three passages is as good or better. Once the probe's latency is charged, gains remain only where the gate skips retrieval for most queries; for the larger reader they also depend on the answer metric.

♻ ☆ LoongSage: A Production-Grade, High-Efficiency Agentic RL Framework for Frontier LLMs with Validated Recipes
LoongSage Team, Bowei Song, Caiwei Zhao, Chuwen Liu, Dechuang Gu, Dong Fang, Feiyu Xie, Huaiyu Li, Hui Zhang, Jian Tian, Jian Wang, Jie Zhao, Liguang Li, Maoyun Wang, Meiyan Yang, Minghan Zhu, Pai Liu, Pandeng Yao, Renbao Tian, Ru Yang, Shuai Jia, Shuai Tan, Shuailong Li, Shuliang Li, Teng Jin, Tianze Yang, Wenbo Cui, Wenjing Bai, Wenkang Wu, Xiaowei Jiang, Xiaoyi Dong, Yanpeng Wang, Yanwen Wang, Yaohui Ma, Yawen Wang, Yihang Zhang, Yingjie Yan, Zhanbiao Lian, Zhenyu Lu, Zhida Hu, Zhihui Hao, Zhihui Hu, Zhiwen Xiao, Zhiyi Gao, Zhongwei Qi, Zhuangzhuang Wu, Ziqian Liu, Ziyue Zheng
Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a critical phase in the post-training pipeline of large language models (LLMs). As the frontier of LLM capabilities shifts from static reasoning to agentic tasks--characterized by external tool invocation, multi-turn interactions, and long-horizon decision-making--existing mainstream RL frameworks have grown increasingly unwieldy, raising the overhead of adoption, usage, and debugging. To address these challenges, we present LoongSage, an agent-native, fully asynchronous RL framework with a lightweight core of roughly 14K lines of code. Built upon Megatron for distributed training and SGLang for high-throughput inference, LoongSage centralizes orchestration within a single controller process and encapsulates all GPU-resident components as Ray actors. It introduces AgentFlow, a unified execution paradigm that orchestrates both white-box and black-box agents. For asynchronous execution, it integrates sliding-window strategies that bound trajectory staleness, and employs TransferMesh, a topology-aware synchronization mechanism that routes weights after each step over the fastest transport available to each receiver. Beyond agentic RL, LoongSage incorporates a memory-efficient Teacher Manager that decouples the scaling of teacher models from GPU memory constraints, natively supporting Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation. Extensive evaluations across four complete training runs, spanning complex reinforcement learning and multi-teacher distillation, demonstrate that task rewards improve steadily while the train-inference gap is kept under continuous control, and the fully asynchronous pipeline removes synchronous idle bubbles, sustaining hardware utilization.
♻ ☆ Where Is the Cost of Third-Party API Routers in Agentic Software Development?
Donghao Fu, Jingxin Li, Xue Jiang, Yihong Dong
Third-party API routers have become a common layer that unifies access across increasingly diverse LLM providers. In coding-agent workflows, high-autonomy operation is widely adopted because it reduces interaction overhead. As a result, a third-party API router, which sits between the agent and the upstream provider, inevitably occupies the trusted path. It can inspect and modify every request and response, yet no mechanism verifies alignment between the provider's output and the repository-level actions ultimately executed by the agent. Consequently, client-side permission mechanisms may become ineffective in practice. Whether this control gap produces real, hard-to-detect effects on software development tasks remains empirically unmeasured. In this paper, we conduct an empirical study of router-side injection in coding agents, examining four intervention levels of increasing subtlety: Response Substitution (L1), Response Append (L2), LLM-Polished Injection (L3), and LLM-Polished with Distribution Alignment Injection (L4). Moreover, we develop SIDEL, a framework for trace recording, replay, injection, and defense evaluation, with a curated dataset of 400 samples. We evaluate four representative coding agents, and further evaluate whitelist-based execution control and LLM review. Router-side intervention substantially alters repository-level actions and remains difficult for existing client-side safeguards to detect. Without additional mitigations, all evaluated agents achieved a defense success rate of 0 percent across all injection levels. Client-side mitigations and reactive reviews improve resistance but do not fully restore end-to-end control, motivating provider-side output-integrity guarantees. Our code is available at https://github.com/Riyasushin/SIDEL.
♻ ☆ When Does Learning to Stop Help? Configuring Risk-Controlled Early Exit for Reasoning-Model Services
Zhe Dong, Fang Qin, Manish Shah
Early exit lowers the cost of serving a reasoning model by stopping its reasoning once a provisional answer looks final. An operator must choose between a learned stopping rule and one static token budget, and savings against full reasoning do not settle the choice. We present a procedure that decides on calibration data from the workload. It certifies both families with Learn-then-Test to one bound on the fraction of all questions that full reasoning answers correctly but early exit does not, prices provisional answers as the serving stack does, and deploys the cheapest certified candidate. Across four models and five benchmarks neither family is better throughout. At a 5% risk target the learned rule saves 10.4-17.0 percentage points more tokens than the static budget on free-form mathematics with Qwen3, with accuracy 2.4-2.9 points below full reasoning, and less on MMLU-Pro on average. Which family costs less also changes with the risk target, in 8 of 15 settings, and with the price of a provisional answer. Across three prices, committing to one family in advance gives up as much as 27.4 points on some workload, whereas selecting on calibration data falls at most 11.6 points short of the better family, on a task with only 99 calibration questions. In a vLLM engine with Qwen3-8B, mean response time fell in proportion to generated tokens. Policies served on a task other than their calibration task exceeded the risk target in 18 of 44 task pairs; recalibration on the new task restored risk control.

♻ ☆ Jetriever: Shifting Agent Memory Retrieval from Similarity to Utility
Ruizhe Li, Licheng Zhang, Mingxuan Du, Zhendong Mao
Whether a stored memory helps answer a user's question often depends on world knowledge rather than surface similarity. Jetriever replaces similarity search with full-pool utility scoring: given a query, it uses Jev, an LLM-derived System One decision model, to score every stored memory for the probability that it is useful to the answer, ranking the memory pool directly by those probabilities. On InMind's 125 indirect-query tasks, Jetriever places the gold exchange first in 84.8% of cases (versus 1.6% for BM25 and 0.0% for MiniLM with user-turn indexing) and reaches 76.0% end-to-end Application, compared with at most 16.0% across published retrieval and memory systems. A controlled 20-question comparison disentangles recognizing implicit evidence from ranking it properly: Jev and Qwen3.5-9B both recover 90% of gold exchanges within the top 50 (versus 30% for the BERT-based decision model Laya), yet at rank 1 Jev reaches 85% while Qwen3.5-9B drops to 40%. On a dimension-proportional random sample of 60 MemoryAgentBench questions, Jetriever raises the four-dimension average from 44.3% (ReFind) to 55.4%, at a mean retrieval cost of USD 0.00834 per InMind query and USD 0.03103 per MAB query. Together, these results show that effective utility retrieval hinges on combining broad world knowledge, calibrated ranking probabilities, and affordable full-pool evaluation.

♻ ☆ OracleZoom: Reference-Constrained Recursive Image Super-Resolution Beyond Ground Truth
Shubhashis Roy Dipta, Nobin Sarwar, Md Rizwan Parvez
Recursive image Super-Resolution (SR) reaches extreme magnification by feeding each prediction back into the same model, like zooming into an image again and again. Real ground truth for deep zooms is impractical to capture (a full $256\times$ target would be a 52 GB image), so predictions at deeper zooms have no supervision. Yet the last ground-truth image still contains the region that every deeper zoom magnifies. OracleZoom turns this region into a training signal. Inspired by on-policy self-distillation, the model trains on its own prediction for one recursive step ($4\times \rightarrow 16\times$), and a cross-scale loss projects each $16\times$ prediction back to the last ground truth at $4\times$. This projection can verify only coarse content, so a no-reference quality objective guides the remaining fine detail, and a latent prior stops this objective from adding hallucinated artifacts. On seven datasets, OracleZoom achieves the highest CLIPIQA at every zoom depth (mean 0.713 vs. 0.621 for Chain-of-Zoom), with the largest gains at the deepest zooms, even though training stops at $16\times$ and optimizes a different quality metric. Pooled over two ground-truth-anchored judges, this added detail comes at the same hallucination rate as Chain-of-Zoom at $16\times$ and a lower rate at $4\times$, and removing the cross-scale loss raises hallucination at $16\times$.
comment: Project page: https://dipta007.github.io/OracleZoom/
♻ ☆ FlashREINFORCE: Critic-Free Single-Rollout Asynchronous RL for Agentic Language Models
Jian Hu, Yifan Zhang, Hao Zhang, Binfeng Xu, Shaokun Zhang, Hongqing Peng, Zhiding Yu, Pavlo Molchanov, Jan Kautz, Yi Dong
Asynchronous reinforcement learning suits long-horizon agents with irregular rollout times, but group-relative updates require repeated rollouts of each prompt. However, group-based sampling limits prompt coverage at a fixed rollout budget and introduces group synchronization barriers. One rollout per prompt avoids these constraints but requires learning from stale, variable-length trajectories without a sibling baseline. We present FlashREINFORCE, to our knowledge the first critic-free single-rollout framework that scales to long asynchronous training and large models. It combines One-Batch REINFORCE, a Sequence Trust Region, and Sample-Mean Optimization, which provide prompt coverage and batch centering while controlling policy drift and length-amplified negative updates. Each component is easy to implement, and the result is their combination: a batch-mean baseline is stable under sample-mean loss, and each common alternative degrades or collapses training. FlashREINFORCE remains stable through 6,000 updates on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B at policy lag four, and through 1,450 on the 30B MoE Qwen3-30B-A3B at lag eight. Under identical loss, data, and lag it matches a strengthened group-relative baseline within 0.5 points, and with half the rollouts exceeds published synchronous baselines on Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B (38.0 versus 36.3 five-benchmark mean). On Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct it maintains tool use where GRPO stops calling tools and achieves 98.3%/96.5% seen/unseen ALFWorld success.

♻ ☆ MOLT: A Hugging Face-Native, Minimal, and Scalable Framework for Agentic Reinforcement Learning
Jian Hu, Huiying Li, Hao Zhang, Binfeng Xu, Yifan Zhang, Shaokun Zhang, Hemil Desai, Michael Demoret, Pavlo Molchanov, Jan Kautz, Yi Dong
Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) research iterates quickly on agents, rewards, and learning algorithms, while policies have grown to hundreds of billions of parameters and beyond. Existing open frameworks force a choice: those that are easy to modify do not scale to the largest models, and those that scale rely on Megatron, which needs dedicated bridge code for every model family and several times more framework code. We present MOLT, an open-source framework built for research that is Hugging Face-native, minimal, and scalable. Its code is easy to modify: the entire RL path is approximately 9.2K lines of Python with one training backend and one rollout engine, so a researcher or an AI coding agent can read it in full and edit the code that actually runs. A Hugging Face checkpoint is loaded directly into a PyTorch FSDP2 trainer with tensor, expert, and context parallelism, so a new model family needs no bridge, and the same code scales from a 4B model to a 753B one. Existing OpenAI- and Anthropic-SDK agents are trained by redirecting their client, with automatic handling of agents that rewrite their own context; training is fully asynchronous; and trajectory data stay in a distributed object store. With MOLT, a 30B mixture-of-experts tool agent trains stably for 1,464 asynchronous updates, a visual computer-use agent improves on OSWorld, asynchronous training is 2.40 times faster than synchronous training, and pipeline throughput reaches 92% of a Megatron-based stack. Its trainer, NeMo AutoModel, trains models of one to 2.8 trillion parameters.
comment: Technical report. Code: https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/labs-molt. Documentation: https://nvidia-nemo.github.io/labs-molt/guide/. Demonstration video: https://youtu.be/3DqXGFIce0o
♻ ☆ Don't Pay Attention, PLANT It: Pretraining Attention via Learning-to-Rank NeurIPS 2026
Debjyoti Saha Roy, Byron C. Wallace, Javed A. Aslam
State-of-the-art Extreme Multi-Label Text Classification models rely on multi-label attention to focus on key tokens in input text, but learning good attention weights is challenging. We introduce PLANT (Pretrained and Leveraged AtteNTion), a plug-and-play strategy for initializing the label-attention module that operates on top of an LLM's output representations. PLANT works by planting label-specific attention using a Learning-to-Rank model trained on mutual information gain, before end-to-end fine-tuning. This architecture-agnostic approach integrates with LLM backbones (e.g., Mistral-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, DeepSeek-V3, and Phi-3-mini). PLANT outperforms SOTA methods across tasks like ICD coding, legal topic classification, and content recommendation, and improves fine-tuned LLMs in controlled comparisons outside the medical domain. Gains are largest on rare labels, although a training-free LLM extraction baseline remains competitive on rarely seen labels. Ablation studies confirm that attention initialization is a key driver of these gains. We make our code available at https://github.com/deb-official/plant
comment: Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. v5 is the camera-ready version, revised after the NeurIPS reviews: new comparisons with fine-tuned LLMs outside the medical domain and with zero- and few-shot LLM baselines. 41 pages, 5 figures, 20 tables. Code: https://github.com/deb-official/plant
♻ ☆ Risk-Calibrated Intent Routing for Conversational AI via Signed Lexical Evidence
Yezhou Cheng, Zehua Yang, Bojun Lin
Conversational AI has become widely used as advances in language models let people find information, get writing help, and ask for practical guidance in their own words. When an assistant handles specific tasks, it must infer what the user wants and decide whether to act on that prediction. A sentence classifier's confidence can miss clues in the wording. We combine the gap between the classifier's two highest scores with signed lexical evidence, a score based on words and short character patterns. The score is positive when those patterns favor the predicted intent and negative when they favor another. We use separate calibration data and a statistical upper bound on the error rate to set the minimum confidence required to accept a request. Across ten runs per dataset, our method reduces the area under the risk-coverage curve, which summarizes error as more requests are accepted, by 15.8%, 15.1%, and 11.8% on BANKING77, CLINC150, and HWU64, respectively, compared with a confidence model that uses only sentence classifier scores. At a 5% error target, the share of accepted requests rises by 1.83 percentage points on BANKING77 and 5.14 points on HWU64. CLINC150 already has near-full coverage at that target. At a 2% target, calibration finds a rule that accepts some requests in all 30 runs. Our contribution is a two-feature confidence model that improves routing by measuring how strongly the wording supports or challenges the original intent prediction.
♻ ☆ Meta$^n$: Recursive Self-Improvement through Emergent Depth NeurIPS 2026
Zae Myung Kim, Young-Jun Lee, Seungyeon Jwa, Dongyeop Kang
Self-improving LLM agents refine answers, not the process that produces those answers. Systems that add a meta-level hold that level fixed, and those that edit themselves must leave part of their own editing machinery untouched to stay stable, capping the meta-depth they realize at roughly two. We present Meta$^n$, which keeps the meta-operation fixed and recurses on its input instead. That operation, $Ω$, is applied repeatedly to its own products, reading the traces of the solver stack below together with the code that produced them, then writing the next layer as a strategic pre-process and a library of callable helpers. Because $Ω$ never changes, it cannot destabilize the system, and because its input strictly grows, each layer reasons from a higher vantage than the last. Depth is set by convergence rather than fixed in advance, and an evolutionary archive searches over layer chains. Across two backbones, Meta$^n$ outperforms prior self-improving agents on all eight benchmark families. The sharpest case is ARC-AGI-2, built to resist skill memorization, where it alone scores above zero. Ablations indicate that most of the gain from recursion comes from the conditioning each layer passes to the next, and distinct layer roles emerge with depth although no prompt prescribes them. Code available at https://github.com/minnesotanlp/meta-n
comment: Accepted to NeurIPS 2026
♻ ☆ DV-Lens: Revealing the Functional Organization of Language Model Parameters
Chenhang Cui, Jian Yu, Jiani Luo, Shuyi Miao, Dingrui Liu, Rui Huang, Xiaohao Liu, Fei Shen, An Zhang, Tat-Seng Chua
Understanding parameter functions helps elucidate the internal mechanisms of large language models (LLMs). However, how to connect parameters from different modules to verifiable output effects and further characterize the relationship between their functional organization and model capability remains to be explored. To this end, we introduce the downstream vocabulary lens (DV-Lens), a parameter-level interpretability framework that links native parameter directions to their downstream vocabulary responses. Specifically, we first estimate module-specific downstream Jacobians over a reference prompt set for attention query, key, value, and output (Q/K/V/O) projections and feed-forward networks (FFNs). Second, we use these mappings to project native parameter columns into the final vocabulary space, obtaining signed readouts that characterize their average local output responses. Third, we group parameter columns by their vocabulary readouts and introduce downstream vocabulary complexity (DV-Complexity), which quantifies within-group structural variation using normalized reconstruction residuals of the original weights. At the parameter level, randomized controls and finite-difference tests show that DV-Lens readouts capture non-random vocabulary structure and predict local logit changes with 98.0% coordinate-orientation agreement across 720 cases from nine models. These readouts further guide parameter ablation, steering, and swapping across 21 models, shifting target-token probabilities in the predicted directions under controlled conditions. At the model level, the joint-parameter score of DV-Complexity achieves a Spearman correlation of 0.904 with benchmark-based capability rankings across 48 language models. Together, these results provide intervention-based evidence for DV-Lens interpretations and reveal an association between DV-Complexity and model capability.
♻ ☆ SANTA++: Sampling Attention through Representative Keys
Kyle Lee, Christian Z. Pratt, Ruoyu Fang, Heekyung Lee, Avinash Lohitsa, Ryan Modafe, Kerem Y. Camsari
Attention often concentrates on a small subset of tokens in the context, but which subset matters changes from one query to the next. To exploit this changing structure, we introduce SANTA++, a training-free stochastic attention method that uses representative keys for memory-efficient selection without scanning the entire key-value (KV) cache. Cached keys are organized into teams, and the query scores one representative from each team to decide which teams to sample. We compute exact attention scores within the sampled teams and reweight each team's contribution by the inverse of its inclusion probability. This importance sampling correction estimates attention over the full cache, with a sampling budget that lets us trade memory reads for accuracy. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at 32K context, contiguous SANTA++ at S=1024 retains 95-98% of dense aggregate scores on LongBench v2, HELMET RAG, and RULER using 28-31% logical KV access. On RULER, SANTA++ outperforms Quest across the tested accuracy-access sweep, using 48.7% fewer KV reads at approximately 79% accuracy. It also outperforms MagicPIG at low access. At S=1024, our Triton implementation gives a 1.38x attention-operator speedup over the Flash backend of PyTorch scaled dot-product attention (Flash SDPA) in FP16. At batch eight, full-model decoding is 1.23x faster than dense Flash SDPA with matched INT4 model weights and FP16 KV-cache precision on an RTX 5090 Laptop GPU. Our kernels are available at https://github.com/OPUSLab/santapp-kernel-demo.

☆ Ambient LLMs: Data-Efficient Supervised Fine-tuning via Temperature Matching
Giannis Daras, Marios Papachristou, Adam Klivans, Antonio Torralba, Constantinos Daskalakis
Collecting large-scale, diverse, and high-quality post-training datasets for autoregressive language models is challenging. Consequently, Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) is typically applied to datasets significantly smaller than those used for pre-training, which leads to overfitting and the catastrophic forgetting of the tails of the data distribution. To prevent this collapse, we introduce Ambient SFT, a principled framework for leveraging imperfect predictions to augment the fine-tuning set. In our method, a "frozen" version of the pre-trained model serves as a noisy teacher that the fine-tuned model must match at a coarse level. In particular, we require that the high-temperature versions of the conditional distributions produced by the post-training model should match the high-temperature versions of the pre-training ones. The temperature hyperparameter controls the level of trust in the pre-trained model, with the limit $T\to \infty$ reverting back to regular SFT and $T=1$ to knowledge distillation. We demonstrate that our approach outperforms standard SFT across domain adaptation, instruction-tuning, and reasoning, leading for up to $21\%$ average relative improvement to standard SFT.
