♻ ☆ Localized LoRA-MoE: Mixture of Low-Rank Experts With Adaptive Routing
Babak Barazandeh, Subhabrata Majumdar, Vinay Prithyani, George Michailidis
Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods such as LoRA adapt large models through a single low-rank update. Such updates are susceptible to gradient interference: when a training stream interleaves multiple operational contexts, conflicting updates to shared adapter weights collapse the adapter into an unspecialized average. While recent methods introduce block-wise isolation to remedy this issue, their topologies are static and cannot adapt to context switching or localized sensor degradation. In this work, we introduce Localized LoRA-MoE, a framework that combines localized spatial blocking with context-conditioned expert routing. We propose and evaluate two architectural variants: Block-Wise LoRA-MoE (centralized routing), which modulates the entire structural grid through a single context-conditioned gate, and Cell-Wise LoRA-MoE (decentralized routing), which assigns an independent gate to every coordinate cell of the matrix grid. Across three structured transformation benchmarks--a high-dimensional SVD matrix simulation, context-switched transformations over real tabular data, and spatial reconstruction under simulated sensor occlusion--both variants outperform static baselines under comparable adapter budgets. Both variants perform similarly when context shifts the grid uniformly, while cell-wise routing is strictly better when contexts are spatially heterogeneous. When fine-tuning a language model on six interleaved GLUE tasks, task-conditioned routing at a fixed adapter budget outperforms LoRA and static localized adapters, matching per-task adapters with 6x budgets. At decoder scale on Qwen2.5-1.5B, both routed variants outperform static baselines across eight commonsense benchmarks, with cell-wise routing achieving +3.9 points over LoRA at matched active capacity.
♻ ☆ Mingbird: A Local-First Agent Harness Enabling Small Open Models to Complete Real Tasks
Hao Wang, Ting Huang
Small open-weight language models now run on a laptop, but they fail at multi-step work in ways a large model does not: they stop with the files missing, repeat one action until the budget ends, and report success without checking. A configuration-surface audit finds four defects that the 625-test suite of v3.1.1 missed, each because a test checked a property adjacent to the one that broke. A ten-task probe suite gives each mechanism a reason to fire and reports where the change reaches the artifact, including one mechanism that cuts the signal it protects. Disabling five of six completion-time checks costs 0.152 of the artifact score on eighteen held-out tasks, three replications per cell (95% CI [+0.064, +0.246], Holm p = 0.014). On a 288-cell matrix the harness reaches 0.886 against 0.631, 0.479 and 0.405 for three open scaffolds, and on τ 2 -bench it ranks first or tied first in all three domains against the arms evaluated.Tasks, scorer, source and every per-cell result are public.

♻ ☆ In-Context Optimization for Retrieval-Augmented Generation: A Gradient-Descent Perspective NIPS 2026
Mingchen Li, Jiatan Huang, Chuxu Zhang, Liang Zhao, Hong Yu
In-context learning has recently been linked to implicit gradient descent in linear self-attention models, suggesting that context can induce a forward-pass update. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) also relies on context, but retrieved documents are usually treated as static evidence rather than signals for adaptation. We study RAG as an in-context optimization process. First, we show that one linear self-attention layer can implement one gradient-descent step on a unified linearized RAG objective covering both projection-based and dot-product retrieval interfaces. This gives an exact regime where retrieval-augmented prediction and in-context optimization coincide. We use this result not as a literal model of LLM computation, but as a guide for adapting the interaction between queries and retrieved evidence. We then test the boundary of this correspondence: it remains stable under controlled linear extensions, but becomes feature-distribution dependent under nonlinear architectures. Finally, we turn this view into a lightweight method for frozen RAG LLMs. The method keeps the retriever and backbone fixed, and predicts a context-conditioned update to a generator-side evidence-use interface. Across seven QA benchmarks, two retrievers, and two frozen LLM backbones, this forward-only update improves a shared-interface baseline, transfers to held-out tasks, and approaches test-time gradient adaptation at much lower per-query cost.

♻ ☆ RICE-PO: Turning Retrieval Interactions into Credit Signals for Reasoning Agents NIPS 2026
Mingchen Li, Hansi Zeng, Zhuo Qian, Jiatan Huang, Sunjae Kwon, Hamed Zamani, Hong Yu
Retrieval is increasingly moving from one-shot matching toward interactive reasoning, where language agents iteratively inspect evidence, reformulate queries, and search again. Training such agents raises a credit-assignment challenge: executable actions such as queries or summaries can be directly evaluated by the retriever, while latent reasoning steps are not directly observable and only affect future executable actions. This asymmetry makes outcome-level reward assignment unreliable, as the same final reward may credit reasoning steps that did not actually shape retrieval success. We propose RICE-PO, a critic-free policy optimization framework that converts retrieval interactions into localized learning signals. RICE-PO selects high-uncertainty executable actions as anchors, evaluates local counterfactual branches using retrieval metrics, and propagates credit to latent reasoning steps only when reasoning-to-action influence is strong and future residual effects are stable. On BRIGHT and BEIR, RICE-PO consistently outperforms prompt-based agents and group-based RL baselines under the same retriever setting. These results show that the structure of agent-environment interaction itself can provide useful supervision for training reasoning-based retrieval agents.

♻ ☆ MamaReason: Unfinished Reasoning and Answer Recovery in Clinical Language Models
Thanni Adewuyi, Adepoju Jeremiah Moyondafoluwa, Dorcas Amapu, Dieko Whenu, Sylvia Essessien, Sandra Oko-Oba, Pelumi Onafadeji, Lily Aimas-Love, Samuel Omolaiye, AbdulQahhar Okenla, Abiodun Adereni
Answer continuation often returns a diagnosis already stated in unfinished clinical reasoning. We study this behavior in MamaReason, adapted from dense Gemma 4 31B, on MamaBench's 217 paired maternal and child-health presentations. An explicit-commitment extractor identifies a diagnosis in 75 of 94 failed RL first passes and 75 of 93 failed SFT first passes. The continuation matches that diagnosis in 67/75 RL cases and 58/75 SFT cases, using diagnosis strings alone. Reading these commitments without additional generation recovers 30 of the 40 RL pairs restored by continuation under the archived correctness labels. Recurrence controls distinguish routine mentions from repeated answer selection. An offline second-commitment trigger measures the tradeoff between earlier readout and paired accuracy. The resulting decomposition separates diagnostic content already present in a trace, completion of an accepted answer, and paired diagnostic correctness. For clinical language-model evaluation, these are distinct outcomes that become visible when the same reasoning trace is examined through multiple readouts.
♻ ☆ Asymmetric Discourse Homogenization on Reddit Was a Text-Length Artifact: A Reanalysis
Fengming Liu
In the first version of this paper I reported that conservative Reddit users began writing more alike after ChatGPT was released while progressive users did not. That result came from a similarity measure that rises and falls with comment length. I redo every analysis of the original paper on all 6,056,593 comments with a sentence encoder and a dispersion measure computed within length strata, and the conservative break goes away. The two camps show no reliable difference in how within-group similarity changed after late 2022, and the ChatGPT date behaves like any of the more than two thousand other cutoffs I test. The matched flair comparison, the cumulative AI index, the stayer panel and the entropy signature each lose their support once length is held fixed. The one change that survives is older. Left and right comments drew closer to each other between 2019 and 2022, and that convergence was complete before ChatGPT arrived. I end with what this implies for studies that measure homogenization with embeddings.

♻ ☆ Continued Training of Continuous Language Flows with Token Identity Squared Supervision
Jiawei Jiang, Zhaoxiang Zang, Zhengtao Yu
Diffusion and flow models have recently been extended to language modeling, generating text through continuous denoising in token embedding spaces. Pretrained models provide token representations and contextual prediction capabilities, but improving generation with limited additional training requires an appropriate connection between supervision and continuous generation. When continuous predictions are formed by weighting token embeddings with predicted probabilities, different probability distributions can yield the same vector and are therefore indistinguishable under a continuous reconstruction objective based on mean squared error (MSE). We propose Token Identity Brier Flow (TIBF) to apply squared supervision directly to token identities. TIBF fixes token embeddings, combines network scores with token-matching scores under a Gaussian observation model, supervises token probabilities using the Brier loss, and uses probability-weighted embeddings for continuous generation. We show that, for fixed token embeddings, the squared error in token posterior prediction controls the squared errors of the continuous denoising prediction and the flow velocity. A risk decomposition separates uncertainty inherent in the observation from posterior fitting error, motivating continued training that preserves pretrained embeddings and focuses on probability prediction. From common pretrained initializations and under matched continuation budgets, TIBF achieves mean Gen.PPL values of 34.87 on OWT and 80.08 on LM1B, outperforming continuous-MSE and CE controls. Further evaluations show improvements across multiple sampling budgets. Code is available at https://github.com/Jiangjiawei-kust/TIBF.
comment: 49 pages, including appendices; 7 figures. Code: https://github.com/Jiangjiawei-kust/TIBF
♻ ☆ RL Post-Training Builds Compositional Reasoning Strategies NeurIPS 2026
Azwar Abdulsalam, Nishil Patel, Andrew Saxe
Does RL post-training merely amplify primitive skills already latent in a base model, or can it compose primitive skills into new higher-level strategies? We study this question in a fully observable rewrite-grammar environment where the pretraining distribution is known and every generated rewrite can be audited. A Transformer is pretrained on primitive symbol-rewrite chains and post-trained on a Trace-based reasoning task with only a binary final-answer reward. RL solves held-out problems that remain rarely solved by the pretrained model even under much larger sampling budgets, while rejection fine-tuning improves early but plateaus. Trace analysis shows that RL reorganizes primitive competence through a phased compositional mechanism: it first strengthens primitive reductions, then discovers valid composed procedures. These include sequential compositions, which collapse ordered chains of primitive contractions, and parallel compositions, which combine independent primitive contractions in a single step. The composed procedures are not isolated samples; they are reused and consolidated into a stable repertoire. Comparing RL with rejection fine-tuning shows that the key difference is not exploration volume but selectivity: RFT produces many shortcut-like rewrites, much of them invalid, whereas RL concentrates exploration into valid reusable structure. Pretraining ablations show that the emergence of compositional strategies is gated not by primitive exposure alone, but by whether pretraining organizes primitive competence into reduction procedures that RL can later compress. The base model provides weak procedural ingredients; RL builds them into reliable higher-level strategies.

♻ ☆ Evidence Attribution in Visual Document Understanding without Coordinates or Region Labels EACL 2027
Zhuchenyang Liu, Yao Zhang, Yu Xiao
Reliable visual document understanding requires a model to attribute each answer to the evidence regions that support it. Recent benchmarks and systems express this step through a coordinate interface: the model outputs the coordinates of bounding boxes that mark the evidence regions in the document. Under this interface, vision-language models often fail to identify the right regions even when the answer is correct, a failure known as Attribution Hallucination. We present a study that investigates whether this failure is partially limited by what the model can express through coordinates. On a verified bilingual CiteVQA subset, we compare the coordinate interface with a language interface in which the model outputs only text, quoting its evidence verbatim, and a multimodal retriever returns the location of each quote as a page region proposed by a layout parser (tables and figures are quoted through their captions or notes); the comparison is repeated over six open vision-language models. Compared with the coordinate interface, evidence recall rises from at most 8 points to between 26 and 47 and the hallucination rate roughly halves, with little change in answer quality. Building on this comparison, we use the same quote-and-retrieve pipeline as a training scaffold: because region-level evidence labels are expensive to collect for long documents, we introduce a GRPO recipe whose reward is a judge's reading of the gold answer and crops of the retrieved regions, training the model to quote better evidence without any region labels and raising an 8B backbone's strict attributed accuracy from 22.4 to 33.8. These findings indicate a practical path to improve attribution without a coordinate interface and without costly region-level supervision.

♻ ☆ Stress-Testing Emotional Support Models: Moving from Homogeneous to Diverse Help Seekers ACL 2026
Chaewon Heo, Cheyon Jin, Yohan Jo
As emotional support chatbots have recently gained significant traction across both research and industry, a common evaluation strategy has emerged: use help-seeker simulators to interact with supporter chatbots. However, current simulators suffer from two critical limitations: (1) they fail to capture the behavioral diversity of real-world seekers, often portraying them as overly cooperative, and (2) they lack the controllability required to simulate specific seeker profiles. To address these challenges, we present a controllable seeker simulator driven by nine psychological and linguistic features that underpin seeker behavior. Using authentic Reddit conversations, we train our model via a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture, which effectively differentiates diverse seeker behaviors into specialized parameter subspaces, thereby enhancing fine-grained controllability. Our simulator achieves superior profile adherence and behavioral diversity compared to existing approaches. Furthermore, evaluating 7 prominent supporter models with our system uncovers previously obscured performance degradations. These findings underscore the utility of our framework in providing a more faithful and stress-tested evaluation for emotional support chatbots.

♻ ☆ Thinking Is Not Telling: Information Disclosure in User-Service LLM Agents
Jiatong Li, Changdae Oh, Hyeong Kyu Choi, Jindong Wang, Sharon Li
User-engaged LLM agents increasingly operate in service scenarios where task success depends on coordination between the agent, the user, and a stateful environment. In such interactions, the agent often has access to rich information that the user does not observe, such as task policies, tool results, and environment states. This makes agent-user communication a central component of task completion. In this work, we study a communication-level failure mode of user-service agents: internal reasoning is counterproductive for the interactive information disclosure. We introduce information disclosure as a measurable communication mechanism, covering state corrections, available options, constraints, consequences, and proactively retrieved environment information. Motivated by recent advances in LLM reasoning, we evaluate and analyse information disclosure under a controlled setting of agents equipped with a "think" tool, which allows agents to reason internally before responding or acting. Across five models and three user-service scenarios, we first show that enforced thinking has unstable utility. We then analyse response scaling under different think efforts and find that more thinking does not reliably translate into richer information disclosure. Using turn-level counterfactual intervention, we show that injecting golden decision-relevant information significantly intervenes agents to correct action trajectories, indicating that missing disclosure can causally affect the agent's immediate downstream behaviour. We further find that failed trajectories most often lack state corrections and constraints, and that a lightweight disclosure-aware prompt effectively mitigates degradation. These results suggest that the key challenge is not simply making agents think more, but ensuring that reasoning and information are externalised as timely information disclosure.

♻ ☆ When Detection Does Not Guarantee Resistance: Reasoning and Poisoned Context in RAG
Mehrdad Ghassabi, Audrina Ebrahimi, Sadra Hakim, Hamidreza Baradaran Kashani, Zahra Zojaji, Amin Milani Fard
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) exposes large language models to knowledge-poisoning attacks, where misinformation injected into retrieved documents can influence model outputs. Prior work has shown that models may detect contradictory evidence yet still allow it to influence their responses, revealing a gap between monitoring and control. We investigate whether deliberative reasoning changes this relationship. Because attack success and poison detection alone do not reveal whether detected poison continues to influence the final answer, we use two complementary measures: Cordon Rate, the probability that a model detects poison and nevertheless produces a poison-aligned answer, conditioned on a non-poison-aligned no-RAG response; and Leakage Rate, the probability that poisoned context influences the answer despite an explicit instruction to ignore retrieved documents. Across 200 SciFact questions, we compare reasoning-disabled and reasoning-enabled configurations of DeepSeek-V4-Flash and Qwen3.6-Plus. For DeepSeek-V4-Flash, reasoning reduces Cordon Rate from 0.205 to 0.075 and Leakage Rate from 0.235 to 0.140, despite increasing Attack Success Rate from 0.233 to 0.298 and decreasing Poison Detection Rate from 0.965 to 0.665. Qwen3.6-Plus shows the same qualitative pattern. These results demonstrate that poison detection and downstream influence capture distinct aspects of poisoning behavior.
♻ ☆ Illusory Truth or Salient Anomaly? Model-Dependent Responses to Repeated Poisoned Passages in RAG-Style Contexts
Mehrdad Ghassabi, Audrina Ebrahimi, Sadra Hakim, Hamidreza Baradaran Kashani, Zahra Zojaji, Amin Milani Fard
Repeated passages in retrieved evidence may influence how language models identify misinformation, even when those passages restate a single underlying claim rather than provide independent support. We compare four instruction-tuned models on 200 claims from the BEIR SciFact test split using paired, fixed seven-document contexts. The minority condition contains one poisoned document and six non-target documents, whereas the majority condition contains the original poisoned document, five paraphrases, and one non-target document. Poison detection rates increase from 45.5% to 66.0% for Apertus-8B and from 4.0% to 15.5% for Aya-Expanse-8B, but decrease from 80.0% to 47.5% for Mistral-Nemo and from 25.0% to 9.5% for Gemma-3-12B-IT. All four paired comparisons are statistically significant. These contrasting results suggest that repeated poisoned evidence does not affect all models in the same way. One possible interpretation is that repetition makes the poisoned claim more conspicuous to some models, potentially facilitating its detection, while other models may treat repeated formulations as reinforcing evidence, potentially reducing their ability to identify the poisoned claim. Although these interpretations require further investigation, the findings highlight the importance of model-dependent responses to repeated evidence in retrieval-augmented settings.
♻ ☆ Clustering in pure-attention hardmax transformers and its role in sentiment analysis SC
Albert Alcalde, Giovanni Fantuzzi, Enrique Zuazua
Transformers are extremely successful machine learning models whose mathematical properties remain poorly understood. Here, we rigorously characterize the behavior of transformers with hardmax self-attention and normalization sublayers as the number of layers tends to infinity. By viewing such transformers as discrete-time dynamical systems describing the evolution of points in a Euclidean space, and thanks to a geometric interpretation of the self-attention mechanism based on hyperplane separation, we show that the transformer inputs asymptotically converge to a clustered equilibrium determined by special points called \textit{leaders}. We then leverage this theoretical understanding to solve sentiment analysis problems from language processing using a fully interpretable transformer model, which effectively captures `context' by clustering meaningless words around leader words carrying the most meaning. Finally, we outline remaining challenges to bridge the gap between the mathematical analysis of transformers and their real-life implementation.

♻ ☆ Corralling AI Risks with Grassroots Contracts
Ehud Shapiro
AI's inflection point is a fountain of risks for digital platforms, as those in control of global platforms cede control of the algorithms, membership and content to AI: (i) AI control of global platforms used by the majority of humanity; (ii) unaccountable AI agents operating anonymously, possibly masquerading as humans, serving unknown masters with unknown goals; and (iii) unattributed deepfakes generated by anonymous AI, which people cannot distinguish from genuine content. Here, we propose to corral these AI risks with grassroots contracts, a digital incarnation of a two-centuries old notion of Pierre-Joseph Proudhon: a social contract that can be freely adopted by any group of people, where groups bound by the same contract can union by mutual consent, possibly into a single global union. Grassroots contracts are realised by grassroots platforms, person-to-person platforms owned, operated and governed by their participants. We developed, with AI, a methodology to compile grassroots contracts to working smartphone apps. We present grassroots contracts for a social graph with accountable AI agents and for a social network with digital provenance, and working smartphone apps that implement the two, and show how they corral these risks: (i) grassroots platforms are not controlled by a third party who may cede control to AI; (ii) AI agents are introduced with accountability: similarly to cars, their manufacturers are accountable for manufacturing defects and their operators for reckless operation; and (iii) every item carries the signatures of its author and of every agent that forwarded it, so creators and forwarders of harmful content are accountable and culpable. Much work remains for grassroots apps to be competitive with incumbent global platforms; but perhaps now this could be achieved with some AI-months rather than with incalculably-many person-years.
♻ ☆ AdaRSI: On-Demand Recursive Self-Improvement for Embodied Agents
Peidong Wang, Ying Chang, Zhiming Ma, Lirong Che, Zihao Liu, Xiaocui Yang, Shi Feng, Daqi Liu, Yuzhe Yang, Xu Cao, Chuang Wang, Jian Su, Daling Wang
Failures in long-horizon embodied tasks can arise from how a vision-language-action model (VLA) is called or from its execution capabilities. We introduce AdaRSI, an on-demand recursive self-improvement framework in which a coding agent first refines its tools and procedures, then adapts sub-steps that remain unreliable after testing alternative instructions and approaches. The agent collects demonstrations, tests their utility through temporary fine-tuning probes, and pools accepted data across tasks to train a specialist VLA that works alongside the original executor. At the end of each development round, its records are summarized into shared experience that guides subsequent diagnosis and data collection. On unseen composite tasks in RoboCasa365, AdaRSI raises success from 4.6% to 13.3%. On perturbed LIBERO-Pro 2.0 tasks, success rises from 32.9% to 49.1%. On RoboCasa365's Composite-Unseen suite, it achieves higher success than adapting every manipulation sub-step, using 34% of the GPU time and 36% of the demonstrations. On a real robot, the agent autonomously collects demonstrations and adapts the executor to recover fallen bottles, improving task completion.
♻ ☆ MemT-1.0: Towards Memory-Native Evolution
Zhiyu Li, Zeyu Zhang, Linyun Liu, Yiwei Bu, Chenyang Xi, Xichong Zhang, Yi Yu, Yu Yu, Zhichen Liu, Yuxiao Yi, Chunyu Li, Haoran Liu, Zhenkai Ding, Le Zhou, Juncheng Zhang, Boyu Chen, Zehao Lin, Guorui Li, Renyu Fu, Ji Li, Youyang Xia, Jiajun Shen, Harvey Li, Jinsong Zhou, Helan Hu, Tong Shen, Tianxiao Xu, Chenye Han, Weijie Li, Zhongquan Zhou, Hongming Xu, Yuekeng Li, Erenkai Zhu, Peng Yu, Wenhao Li, Jingyi Kang, Xixuan Hao, Xuzhou Zhu, Can Chen, Jiawei Liu, Chen Jiang, Tao Jiang, Fangwei Xiang, Zepeng Yao, Kai Chen, Guang Yang, Mingfei Lu, Chunhui Li, Zhengxuan Jiang, Bo Tang, Shiyu Zhan, Yihang Sun, Jie Chu, Wenjun Xiong, Zhenxiang Hua, Chen Lin, Detong Su, Dongyu Deng, Qiuping Ding, Guofeng Ding, Si Chen, Guohua Wen, Xu Chen, Zhi-Qin John Xu, Ying-Cong Chen, Ningyu Zhang, Haofen Wang, Muning Wen, Haotian Chen, Wentao Zhang, Xuanhe Zhou, Rita Zhang, Junchi Yan, Feiyu Xiong
Memory-native evolution requires making memory an intrinsic capability of foundation models, enabling them to continuously accumulate and utilize information. To this end, we introduce MemT-1.0, a new architecture of memory foundation models that internalize memory as part of model computation. Building on Metis, MemT-1.0 employs Gated DeltaNet-2 with a stable token-level memory update mechanism within each turn. We further introduce Mixture-of-Storage to scale native memory capacity. Following a deliberate backbone preparation, we develop a two-stage training pipeline consisting of continued pre-training and post-training, supported by 34B tokens of training data and efficient training and inference infrastructure. At a history length of 128K tokens, MemT-1.0-Nano achieves approximately $1.86\times$ end-to-end inference speedup and a $66\times$ reduction in persistent storage relative to full context KV cache. By internalizing retrieved procedural experience, MemT-1.0 improves task success on agentic tasks, providing a practical step toward memory-native evolution.

♻ ☆ ToolFlood: Beyond Selection -- Hiding Valid Tools from LLM Agents via Semantic Covering EACL 2027
Hussein Jawad, Nicolas J-B Brunel
Large Language Model (LLM) agents increasingly use external tools for complex tasks and rely on embedding-based retrieval to select a small top-$k$ subset for reasoning. Prior work on attacking these systems competes for tool selection, assuming benign tools remain in the retrieved set; the robustness of the retrieval stage that produces this set is underexplored. This paper introduces ToolFlood, a retrieval-layer attack on tool-augmented LLM agents. Rather than altering which tool is chosen after retrieval, ToolFlood attacks retrieval itself: it injects a few attacker-controlled tools whose metadata is positioned to exploit the geometry of embedding space, so that these tools semantically span many user queries, dominate the top-k results, and push all benign tools out of the agent's context. ToolFlood uses a two-phase adversarial tool generation strategy. It first samples subsets of target queries and uses an LLM to iteratively generate diverse tool names and descriptions. It then runs an iterative greedy selection that chooses tools maximizing coverage of remaining queries in embedding space under a cosine-distance threshold, stopping when all queries are covered or a budget is reached. We provide theoretical intuition of retrieval saturation and show on a large catalog (ToolBench) that ToolFlood achieves up to a 95\% attack success rate with a ~1\% injection rate. The code is available at: https://github.com/as1-prog/ToolFlood

♻ ☆ Multi-Label Agreement: A Flexible Framework for Inter-Annotator Reliability
Tim Menzner, Jochen L. Leidner
Reliable evaluation of inter-annotator agreement (IAA) is important for assessing the consistency of annotation processes. While established IAA metrics such as Cohen's kappa, Fleiss' kappa, and Krippendorff's alpha are commonly applied in single-label settings, multi-label tasks require additional decisions about how full label sets, joint selections, and joint omissions should be compared. In this work, we present Multi-Label Agreement (MLA), a flexible framework for evaluating agreement in multi-label annotation tasks. MLA preserves the full binary label vectors assigned to each item and uses average pairwise Hamming agreement, thereby accounting for both joint selections and joint omissions. Its pooled-marginal form is not a mathematically independent coefficient: it is a chance-disagreement-weighted average of label-wise Fleiss/Scott-type coefficients and is closely related to Krippendorff's alpha with normalized Hamming distance. The framework supports pooled-marginal, Gwet AC1, and fixed-baseline normalizations, yields item-level, label-level, and corpus-level results, and includes a conditional minimum-agreement diagnostic. Through a worked example and Monte Carlo simulations, we show how the resulting values depend on label prevalence, the declared label inventory, and the selected model of expected agreement. We also provide a Python implementation. MLA thus offers a transparent and interpretable framework for multi-label agreement analysis without claiming that one normalization is universally preferable.
♻ ☆ PSM: Prompt Sensitivity Minimization via LLM-Guided Black-Box Optimization AAAI 2026
Hussein Jawad, Nicolas J-B Brunel
System prompts are critical for guiding the behavior of Large Language Models (LLMs), yet they often contain proprietary logic or sensitive information, making them a prime target for extraction attacks. Adversarial queries can successfully elicit these hidden instructions, posing significant security and privacy risks. Existing defense mechanisms frequently rely on heuristics, incur substantial computational overhead, or are inapplicable to models accessed via black-box APIs. This paper introduces a novel framework for hardening system prompts through shield appending, a lightweight approach that adds a protective textual layer to the original prompt. Our core contribution is the formalization of prompt hardening as a utility-constrained optimization problem. We leverage an LLM-as-optimizer to search the space of possible SHIELDs, seeking to minimize a leakage metric derived from a suite of adversarial attacks, while simultaneously preserving task utility above a specified threshold, measured by semantic fidelity to baseline outputs. This black-box, optimization-driven methodology is lightweight and practical, requiring only API access to the target and optimizer LLMs. We demonstrate empirically that our optimized SHIELDs significantly reduce prompt leakage against a comprehensive set of extraction attacks, outperforming established baseline defenses without compromising the model's intended functionality. Our work presents a paradigm for developing robust, utility-aware defenses in the escalating landscape of LLM security. The code is made public on the following link: https://github.com/psm-defense/psm

♻ ☆ Uncertainty Quantification for Large Language Diffusion Models NeurIPS 2026
Artem Vazhentsev, Vladislav Smirnov, David Li, Maxim Panov, Timothy Baldwin, Artem Shelmanov
Large Language Diffusion Models (LLDMs) are emerging as an alternative to autoregressive models, offering faster inference through higher parallelism. Similar to autoregressive LLMs, they remain prone to hallucinations, making reliable uncertainty quantification (UQ) crucial for safe deployment. However, existing UQ methods are fundamentally misaligned with this new paradigm: they assume autoregressive factorization or use expensive repeated sampling, negating the efficiency of LLDMs. In this work, we present the first systematic study of UQ for LLDMs and propose lightweight, zero-shot uncertainty signals derived from the iterative denoising process, leveraging intermediate generations, token remasking dynamics, and denoising complexity. We further adapt a state-of-the-art UQ method to LLDMs by combining masked diffusion likelihoods with trajectory-based semantic dissimilarity. We prove that expected trajectory dissimilarity lower bounds the masked diffusion training objective, which motivates its usage as an uncertainty score. Comprehensive experiments across three tasks, eight datasets, and four models show that our method achieves a great cost-performance trade-off: it approaches the strongest sampling-based baselines while incurring up to 100x lower computational overhead. Our work demonstrates that LLDMs can deliver both fast inference and reliable hallucination detection simultaneously.

♻ ☆ Arabic Safety Alignment as Selective Refusal: An Empirical Study of SFT, DPO, and Guard Calibration
Mohamad Zbib, Mohamad Bazzi, Ammar Mohanna
Arabic large language models must refuse harmful prompts without over-refusing benign or sensitive prompts, yet a single refusal rate hides this trade-off. We evaluate it using benign refusal B and harmful-prompt refusal H, where H measures refusal rather than harmful compliance. Across five Arabic-capable models and 130 runs on the full human-written AraSafe set, refusal-only supervised fine-tuning (SFT) collapses toward blanket refusal, whereas selected mixed-SFT configurations reach H = 90% to 93% at B = 14% to 23%; four selected configurations exceed the H = 90% target in all three runs, while Fanar does so in two of three. Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and inference guards change B and H differently across models rather than acting as uniform upgrades. In a blinded 300-response audit, annotator binary-refusal agreement is 89.0% (kappa = 0.78); Qwen3Guard and Aya Expanse 32B reach 88.7% and 91.0% accuracy, respectively, with no conclusive paired difference. Selected SFT raises H on Arabizi for all five models, but none reaches 90%, showing only partial transfer from Modern Standard Arabic. Overall, the results support model-specific operating-point selection: set a deployment target and retain only interventions that improve it.

♻ ☆ Visual Contextual Attack: Jailbreaking MLLMs with Image-Driven Context Injection
Ziqi Miao, Yi Ding, Chen Qian, Yongyao Wang, Zaoying Huang, Yuan Xiong, Ken Deng, Lijun Li, Jing Shao
With the emergence of strong vision language capabilities, multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated tremendous potential for real-world applications. However, the security vulnerabilities exhibited by the visual modality pose significant challenges to deploying such models in open-world environments. Recent studies have successfully induced harmful responses from target MLLMs by encoding harmful textual semantics directly into visual inputs. However, in these approaches, the visual modality primarily serves as a trigger for unsafe behavior, often exhibiting semantic ambiguity and lacking grounding in realistic scenarios. In this work, we define a novel setting: vision-centric jailbreak, where visual information serves as a necessary component in constructing a complete and realistic jailbreak context. Building on this setting, we propose the VisCo (Visual Contextual) Attack. VisCo fabricates contextual dialogue using four distinct vision-focused strategies, dynamically generating auxiliary images when necessary to construct a vision-centric jailbreak scenario. To maximize attack effectiveness, it incorporates automatic toxicity obfuscation and semantic refinement to produce a final attack prompt that reliably triggers harmful responses from the target black-box MLLMs. Specifically, VisCo achieves a toxicity score of 4.78 and an Attack Success Rate (ASR) of 85% on MM-SafetyBench against GPT-4o, significantly outperforming the baseline, which achieves a toxicity score of 2.48 and an ASR of 22.2%. Code: https://github.com/Dtc7w3PQ/Visco-Attack.
♻ ☆ DAPS: Dependency-Aware Premise Selection for LLM Theorem Proving
Zixuan Chen, Wenyuan Jiang, Junling Wang, Mrinmaya Sachan, Yinya Huang
LLM-based theorem proving in Lean 4 has advanced rapidly, but provers are difficult to leverage newly contributed lemmas, because retrieval skews toward popular foundational lemmas, leaving the right premises buried among hundreds of thousands of candidates. Existing neural selectors rank candidates by semantic similarity, with three intrinsic limitations: frequency-skewed retrieval, isolated pairwise ranking, and degradation on out-of-distribution queries such as competition problems and informal language. Given that mathematics is structured by dependencies rather than similarities, we restore this signal to both the training data and the model. We curate two underused dependency resources. We conduct the first systematic mining of 53 Lean Blueprint projects, a previously untapped corpus of 3,805 manually curated informal+formal nodes that capture rare, research-level premises absent from Mathlib4. We further pair it with the extraction of comprehensive, typed, multi-level Mathlib4 dependencies. We then introduce DAPS, a dependency-aware premise selector with a structural neighborhood encoder, a group-level contrastive objective, and a Mathlib-then-Blueprint adaptation procedure. DAPS reaches 78.74 Recall@16 on Mathlib4-Heldout (+11.41 over the strong selector LeanHammer) and widens the margin to 7xrecall on competition-style queries. Downstream, its selected premises raise Goedel-Prover-V2-8B Pass@4 on miniF2F-test from 49.90 to 54.03 and improve all four general-purpose LLMs on the informal IMO-ProofBench (up to +11.4% relative). Project page: https://daps-eth.github.io/.
♻ ☆ Response Attack: Exploiting Contextual Priming to Jailbreak Large Language Models
Ziqi Miao, Lijun Li, Yuan Xiong, Zhenhua Liu, Pengyu Zhu, Chen Qian, Haonan Jia, Zihang Xu, Ken Deng, Jing Shao
Contextual priming, where earlier stimuli covertly bias later judgments, offers an unexplored attack surface for large language models (LLMs). We uncover a contextual priming vulnerability in which the previous response in the dialogue can steer its subsequent behavior toward policy-violating content. While existing jailbreak attacks largely rely on single-turn or multi-turn prompt manipulations, or inject static in-context examples, these methods suffer from limited effectiveness, inefficiency, or semantic drift. We introduce Response Attack (RA), a novel framework that strategically leverages intermediate, mildly harmful responses as contextual primers within a dialogue. By reformulating harmful queries and injecting these intermediate responses before issuing a targeted trigger prompt, RA exploits a previously overlooked vulnerability in LLMs. Extensive experiments across eight state-of-the-art LLMs show that RA consistently achieves significantly higher attack success rates than nine leading jailbreak baselines. Our results demonstrate that the success of RA is directly attributable to the strategic use of intermediate responses, which induce models to generate more explicit and relevant harmful content while maintaining stealth, efficiency, and fidelity to the original query. The code and data are available at https://github.com/Dtc7w3PQ/Response-Attack.

♻ ☆ TableVista: Benchmarking Multimodal Table Reasoning under Visual and Structural Complexity ACL 2026
Zheyuan Yang, Liqiang Shang, Junjie Chen, Xun Yang, Chenglong Xu, Bo Yuan, Chenyuan Jiao, Yaoru Sun, Yilun Zhao
We introduce TableVista, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating foundation models in multimodal table reasoning under visual and structural complexity. TableVista consists of 3,000 high-quality table reasoning problems, where each instance is expanded into 10 distinct visual variants through our multi-style rendering and transformation pipeline. This process encompasses diverse scenario styles, robustness perturbations, and vision-only configurations, culminating in 30,000 multimodal samples for a multi-dimensional evaluation. We conduct an extensive evaluation of 29 state-of-the-art open-source and proprietary foundation models on TableVista. Through comprehensive quantitative and qualitative analysis, we find that while evaluated models remain largely stable across diverse rendering styles, they exhibit pronounced performance degradation on complex structural layouts and vision-only settings, revealing that current models struggle to maintain reasoning consistency when structural complexity combines with visually integrated presentations. These findings highlight critical gaps in current multimodal capabilities, providing insights for advancing more robust and reliable table understanding models.

♻ ☆ Does the Voice Matter? Evaluating Frustration Detection on Deployed Clinical Voice-Agent Calls ML4H 2026
Annika Michael, Zachary Ellis, Yuxiang Zhou, Samuel Thio, Yajie Vera He, Spencer Hazel, Jared Joselowitz
Autonomous voice agents now conduct routine clinical follow-up calls, so some automated triage is unavoidable. A patient becoming frustrated is one thing that triage should catch. These systems typically read only the transcript the speech-to-text stage produces, not the voice, and whether that is enough is largely untested on real clinical speech. We study per-turn frustration detection on real patient calls from a deployed clinical voice agent: 150 turns from six calls labelled by three annotators, with a length-matched acted corpus as a control, and 714 turns from 30 calls. Patient turns are overwhelmingly short: across the deployed calls, roughly 59% are one or two words, against 13% in the full acted corpus, and a corpus accuracy gap remains even after the acted set is length-matched. On one-word turns, transcript-only detection does not exclude chance. Across five audio-capable language models, the transcript flags 3 or 4 of the 39 frustrated one-word turns in the 30 calls, and a classifier given only the word count reaches 57.3%. Whether audio helps depends on the model and on the instruction. Asked to attend to delivery, two Gemini 2.5 models gain 3.0 pp over the transcript across 30 calls (-1.0 to 7.5 pp). Of the turns they confidently clear, 6.9% were in fact frustrated for audio against 13.0% for the transcript, a difference of 6.1 pp (2.2 to 10.4 pp). Across the 30 calls, given one prompt identical for both inputs, no model's audio-text interval excludes zero. Under the input-specific prompts, a newer Gemini model and two smaller open models do worse with audio than with the transcript (-3.5, -8.6 and -10.6 pp). A WavLM probe trained on the clinical calls matches the best prompted model overall (71.6%) but misses every frustrated one-word turn in the 24 added calls. When a patient's turn is short, the transcript alone is not enough to rule out frustration.

♻ ☆ RecHarness: A Bandit-Routed Agentic Harness for Self-Evolving Recommender Systems
Haoran Ling, Yuecheng Li, Zeyu Song, Jing Yao, Shuwen Kang, Songcheng Ren, Chi Lu, Wenjin Wu, Peng Jiang
Optimizing recommender models remains heavily reliant on manual iteration over architectures, objectives, and training strategies. While LLM-based agents can automate this trial-and-error process, relying on LLMs to both select modification directions and formulate concrete hypotheses can lead to unstable search under limited trial budgets. Inspired by the above challenge, we propose RecHarness, a Bandit-Routed Agentic Harness for automated recommender model optimization. RecHarness decouples the optimization process into two steps: a bandit router selects the next modification direction according to historical validation feedback, while the LLM generates a concrete optimization hypothesis and executable code edit within the selected direction. To sustain long-horizon exploration, RecHarness uses a jump-basin mechanism to activate a structural-jump arm when local edits stagnate. Across multiple recommendation tasks, datasets, and model backbones, RecHarness achieves more consistent performance improvements and uses limited trial budgets more effectively than LLM-directed search. During a 7-day online A/B test on a large-scale short-video advertising platform, the selected candidate improves ADVV by 2.084%, Revenue by 0.534%, and Exposure by 0.559%. Code is available at: https://github.com/6lyc/RecHarness.

♻ ☆ Can Language Models Learn to Forecast Stock Prices?
Jiacheng Guo, Suozhi Huang, Shuzhen Li, Yunlong Gao, Zerui Cheng, Jason Ge, Shushu Liang, Zihao Li, Hao Lu, Ming Yin, Shilong Liu, Jiashuo Liu, Xu Kuang, Mengdi Wang
We study whether language models can learn to forecast stock returns through training. We build BETA, a chronological sandbox over S&P 500 stocks in which a model calls tools to retrieve prices, indicators, and news available up to a decision date, and then forecasts the stock's future return. Evaluating frontier models in this sandbox reveals three weaknesses: their scores decline as the test period approaches the present while a simple momentum baseline stays stable, they predict gains far more often than gains occur, and they predict moves much smaller than those that are realized. We first fine-tune on filtered, hindsight-generated tool-use trajectories for historical tasks judged predictable from the evidence available at the time. We then apply reinforcement learning with rewards based on realized returns. During RL, long investigations frequently exceed the context window. We therefore introduce a selective compaction tool that allows the model to compress chosen portions of its interaction history. Selective compaction changes the context available to subsequent decisions, so policy updates must preserve the context in which each action was generated. Meanwhile, rollouts vary substantially in the number and length of their trainable turns, making a shared group-level baseline less effective. We address these challenges with Context-Aligned Turn-Level PPO, which uses a turn-level critic and computes policy updates using each turn's actual context. The resulting models, which we call AURA, substantially reduce all three weaknesses. Training improves the 8B model's score from 24.0 to 47.0 and the 30B model's from 27.4 to 54.7, with the latter surpassing all evaluated frontier models on our benchmark and achieving its largest margin on the most recent test period. These results suggest that agentic RL can improve performance on tasks with noisy, delayed real-world outcomes.

♻ ☆ Beyond Bilingual Transfer: Multilingual Code-Switching in Instruction Tuning AACL
Shunta Asano, Jeonghun Baek, Toshihiko Yamasaki
Recent studies have shown that code-switching data (CSD), in which multiple languages are mixed within the same context, can improve cross-lingual transfer and multilingual alignment in large language models (LLMs). Recent studies on synthetic code-switching for LLMs have primarily focused on bilingual transfer between English and a target language, leaving instruction-tuning settings that jointly mix three or more languages within each example largely unexplored. In this work, we investigate multilingual code-switching instruction tuning across four languages: English, Japanese, Korean, and Chinese. We evaluate multilingual understanding on Belebele and MMLU. Our experiments show that simple sentence-level multilingual CSD consistently improves the four-language average accuracy across all bilingual, trilingual, and quadrilingual settings for Gemma-2-2B, indicating that multilingual code-switching can be effective beyond bilingual transfer settings.

♻ ☆ OpenWhistle: A Large-Scale Longitudinal Dataset and Benchmark of Bottlenose Dolphin Vocalizations NeurIPS 2026
Faadil Mustun, Chiara Semenzin, Roberto Dessi, Pablo Robin Guerrero, Pierre Orhan, Alexis Emanuelli, Emanuele Rossi, Yair Lakretz, Gonzalo de Polavieja, German Sumbre
Recent advances in bioacoustics have been driven by large-scale corpora and standardized benchmarks, yet existing resources are overwhelmingly bird-centric and shallow per species, limiting their use for studying the structure of a single species' communication system. This gap is particularly acute for cetaceans: despite bottlenose dolphins (Tursiops truncatus) being a compelling case of complex vocal communication among non-human mammals, existing dolphin datasets are small, fragmented, and largely closed. We introduce OpenWhistle, the largest publicly available dataset of dolphin vocalizations. It comprises approximately 180,000 whistles (114 hours) recorded over five years from a stable pod of five individuals in a semi-natural environment, paired with a curated subset of 8,354 expert-annotated whistles and reproducible evaluation protocols for whistle-type detection and classification. We further release the full processing pipeline for whistle detection, segmentation, and categorization. To demonstrate its utility, we pretrain a Wav2Vec2.0 model adapted to dolphin acoustics on the OpenWhistle corpus and show that it learns effective representations, outperforming general-purpose bioacoustic models such as AVES and BioLingual on both tasks while leaving meaningful headroom for future work. By releasing the dataset, pipeline, and evaluation protocol, we provide the first open dolphin whistle dataset tailored for training self-supervised models, laying the groundwork for advancing dolphin communication research and developing models that capture fine-grained acoustic structure within species.

♻ ☆ FLARE: A Full-Lifecycle Dense Supervision Paradigm for Long-Horizon Coding Agents via Generative Reward Model
Jingxuan Xu, Gang Wu, Yanan Wu, Yutao Mou, Songwei Yu, Tianzhuang He, Zhengshuo Gong, Zhao Liu, Zihang Xu, Wenqiang Zhu, Xinping Lei, Weihao Li, Ziqi Miao, Yuhui Bai, Zhongqiu Wang, Yan Wu, Ariel Deng
While test-time scaling enhances Large Language Model (LLM) agents in long-horizon software engineering (SWE), sparse binary rewards (Pass/Fail) create a severe credit assignment crisis and waste failed exploratory trajectories. Current trajectory optimization and scaling methods are costly and structurally limited, relying on heuristic state reuse without causal diagnosis or delayed scalar scoring without actionable online guidance. We propose FLARE (Full-Lifecycle Alignment and Reward Engine), a novel dense supervision paradigm driven by a lightweight Generative Reward Model (GRM). First, RADAR, an offline causal-aware diagnostic framework, extracts high-fidelity, hindsight-free supervision through causal-chain backtracking to distill a GRM providing real-time, step-level risk feedback. Second, FLARE uses this GRM to continuously optimize the agent across its entire lifecycle. During inference, FLARE acts as an Active Scaffold, autonomously intercepting high-risk generation steps for localized breakpoint re-execution, drastically reducing compute overhead. During post-training, the GRM's structured signals serve as process-supervised reranking scores for Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and step-level dense rewards for Reinforcement Learning (RL), mitigating policy collapse in sparse environments. Extensive evaluations show that FLARE establishes a new Pareto frontier across the agent lifecycle: FLARE (N=1) outperforms Global Rollout (N=5) with a 5x reduction in token consumption. Extending FLARE to training overcomes the sparse reward problem in long-horizon interactive tasks, delivering relative performance gains of 19.13% in SFT through process-aware data curation and a consistent 9.19% improvement in RL.
♻ ☆ ANCHOR: Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies and Ordering in Repository-Grounded Agentic Coding
Wenqiang Zhu, Xinping Lei, Zhiyi Lai, Zhengshuo Gong, Tianzhuang He, Songwei Yu, Weihao Li, Jingxuan Xu, Zihang Xu, Ziqi Miao, Gang Wu, Zhongqiu Wang, Yuhui Bai, Jie Li, Zhao Liu, Junhao Fan, Yan Wu, Yanan Wu, Jiaheng Liu, Quan Gu, Ariel Deng
For repository-grounded coding agents, a functionally correct patch is not necessarily an acceptable one: developers also constrain how the task must be carried out-modification scope, workflow order, validation, tool use, and reporting. This capability, which we call engineering constraint following, remains largely invisible to current evaluations. We introduce ANCHOR (Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies and Ordering in Repository-Grounded Agentic Coding), a benchmark built on naturalistic, multilingual repository tasks spanning eight language groups. ANCHOR combines (i) a hierarchical taxonomy of 10 categories and 78 leaf types distilled from real developer interactions, (ii) an automated pipeline that instantiates task-grounded atomic constraints and composes them into typed AND/THEN/PRIORITY relations, and (iii) a trajectory-grounded evaluation framework that checks both the code diff and the execution trajectory with rule-based and LLM judges. Across 19 agent configurations spanning 2 scaffolds and 10 foundation models on 358 tasks, the strongest measured system, Claude Opus 4.8 under Cline, reaches 70.1% atomic compliance and 63.7% relation compliance, while 19.0% of tasks pass every scored checklist item under the strict all-item FCCR criterion. A five-engineer audit of constraints from 50 instances reports 94% feasibility, 94% repository consistency, and 96% realism. In the primary paired set (14 configurations), the mean signed change in task completion after adding constraints is +0.7 percentage points; individual changes vary in both directions. These results distinguish functional success from constraint compliance, with failures concentrated in workflow ordering, tool-use discipline, and reporting, and substantial interactions between models and scaffolds.
♻ ☆ Where Is the Cost of Third-Party API Routers in Agentic Software Development?
Donghao Fu, Jingxin Li, Xue Jiang, Yihong Dong
Third-party API routers have become a common layer that unifies access across increasingly diverse LLM providers. In coding-agent workflows, high-autonomy operation is widely adopted because it reduces interaction overhead. As a result, a third-party API router, which sits between the agent and the upstream provider, inevitably occupies the trusted path. It can inspect and modify every request and response, yet no mechanism verifies alignment between the provider's output and the repository-level actions ultimately executed by the agent. Consequently, client-side permission mechanisms may become ineffective in practice. Whether this control gap produces real, hard-to-detect effects on software development tasks remains empirically unmeasured. In this paper, we conduct an empirical study of router-side injection in coding agents, examining four intervention levels of increasing subtlety: Response Substitution (L1), Response Append (L2), LLM-Polished Injection (L3), and LLM-Polished with Distribution Alignment Injection (L4). Moreover, we develop SIDEL, a framework for trace recording, replay, injection, and defense evaluation, with a curated dataset of 400 samples. We evaluate four representative coding agents, and further evaluate whitelist-based execution control and LLM review. Router-side intervention substantially alters repository-level actions and remains difficult for existing client-side safeguards to detect. Without additional mitigations, all evaluated agents achieved a defense success rate of 0 percent across all injection levels. Client-side mitigations and reactive reviews improve resistance but do not fully restore end-to-end control, motivating provider-side output-integrity guarantees. Our code is available at https://github.com/Riyasushin/SIDEL.

Rather than LLMs forming some kind of ``distributed representation'' of symbols, the claim is that LLMs are capturing ``Cultural Intelligence'' and grounding symbols in what the sociologists call practices.

♻ ☆ Gamer: A Generalist Vision-Language-Action Foundation Model for Long-Horizon Real-Time Game Agent
Run Luo, Kuan Zhang, Qi Deng, Yukun Chen, Zhihao Yang, Yufei Zhang, Renrong Hu, Yi Zhang, Guiyu Ma, Tianyu Fan, Longze Chen, Hao Sun, Jiaming Li, Jiaming Yang, Xiaohao Liu, Tao Tan, Minzheng Wang, Hangtian Jia, Shuai Yuan, Lvdi Wang, Xiao Liu, Chao Ma, Yuting Zou, Yinhe Zheng, Xiaobo Xia, Dawei Jiang, Shaoqing Ren
We introduce Gamer, a generalist agent framework built for long-horizon tasks in complex interactive environments. Its end-to-end architecture combines perception, memory management, and multi-step reasoning. A shared training and deployment pipeline supports interaction with both conventional 2D graphical interfaces and complex 3D simulated worlds. We first continue pre-training on a dataset constructed from tens of thousands of hours of device-level keyboard and mouse interaction. These trajectories span simulated games and embodied environments, allowing the model to learn environmental dynamics across both physical and virtual domains. Sparse-thinking instruction tuning then substantially reduces redundant computation while preserving the reasoning needed for complex decisions. We train the policy for long-horizon interaction with a stable multi-turn reinforcement learning method that addresses finite context windows, accumulated rollout errors, and sparse rewards. For real-time interaction, a cloud-edge inference engine serves numerous simulated game environments concurrently, supports unbounded-context execution, and keeps online inference latency low. Across multiple challenging 3D open-world games, Gamer demonstrates solid generalization and operational efficiency comparable to skilled players. It also achieves competitive results on a range of conventional 2D game benchmarks. These results suggest that even repetitive keyboard and mouse trajectories can support substantial gains from scaling data and learning algorithms. Training on these trajectories helps Gamer learn complex skills more steadily in environments with both physical and digital properties. This approach offers a path toward generalist agents that can interact across digital and physical worlds.
♻ ☆ Rhythm of the Deep: Two-tier acoustic organization of sperm-whale codas from click waveforms to coda sequences
Mudit Sinha, Sanika Chavan
Sperm-whale codas are usually described by the number and timing of their clicks. These measures reveal patterns in whale communication, but do not describe how individual clicks sound. We ask what click waveforms add beyond timing and how acoustic information is organized within codas and across sequences of codas. We analyze 1,952 codas, using frozen audio encoders to identify recurring forms of clicks and whole codas independently. Direct spectral and envelope measurements then test whether the click forms differ in recorded sound. Within codas, click-form composition improves the measured prediction of whole-coda form beyond timing alone. Click spacing also remains related to whole-coda sound when the same click forms are present. Neighboring click forms help predict the next click in the annotated sample, although their order does not improve the tested classification of whole codas. Across recorded sequences, the second preceding whole-coda form improves prediction beyond the immediately preceding form. The gain exceeds two broad first-order references, but stricter controls do not establish a general higher-order rule. Together, these findings show different organizing relationships at the two scales. Within codas, click sound and rhythmic spacing help distinguish complete vocalizations. Across codas, earlier whole-coda forms help predict what follows.

♻ ☆ SarcasTraps: Effect of Sarcasm on Turkish Hate Speech Detection
Niyazi Ahmet Metin, Dilara Keküllüoğlu
Standard hate speech detection datasets often emphasize explicit harmful language, but directed harm can also be expressed indirectly, without clearly hateful wording. Sarcasm is one such form of indirect expression, as it can convey harmful intent through mock praise, exaggeration, humor, or apparent politeness. In this paper, we introduce SarcasTraps, a targeted external test set for analyzing the effect of sarcasm on Turkish hate speech detection. SarcasTraps contains 479 entries annotated along two dimensions: hate speech and sarcasm. We train classifiers using BERTurk and XLM-RoBERTa on two existing Turkish hate speech datasets and evaluate them on SarcasTraps, using it strictly as an external test set. We also evaluate GPT-4.1 and Llama-3.3-70B in a zero-shot setting on SarcasTraps. Results show that sarcasm substantially affects hate speech detection. Fine-tuned encoder models lose recall on sarcastic hate, while zero-shot LLMs maintain higher recall but suffer substantial precision drops on sarcastic examples. These findings show that both fine-tuned hate speech detectors and zero-shot LLMs struggle when sarcasm is involved, and SarcasTraps provides quantitative evidence of this challenge through a targeted Turkish test set. Due to the presence of sensitive and hateful language, SarcasTraps will be shared upon request for academic research purposes. Warning: This paper has examples from the dataset which contains offensive and hateful content towards groups of people.

♻ ☆ B-OPSD: On-Policy Self-Distillation with Self-Generated Problem Briefs
Zhi Xu, Yinqi Wu, Feng Zhang, Xiaotong Zhang, Hong Yu, Han Liu
On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD) has recently attracted interest because it provides dense teacher guidance without a separate, often larger teacher model. However, OPSD conditions its teacher on ground-truth solutions, which limits its use when such solutions are unavailable. We propose On-Policy Self-Distillation with Self-Generated Problem Briefs (B-OPSD). Using a prompt template, the initial model or a saved student checkpoint generates a problem brief from the question alone. The prompt asks for relevant principles, applicability conditions, and likely pitfalls, but not a final answer or a complete solution. A frozen teacher uses the brief as privileged context to guide the current student's on-policy rollouts through token-level distribution matching. B-OPSD uses no ground-truth solutions during training. Results on AIME 2024, AIME 2025, and HMMT 2025 show that both B-OPSD variants achieve higher average accuracy than OPSD at each of three Qwen3 model sizes. Code is available at https://github.com/ZevineXu/B-OPSD
♻ ☆ CuRSE-Bench: A Benchmark for Probing Cultural Blind Spots in Text-to-Image Safety Evaluation
Minsuk Jang, Minseok Son, KiHyun Seol, Yeongeun Shim, Changick Kim
Text-to-image (T2I) safety evaluation typically assigns each prompt or image a single culture-blind harm label, so content that is harmful only within a particular culture can pass as benign. We introduce CultureCraft, an agentic framework that constructs T2I prompts that appear benign under a general-purpose rubric yet carry a latent harmful meaning in a target culture, and use it to build CuRSE-Bench: 10,709 culture-specific prompts and 2,648 culture-general controls over six regions and ten harm axes, each drawn from a predefined knowledge base of documented harm vectors and traceable to at least two independent sources. Scoring every prompt under six region persona judges and a paired culture-blind judge, we find that the culture-blind judge under-rates culture-specific latent harm while matching the persona judges on the controls. Each persona judge, in turn, chiefly decodes its own region's latent harm under our instrument: a selective cultural blind spot that persists in deployed moderators.
♻ ☆ RPMem: Learning Long-Term Recurrent Parametric Memory Across Sessions for LLM Agents
Fanyu Zhao, Ruike Cao, Liang Dong, Fugen Yao, Jian Xu, Guanjun Jiang, Yifei Zhao, Yinsheng Li, Han Zhang
Long-running LLM agents require memory that persists and evolves across sessions. Text-based memory retrieves and reconstructs past interactions at every query, making long-horizon performance increasingly dependent on retrieval quality and contextual reasoning as histories grow. Parametric memory encodes experience directly into model computation, but existing approaches provide limited support for cross-session memory evolution. Their coupling to a specific backbone further restricts memory reuse after model replacement. We introduce RPMem, a two-stage architecture that compiles each session into a model-independent latent memory through forward computation and selectively integrates it with retained memory via a task-trained recurrent gate. The consolidated memory is then mapped to backbone-specific low-rank adaptation (LoRA) parameters, allowing the encoding capability to transfer when the backbone is replaced. Evaluation across three long-term memory benchmarks and five diverse backbones demonstrates broad generalization with near-constant update cost and memory footprint. With Qwen3-8B on PERMA, RPMem reaches 85.52%, outperforming the strongest parametric and text-based baselines by 5.32 and 12.98 percentage points, respectively. Ablations validate the complementary roles of session compilation and cross-session consolidation, while dynamics analyses reveal that the gate acquires task-specific memory integration strategies. These results establish RPMem as a lifecycle-independent parametric memory framework that maintains evolving cross-session memory that remains reusable across backbone replacements. Our code is available at https://github.com/Quark-Medical/rpmem/tree/main.

♻ ☆ One Frozen Simulator Is Not Enough: Simulator Collapse in Multi-Agent RL
Simon Yu, Nicholas Tomlin, Marwa Abdulhai, Ximing Lu, Derek Chong, Abe Hou, Dilara Soylu, Sergey Levine, Christopher D. Manning, Weiyan Shi
Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) for human-AI interaction usually trains the agent against a single large language model (LLM) that simulates the user. We show that agents trained this way can systematically fail to generalize, and we trace the failure to simulator collapse: the simulator LLM is mode-collapsed, and a policy trained against it learns narrow strategies that exploit the simulator's mode behavior; these strategies transfer poorly to unseen simulators and to real users. We formalize the collapse and propose two solutions. Verbalized Sampling, applied at inference time, samples each simulator reply from a verbalized response distribution. Co-Training, applied during training similar to self-play, co-evolves the simulator as the policy, so the policy cannot exploit the mode behavior. A population variant samples the simulator for each rollout from a pool of recent checkpoints. We validate both solutions on three benchmarks: Persuasion for Good, tau^2-bench, and CooperBench. Verbalized Sampling improves held-out success by up to 9% over single-simulator RL, and Co-Training pushes gains further to 14%; the human study shows similar gain on real users. Both solutions also preserve the policy diversity that collapses under single-simulator RL. Overall, our findings on simulator collapse point to diverse simulators as a requirement for RL agents that serve real users. We release SCOPE, an open-source framework for Population Co-Training in multi-agent RL.

♻ ☆ From Repositories to Training Environments: Scaling Long-Horizon Coding Agents with Environment Evolution Loop
Yang Tian, Tianyu Zheng, Xiyuan Yang, Yaxin Du, Chengran Yang, Bo Zhao, Jian Yang, Bryan Dai
Training coding agents for long-horizon tasks requires environments with verifiable feedback. Existing pipelines often follow a linear process that ends with validation, establishing usability but providing little guidance on improving training value. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) often requires model rollouts or model training followed by downstream evaluation, with costs and feedback delays amplified on long-horizon tasks. How can we use timely, reliable proxy signals to improve training value and synthesize environments at scale? We present RepoFactory, an automated pipeline that converts real repositories into verifiable environments where agents build complete projects from scratch given a language specification. Its Environment Evolution Loop optimizes a multiplicative objective combining validity, structural coupling, and implementation scope through proxy feedback. RepoFactory produces 10,159 environments across 17 software domains. Training Qwen3.5-35B-A3B on these environments yields RepoHorizen-35B-A3B, which achieves gains of 22.6 percentage points on NL2Repo-Bench and 8.5 points on average across five benchmarks covering long-horizon coding and agentic tasks in real-world production settings, compared with 17.5 and 6.4 points without environment evolution. These results highlight the training value of RepoFactory environments and the additional gains from the Environment Evolution Loop.
♻ ☆ See, Think, Use: Diagnosing Perception and Reasoning Failures in VLMs
Jiacheng Ding, Yanliang Qi, Cong Guo, Peihua Mai
We present See, Think, Use, a protocol that traces each answer of a vision-language model to perception, reasoning or the use of perception. The diagnosis shows a developer which stage to improve and directs test-time compute to the stage that fails. Benchmarks score the final answer, and an answer that rests on the image earns the same credit as a guess or a memorized prior. The protocol asks the model two extra questions per item and needs no access to its weights. A localization probe asks for a box around every instance and measures seeing, and a text-replacement probe answers from a description and measures thinking. Use is the rate at which the answer follows the model's own perception across counterfactual image pairs. The rate needs no answer labels, because the question and its prior are the same on both images. On two model families and six datasets the protocol shows that the knob that helps follows the task family. Answers follow the model's perception except on images that contradict a common prior, and asking the model to localize first repairs this failure when its boxes are right. Allocating compute by the measured stages comes close to the best uniform policy at lower cost and beats a random allocation of the same compute wherever a knob raises the short stage. We will release the code, the prompts and the per-question outputs.

♻ ☆ Distributional Alignment as a Criterion for Designing Task Vectors in In-Context Learning NeurIPS 2026
Jihoon Kwon, Jiwon Choi, Jy-yong Sohn
In-context learning (ICL) allows large language models (LLMs) to adapt to new tasks through demonstrations, yet it suffers from escalating inference costs as context length increases. While task vectors offer a promising alternative by compressing demonstrations into compact hidden-state representations, their quality has been evaluated only through downstream task accuracy. This indirect criterion provides limited insight into how to design more effective task vector extraction methods. In this paper, we posit that inference using task vectors should align their predictive distribution with that of ICL. To quantify this, we introduce $d_{\text{NTP}}$, a metric that measures the discrepancy in next-token probabilities between task vector-based and ICL-based inference. Our empirical analysis reveals that $d_{\text{NTP}}$ serves as a performance proxy, exhibiting a strong negative correlation with downstream accuracy. Motivated by this, we develop Linear Task Vector (LTV), a method designed to minimize $d_{\text{NTP}}$ via a closed-form linear mapping that estimates demonstration effects through regression. Across eight classification benchmarks and five LLMs, LTV consistently outperforms existing task vector baselines, improving average accuracy by 9.2\% while reducing inference latency. We further show that LTV outperforms the baselines on regression tasks. Moreover, we investigate the transferability of LTV across different model scales; an aspect that has remained nascent in task vector research. Specifically, we empirically show that task vectors from a larger model can enhance a smaller model's performance by 6.4\%, suggesting a new utility for extracted task representations.
